# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum XV

## Funding proposal package for FP273

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Papua New Guinea REDD+ plus RBP for results period 2014-2016";

b) No objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s)/disclosure;

d) Secretary's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in its current, unedited form. The GCF Secretariat will make publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

# Proposed and projected REDD-plus results

## A. Achieved volume of REDD-plus results achieved in the pilot programme (ICoE)

The total volume of REDD+ Results achieved by Papua New Guinea (PNG) and reported in the REDD+ Technical Annex to the Final Biennial Update Report (BUR1) for the 2015-2016 period was 7,344,980 ICoE. The BUR2 for the 2016-2018 period was 70,344,100 ICoE.

### BUR 1 Technical Annex: 5,003,314 ICoE (2015-2016)
* 2015: 5,003,314 ICoE
* 2016: 5,045,982 ICoE

### BUR 2 Technical Annex: 61,340,786 ICoE (2016-2018)
* 2016: 20,394,158 ICoE
* 2017: 24,394,158 ICoE
* 2018: 16,552,470 ICoE

## A+ Achieved volume of REDD-plus results offered to the pilot programme in this proposal (ICoE)

PNG offers to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) the results achieved by PNG under the pilot programme. PNG has applied a recalculation of the FREEFLR as described above.

From the total results achieved, 17,115,721 ICoE, PNG has discounted the volume of emissions reductions transferred to the GCF in the period 2014-2016 (38,465 ICoE in 2014/2015) and the transactions made by the April 2017 decision of the GCF on REDD+ Verified Carbon Standard (120,000 ICoE).

Therefore, the achieved volume of REDD+ results offered to the pilot programme is as follows:

* A+: 16,965,256 ICoE
    * 2014: 3,450,168 ICoE
    * 2015: 7,000,000 ICoE
    * 2016: 10,472,258 ICoE

## B+ Expected volume of REDD-plus results to be achieved in the following years of the eligibility period (ICoE)

* B+: 39,565,206 ICoE
    * 2017: 14,200,000 ICoE
    * 2018: 25,365,206 ICoE

## A+B+ Total volume expected to be submitted to the pilot programme (ICoE)

* A+B+: 16,965,256 ICoE

PNG does not plan to submit the results achieved during the 2017 and 2018 period to the GCF REDD+ programme. The country is exploring alternative carbon financing options.

B.1.1 UNFCCC Technical Assessment and Analysis process

*(Consistency of the FREL/FRL)*

Following the submission of the Concept Note on 20 December 2015, Papua New Guinea (PNG) is submitting the Revised Forest Reference Level (FRL) for REDD+ as a basis for policy-based payments to PNG under the paragraph 3.4 of appendix GCF.B-8691: Policy for results-based payments for REDD+ at $60/tonne CO2e. A letter about the submission of the FRL is attached. The assessment of the consistency between the closure of the pilot program in 2022 and the funding submissions as part of the Pilot Program (PP) under the FREL/FRL (paragraphs d) and e) are provided.

In January 2017, PNG submitted to Forest Reference Level Emission Level or Forest Reference Level (FRL) Assessment. A modified version was submitted in July 2017, incorporating improvements to enhance the clarity and support for the assessment of the consistency between the FRL and the funding submissions as part of the pilot program exchange with the Assessment Team (AT) during the Technical Assessment (TA) process.

The most recent national Greenhouse Gases (GHG) inventory available during the assessment was from the Second National Communication (SNC) submitted to UNFCCC in 2015. The Technical Assessment Report (TAR) noted that there were discrepancies in the data, methods, and assumptions used by PNG between the two reports. The TAR highlighted:

* The GHG inventory included in the NC2 and the FREL/FRL were not developed using the same datasets.
* There was a difference of 10% in the total emissions reported for the national GHG Inventory.
* That PNG may wish to broaden the scope of the FRL by assessing the significance of any other activities that could influence their inclusion, especially the conservation of forest carbon stock and sustainable forest management.
* PNG may wish to include other significant pools (deadwood and soil carbon), excluded from the FREL/FRL.

Progress in the BUR1 and BUR2 are as follows:

PNG aimed to achieve full coherence in data and methods in the national GHG inventory to be included in the FREL/FRL. The main difference between the BUR1 and BUR2 is that the BUR2 includes 30 more 2022 GHG inventories included in both BUR’s have increased the overall consistency with the FREL/FRL.

The GHG inventories in the BUR1 and BUR2 were estimated using the same data sources, methods, default values, and assumptions. The main difference between the BUR1 and BUR2 is that the lack of reliable data, PNG decided not to broaden the scope of the FREL/FRL by including other activities.

The main difference between GHG inventories in BUR1 and BUR2 and the FREL/FRL stems from whether land use change emissions are included. The BUR1 and BUR2 do not include land use change emissions, while the FREL/FRL does. This is because PNG has not yet conducted annual land use change assessments as required by the Collect Earth tool. However, observations of forest degradation in PNG have been made over a number of years, which is useful in identifying repeated degradation. In contrast, the GHG inventories included the regression using IPCC 2006 Guidelines for evidence.

The FREL/FRL includes emissions from CO2a due to the lack of reliable data, while the GHG Inventories included CH4 and N2O emissions. For similar reasons, litter and Soil Organic Carbon (SOC) were also not included in the BUR1 and BUR2 inventories. The LULUCF sector of the GHG Inventories using default values for completeness. Fuelwood use was only partially included in the BUR1 and BUR2 inventories due to lack of data.

PNG is considering including options, i.e., dead wood and soil carbon pools, in the forthcoming FREL/FRL as an area of improvement. This will be possible once the National Forest Inventory (NFI), which is currently under implementation, is finalized.

The NFI was launched by the Prime Minister in March 2016. During its initial implementation from 2017 to 2019, 160 plots across 43 districts in seven provinces were assessed. In total, 8,053 trees were measured and mapped using a handheld GPS device.

The NFI was halted in 2020 due to the COVID-19 pandemic and funding shortages. The project required an additional 40 plots for the National Forest Inventory (NFI) to be completed under the Global Facility (GF). During this phase, an additional 40 plots were assessed. Funding for the NFI is now secured through a grant from the Green Climate Fund (GCF), which will allow the submission of the Second Biennial Transparency Report (BTR), which is scheduled for submission in 2026.

### (i.a) Data source of the FRE/LRF:

The PNG FRE/LRF submission was made well before the launch of the GCF Subsidized Payment Mechanism (SPM) and the adoption of the GCF Scoring Framework (GCF SF: 13.2). Although the AT found that the data and information used by PNG in constructing its FRE/LRF are, more transparent, complete, and consistent with the GCF scoring framework, it is important to note that for this project, the use of a linear regression model would not meet the GCF scoring requirements because project-specific factors such as forest degradation and deforestation were not considered.

According to relevant United Nations Framework on Climate Change Convention (UNFCCC) decisions, each submission of a proposed reference level, i.e., the context of results-based payments, will be subject to a peer review. The AT has recommended that PNG should consider revising its FRE/LRF before submitting to the programme a “recalculation of the FRE/LRF”, and results based on submitted and technically assessed FRE/LRFs are not considered for disbursement, without changing the annuallized estimations using the same methodologies.

The recalculation of the FRE/LRF only concerns the construction approach (i.e., projecting the data using a linear regression model) but does not change the underlying assumptions or parameters. It uses the same data set, underlying methods, scale, scope and forest definition and stratification as the technically assessed FRE/LRF.

The recalculation of FRE/LRF implied an increase of 0.1% of the total forest carbon stock over the historical emissions registered during the reference period (5 years) to reflect the country’s conditions at the time of submission.

The recalculated FRE/LRF annual emissions are 42,172,324 CO2e during the FRE/LRF reference period (2014-2018), resulting in lower emissions than the UNFCCC technically assessed FRE/LRF as shown below. The recalculation of the FRE/LRF is more conservative than that of the technically assessed one, being 272,586,510 CO2e lower than the FRE/LRF assessed by PNG.

| Year | UNFCCC assessed FRE/LRF (tCO2e) | Recalculation of the FRE/LRF (tCO2e) | UNFCCC assessed FRE/LRF - Recalculated FRE/LRF (tCO2e) |

### (i.b) If a country is considered HFLD:

In 2015, PNG was classified as an HFLD country according to the TFW of the total national area. The average annual deforestation rate was 0.07% for the period between 2009 and 2013, and it increased to 0.06% for the period 2014-2018 (GWPNG, 2017).

PNG is classified as an HFLD country under the thresholds and criteria established by Fornace et al in 2007 and applied on the basis of the Paris Agreement Joint Commitment on HFLD Climate Finance Mechanism. PNG has exceeded over 50% forest cover.

PNG also qualifies as a HFLD country according to the Architecture for REDD+ Indications: The REDD+ Environmental Excellence Standard (ART-TREES) requirements. ART-TREES uses a quantified threshold of 70% forest cover and an annual deforestation rate below 1%. PNG has exceeded this threshold with an annual score, based on the deforestation rate and the percentage of forest cover. A country must have a score over 0.7 to qualify as an HFLD country; PNG far exceeds this threshold, with a score of 0.7 over the period 2009-2018.

### (i.c) FREL/FRL adjustments for a HFLD country:

Since PNG is classified as an HFLD country and given that emissions from deforestation and forest degradation have been increasing during the reference period, PNG has proposed an upwards adjustment of its baseline to reflect the higher level of deforestation and forest degradation observed during the reference period, which may have underestimated future rates of deforestation or forest degradation.

Emissions from the forestry sector have maintained a sustained increase during the reference period. In 2009, emissions were at 35 million tCO2e; but by 2013, they had risen to over 42 million tCO2e. This represents an increase of approximately 20%.

The increase in emissions coincides with a 12% increase in population over the same five-year period, along with an 83% growth in Gross Domestic Product (GDP) over the same timeframe. Both population and economic growth have contributed to increased deforestation and forest degradation.

natural resources and agriculture lands. These trends have persisted over the last decade with annual population growth rates of 2.5% and GDP growth rates of 3%.

According to the TA Report published in the Info Hub for REDD+, the AT noted that in its submission, PNG government officials indicated that the country’s forest cover had increased by approximately 10% since 2007, which largely supported the expectation of increasing emissions in the period 2014–2018. Notably, the country’s population growth rate is projected to remain high and could further increase future deforestation rates when based solely on the projection of average historical emissions.

In addition, global indicators such as the Human Development Index (HDI) and population projections further suggest that PNG will continue to experience rapid urbanization in the coming years. The HDI has increased between 2001 and 2025, encompassing both the reference and results periods. Similarly, population projections indicate that PNG’s urban population is expected to increase by approximately 3% per year relative to its country’s relatively early stage in the demographic transition. While urbanization levels remain low, this trend could have significant implications for future deforestation rates as people seek new resources. The projection of annual forest loss by PNG appears to be corroborated by these indicators.

Specifically, the data suggests that the pressure on resources has risen significantly—often exceeding global average trends—and is expected to continue rising in the coming years. However, this pressure has not translated into an increase in deforestation due to the actions taken by the country.

Analysis of the historical series presented in the FREL/FRL suggests that deforestation rates increased until approximately 2014 and then decreased significantly since then. The analysis of the projected development indicators demonstrated sustained progress; deforestation trends are being successfully managed, and PNG is on track to meet its REDD+ targets.

It is worth noting some additional elements that demonstrate PNG’s commitment to the results achieved:

- National development policies identify a shift from national planning away from strategies focused on economic growth through natural resource extraction to one that is based on a more sustainable development model.
- PNG has taken measures to prioritize sustainable development, expecting economic incentives to focus on non-timber forest products and other activities.
- PNG’s Prime Minister James Marape, prior to COP29, urged high-emission nations to take meaningful action or provide the necessary resources to keep global warming below 1.5°C.

It is important to note that the proposed upward adjustment of the GCF RFB Scopes criteria generates more conservative results than are assessed and accepted by the UNFCCC.

The proposed upward adjustment means the GCF RFB restrictions, namely, the FREL/FRL should not exceed 20% of the total carbon stock on the account over the period. The upward adjustment is estimated at 0.1% of the total carbon stock on the account over the period.

- The total forest carbon stock in PNG corresponding to the year 2015 was 14,772,869.3 tCO₂e.
- Therefore, 0.1% of the total forest carbon stock corresponds to 14,772.8693 tCO₂e.
- This means that the 5-year eligibility period suggests an allowable upward adjustment of 2,564,572 CO₂e/year.

On the other hand, the assessed 0.1% of the recalculated of the FREL/FRL represents higher values:

- Average emissions as 42,172,324 tCO₂e/yearly. Therefore, 10%, of the recalculated of the FREL/FRL suggests an allowable upward adjustment of 4,217,232 CO₂e/year.

After examination and consideration of the above information, it is concluded that the most restrictive limit or adjustment method, which is 0.1% of the total carbon stock over the accounting period,

As such, the recalculation of the FRELFRL (fallow CO2eq emissions from deforestation and forest degradation) for PNG in the period 2009-2013 (the award valid period for HFLD countries) for the GCF REDD programme applied the following formula:

| Annual emissions (tCO2eq) | = | Emissions(2009 - 2013) | / n |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | (Emissions(2009 - 2013)) | (0.0002 + Forest C stock) |

Where:
- Emissions(2009 - 2013): are the emissions from deforestation and forest degradation during the period.
- n: is the number of years in the period.
- Forest C stock: is the total national forest carbon stock.

(ii) FRELFRL in accordance with 12/CP.17:

PNG FRELFRL was elaborated in accordance with 12/CP.17 as indicated in the Technical Assessment Report.

The FRELFRL covers the entire national territory.
- PNG used the 2006 IPCC methodologies in the construction of the FRELFRL.
- The FRELFRL applies a regression approach enabling PNG to improve it by incorporating better data, improved methodology and additional activities.
- The FRELFRL report include information about the National Circumstances, specially related to the HFLD of PNG and the measures taken to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation.

(iv) FRELFRL transparency:

The AT provided some additional information regarding the issues that were not material to the transparency of the FRELFRL as stated in the T.A. Report.
- Most of these issues were related to data and time restrictions.
- The AT confirmed that the FRELFRL is overall transparent and there are no material issues related to transparency.

During the TA process, the AT raised areas to improve the transparency of the PNG report:
- Most of the information was provided during the process by providing additional information in the modified submission of the FRELFRL.

The most relevant transparency issues raised during the TAR were:

* Clarification on specific data sets referred to in the FRELFRL, such as areas under Special Agricultural and Business Leases, forest plantations and viable concessions that were not provided in a harmonized manner.
* Clarifications on the statistical procedure followed when applying the regression model, including a comparison with other methodologies used by PNG.
* Lack of detailed information on how the country converted the Collect Earth sampling plots information into FRELFRL.

Additional information provided to the AT and all additional actions conducted by PNG have enhanced the transparency, as demonstrated in the BURR and BURR Technical Annexes submitted by PNG:

- PNG increased transparency by implementing a web platform (http://png-rsfrls.org/) that has made public all data used for estimating the FRELFRL, including land use information such as the location of palm oil and logging concessions, cropland distribution, and other spatial data.
- Spreadsheets including all the data used to estimate the FRELFRL were shared with the AT and will be available for further consultations upon request.


---
**Page 10**
---

In addition to the AT noted that further details of the statistical procedures for the estimation of activity from the Collect Earth sample plots are an area of improvement to be addressed.

As a result of the potential issues identified, PNG included further details about the statistical procedure to quantify land use changes in the historical time series. The sampling design is based on a systematic grid of 0.4 degrees and 0.02 degrees in the case of the provincial level and 1 degree by 1 degree for the national level. The conversion of plot information to activity data (i.e. deforestation or forest degradation area) was made based on the number of plots per province and the total area of the province.

During the exchange of information with the AT, PNG provided step-by-step instructions on how to follow the procedure to estimate the sampling points for the FREL/FRL.

To calculate the expansion factor prior to estimating the area, PNG used the Collect Earth package tool Saku. Saku is the most common data analysis software package for generating statistics using Collect Earth data. It has been customised to visualise and analyse Collect Earth data. Countries using Collect Earth data have developed their own tools to generate statistics from the sampling points. Saku organises this wealth of information and enables users to query the data and implement statistical analyses (see the Saku user manual, available on the Collect Earth website for further tutorials). By using the Saku tabular analysis function, land change matrices are produced by drag-and-drop operations.

Saku uses a special algorithm to automatically convert the results of sampling plot analysis into activity data. The output is in the form of a spreadsheet that can be used directly as input for the case area of analysis. In this case, the data is aggregated at the provincial level to properly account for the time series already mentioned earlier.

Exported result files are embedded into the FREL calculation spreadsheet to estimate activity data automatically.

(vi) FRE/LRL companies:

The AT acknowledged that the information provided by the country allows for the reconstruction of the FRE/LRL. However, it was noted that additional information, particularly details related to the activity data collection procedure and emission factors, was shared during the TA process to improve the completeness of the series.

The creation of the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) web platform has contributed to enhancing completeness, as commanded by the AT.

(vii) FRE/LRL consistency:

The AT consider that the FRE/LRL is overall consistent and there are no material issues. Minor issues related to data collection and scaled during the technical exchange of information.

The TA report (para 16) indicates that PNG's method of detecting land use changes in the historical time series generates more accurate results because more satellite images are available in the later years of the series than in the earlier ones.

During the technical exchange of information, PNG explained in detail the method used to assess historical land use changes. The assessment is based on the analysis of Landsat 7 and Landsat 8 data with a spatial resolution of 30 meters using the RapidEye. Previous lands use are analysed using Landsat 7 and Landsat 8 data series with a spatial resolution of 15 meters.

PNG applied a quality assurance/quality control (QAQC) procedure to ensure integrity and consistency during the time series (the QAQC procedure is described in detail in section vii FRE/LRL accuracy)).


(viii) FRE/LRL accuracy:


---
**Page 11**
---

The TA did not raise significant issues regarding the accuracy of the FREE/LRF. However, the AT highlighted several areas for improvement concerning accuracy, mainly related to the use of emission factors.

PNG has implemented a QAQC procedure to ensure the accuracy and consistency of measurements obtained with remote sensing technology used to calculate activity data. The QAQC process is described in detail in the Technical Annexes, including the BUR1 and BUR2 Technical Annexes, subtracted residuals from post-deforestation biomass regrowth using an approximation. However, the accuracy of these estimates could be significantly improved by using crop-specific coefficients for biomass growth rates for non-forest land uses and tree types, as noted by the AT.

The forest degradation emission factor used in the FRLR and subsequently in the BUR1 and BUR2 Technical Annexes, subtracted residuals from post-deforestation biomass regrowth using an approximation. However, the accuracy of these estimates could be significantly improved by using crop-specific coefficients for biomass growth rates for non-forest land uses and tree types, as noted by the AT.

The forest degradation emission factor used in the FRLR and subsequently considers post-deforestation growth. The productivity growth rates were estimated based on a range of past harvests over four years. However, the AT indicates that forests may quickly accumulate in tropical regions, making it difficult to determine what extent the emission factor includes forest regrowth within different periods after logging.

Forest degradation emission factor assumes that all disturbance types generate the same amount of biomass regrowth. The most significant driver of forest degradation is fire, followed by selective logging. Other drivers affect 12% of the total forest area in PNG.

Additionally, gardening is considered a management practice leading to forest degradation in 8.2% of the forests in PNG. It was assumed that all forests subject to gardening were primary forests; however, their actual status could be mixed or secondary/primary forests. Therefore, this assumption may overestimate emissions from forest degradation.

The assumption that forest degradation occurs only once over time would underestimate emissions from forest degradation by excluding emissions from subsequent degradation in such areas.

The assumptions made by PNG to create emission factors applied in the FREE/LRF represent the trend in biomass regrowth after logging and fire. The AT notes that these assumptions are based on the most accurate information available at the time of the FREE/LRF. The sources of information used are primarily derived from the National Forest Inventory (NFI) and other national forest inventories.

PNG is currently working on implementing an NFI, which will enable the country to gather more accurate and reliable information to address issues related to deforestation and forest degradation emissions. The NFI will provide a more detailed picture of forest cover change by distinguishing different disturbance types. The NFI will also provide better information to enhance accuracy related to land use changes in areas where forests are subject to both logging and fire.

As indicated previously, the NFI has been implemented in the country since 2017; halted in 2020 due to the COVID-19 pandemic and funding shortfalls. It resumed in 2024 with financial support from the European Union (EU). The data collected by this initiative is expected to be secured until at least 2030. The data collected from this initiative is expected to contribute to the Second Biennial Report on Forests (BRT), which is planned for submission in 2026.

(viii) Sources of emissions:

PNG FREE/LRF included the most significant emission reduction activities: reducing emissions from deforestation, reducing emissions from forest degradation and enhancement of forest carbon stock. However, the AT notes that as part of the approved approach, the country may wish to broaden the scope of the FRLR by also assessing the significance of any other activities and considering their inclusion.


---
**Page 12**
---

especially the conservation of forest carbon stocks and sustainable management of forests, which were excluded owing to the lack of data to enable current estimations.

Sustainable forest management and conservation of forest carbon stock activities are sensitive to the carbon accounting methodologies applied in the context of large-scale land-use change and other human activities. As mentioned above, the lack of information to identify these activities was the reason to exclude them from this proposal.

However, the methodological approach applied by PNG in the collection of information to estimate activity data, interspersing time-series imagery to identify significant changes in forest cover or biomass on systematically sampled plots, allows for a reasonable estimation of net carbon stock change in forest land and other land uses. As such, these activities are included in the emission factors applied to estimate emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. This allows the country to internally offset any significant emissions related to sustainable forest management and conservation of forest carbon stock activities.

Therefore, all significant emissions or removals that occurred on PNG’s national territory have been included in the FREL/FRLR, and activities not considered were not significant or were integrated into other activities.


(Ix) Significant pools:

The PNG FREL/FRLR included emissions from above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass. Due to the lack of data, these emissions are estimated using a default value as described under the IPCC 2006 FREL/FRLR. Furthermore, based on Tier 1 estimations the excluded carbon pools are not significant in comparison to the total emissions reported in the FREL/FRLR.

The AT considered that the inclusion of dead wood would be insignificant since less than 85% of the population utilized fuel wood with a very high consumption level that was expected to increase in the following years (PNG Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Disaster Management, 2013).

Fox et al. (2010) estimate dead wood may constitute <75-30% of total above-ground biomass (i.e. 7-19% of total living biomass) for primary forest and logged forest respectively. In the case of forest degradation (2006), the proportion is estimated to be less than 40%. The IPCC 2006 Guidelines default value under Tier 1 (IPCC, 2006) and subsequently, emissions are zero. Therefore, emissions from deadwood would not be significant in comparison with other sources of emissions and their inclusion would be insignificant.

In the case of Soil Organic Carbon Emissions, considering that:

- 50-75% of PNG forest carbon could be held in the soil (McKee et al., 2016).
- Deforestation results in a loss of SOC of between 30–40% to 80–90% (IPCC 2006).
- Emissions from SOC in forest land remaining forest land is assumed in equilibrium under the IPCC Tier 1 method.

We can estimate that SOC emission in PNG would represent about a 1.2% of total annual emissions.

Finally, the AT commended PNG for its ongoing work on collecting country-specific data on deadwood, litter and SOC as part of the national forest inventory. This should enable the country to include litter in future updates.


(Em) Emissions from gases:

The FREL/FRLR does not account for emissions from non-CO2 GHG due to insufficient and likely insignificant data. Non-CO2 emissions can result from burning during forest degradation, drainage of organic soils

after deforestation, and carbon mineralisation post-deforestation: In PNG, approximately 3% of the forest is affected by fire, but the specific year and frequency of the fires are mostly unknown.

Additionally, there is a lack of reliable data on the distribution of organic soil and its drainage, which could have an impact on the results.

As part of the stepwise approach the AT considered that PNG may wish to monitor and potentially include the non-CO2 emissions from biomass burning in future FRL submissions if they are considered significant.


(xii) IPCC guidelines for FRE/LFR: Please indicate if the whether the construction of the FRE/LFR (data, methodologies and estimates) was guided by 2003 GPG or 2006 GLR.

The FRE/LFR construction by PNG utilised methodologies outlined in the 2008 FRL. Forest land use changes were determined using a combination of remote sensing data and ground truthing. Annual national emissions were calculated using emission factors specific to different forest categories, which were obtained from scientific literature and the 2005 IPCC Guidelines.


(xiii) Issues related to applying IPCC guidance: The most relevant issues related to the application of IPCC Guidelines raised during the TA Report were addressed in the following sections.

PNG derived the emissions factors for forest degradation from Fox et al. (2010). The study is based on the assumption that the biomass carbon stock in forested areas is lost at a rate of 45% per decade following deforestation, during the first four years. It was assumed that forest regrowth following forest degradation is implicitly included within the land use change category.

The AT noted that in tropical regions, biomass may quickly accumulate, making it very difficult to determine the extent to which the emission factors include forest regrowth. Plots may have been established a few months after deforestation and therefore do not reflect the full impact of forest degradation.


PNG explained that this approach is based on the most comprehensive and reliable data set currently available in the country to estimate C emissions from logging activities.

In addition, PNG noted that there are limitations with the use of remote sensing data as it does not capture all disturbance types (i.e., logging, fire, grazing, logging, portable sawmills, etc.) because the loss due to 45% of total biomass carbon stock is considered a future activity. However, disturbances other than logging affect about 12.0% of the total forest area in PNG.

Therefore, the AT considered that accurate determination of the losses of biomass carbon stocks resulting from forest degradation and deforestation is difficult using remote sensing data alone. This may be an area for future improvement. PNG noted that the ongoing national forest inventory covers different disturbance types but does not provide sufficient information to fully provide the need to implement this approach and improve the accuracy of future reports.


(xiv) Reference period for the FRE/LFR: The FRE/LFR recalculation proposed to the GCF RSB is based on a five-year reference period from 2009 to 2013. PNG is proposing a recalculations of the FRE/LFR, as assessed by the UNFCCC TA to meet the requirements for REDD+.


According to the scoring criteria, the proposal will not if the FRE/LFR is not based on average annual baseline emissions over the reference period and if the total carbon stock exceeds 0.1% of the carbon stock during the eligibility period, and also 10% of the FRL.


As stated in the GCF RSB Terms of Reference footnote 22 “Counties that have already submitted FRE/LFRs to the UNFCCC TA for assessment in 2016 will be able to recalculate a new FRE/LFR and results based on submitted and harmonized data, provided that they are using the same methodologies and criteria as those used by PNG and using the same methodologies”, and complemented by the Project for results-based payments for REDD+ (USG/BI-0017) footnote 48. “The recalculated FRE/FSL shall not be higher than the original

# REDD+ Pilot RBP Funding Proposal

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 11 of 9)

### Calculated FRELFLR if the recalculated FRELFLR is higher than the original calculated FRELFLR shall be used.

The assessed PNG FRELFLR, based on a linear regression of historical emissions from 2001 to 2013, was significantly lower than the published value of the GCF Trust Fund. Therefore, the PNG has recalculated the FRELFLR replacing the linear regression model with historical average emissions and adding an uncertainty factor corresponding to 5.1% of the carbon stock over the eligibility period to comply with the scorecard requirements.

The original reference period (2001-2013) was used in the assessment, but if a regression model is applied by PNG for the period 2004-2013, the recalculated FRELFLR would be higher. PNG has recalculated the FRELFLR considering the reference period 2009-2015 to better represent the current scenario in the country.

The FRELFLR proposed by PNG is significantly lower than the original assessed FRELFLR, so the recalculations of the FRELFLR are considered conservative.

### (xiv) If previous reference level submitted:

No previous reference level has been submitted by PNG.

### (xv) Uncertainties:

PNG FRELFLR included a quantitative and qualitative uncertainty analysis; however, the lack of quantitative information on errors in forest carbon stocks and emission factors prevented the country from reporting this aggregated uncertainty of the FRELFLR.

Regarding the uncertainties associated with the FRELFLR, PNG identified the following sources:
- Major potential sources contributing to the uncertainty of the Collected Earth sampling assessment and the activity data estimations are related to the sampling error, such as:
  - spatial variability
  - variability resulting from the use samples.
- Several important error sources must also be considered regarding emission factors. The emission factors were taken from the literature, and error information is only available for a small portion of the sources.

In the case of quantitative uncertainty, the PNG estimates and reports the uncertainties corresponding to:
- Sampling error for land use classes used and land use conversion from 2000 to 2015.
- Only information on the sampling error in forest carbon stocks was not yet available.

The country is able to report their most detailed information to report uncertainties in the Technical Annexes reported within BURJ1RFLR and as the Second FRELFLR, which are the latest most reliable updates of the data.

The aggregated Uncertainty of the recalculations of the FRELFLR used for this submission (i.e., historical average) was estimated using the uncertainty propagation approach based on the 2006 IPCC Equation 2.13.

Activity data uncertainty for deforestation was 25.52% and 48.53% for forest degradation, while emissions factor uncertainty resulted in 10.07% to both cases. The propagated uncertainty for each activity was 25-39%, with a mean of 30%.

PNG estimates uncertainty based on the error propagation approach (Tier I/ Tier II IPCC). The country plans to quantify uncertainties using the IPCC Equation 2.13 and propagate them through the calculation.

Beyond that, the application of DAQCO procedures have been improved, and new methodologies to estimate activity data are under consideration for future FRELFLR. However, the most relevant measure to reduce uncertainty is the continuous improvement in the implementation and interpretation of the NFI, which will allow the country to use more details and reliable data in future reports.

#### B.2.1. UNFCCC Technical Analysis

**(i) Consistency of results with FREL/FRL**

PNG is seeking funding from the GCF RBP programme, considering the results achieved in 2014, 2015, and 2016 as reported to the GCF in the BUR technical annexes (including the technical annexes for results of 2014 and 2015) and BUR2 (including the technical annexes for results of 2014, 2015, and 2016).

The assumed FREL/FRL submitted in 2017 was based on a linear regression model to represent the increasing deforestation trend in PNG. This approach, allowed by the UNFCCC guidelines, does not make use of the historical average emissions from 2009 to 2013 as required under the GCF RBP. Therefore, the results proposed to the GCF RBP Programme are lower than those reported to the UNFCCC technical annex in 2014 (the results proposed to the GCF were 5.5 MIOtCO₂ and 1.55 MIOtCO₂ for the corresponding period of BUR1 and BUR2, respectively) and higher than those reported to the UNFCCC for the corresponding period of 2014–2016 (see Table 3).

Activity data was generated using the same sample-based approach, systematic grid, data interpretation tool, classification system, and satellite imagery.

Emission factors used in BUR1 (2019) and BUR2 (2022), and the technical annexes of results are derived from the same source. These emission factors primarily rely on the study by Fox et al. (2015) and the default values provided under IPCC 2006 guidelines.

The FREL/FRL and the technical annexes to BUR1 and BUR2 considered the same pools, gases, activities, and forest definition.

## The AT in the TARs that PNG has improved the methodology for testing post-distribution regressions to increase the accuracy of estimations. The methodological improvement is used to increase the accuracy of emissions estimates and the associated uncertainties as described above.

PNG is proposing a recalculation of the FREL/FRLR under the GCF RBP based on the average emission factor (AEF) for each reporting period. This methodological improvement is not applied in the recalculation of the FREL/FRLR, as the linear regression is not applied.

Therefore, the proposed FREL/FRLR and technical annexes of results maintain complete consistency.

### (i) Transparency of the data:

During the Technical Assessment process, PNG provided additional information to the AT to increase the transparency of the data used for estimating emissions.

The most relevant additional information was related to the Collected Earth methods and procedures, including the spreadsheet used in analysis at sample plots to calculate activity data. The AT reviewed these spreadsheets and confirmed that the calculations of emission factors were consistent with the general steps of the statistical procedure.

Detailed information on the data used for estimating emissions is available on the following link (https://png-rfms.org/) for further consultations and public availability upon request.

### (ii) Completeness of the data:

During the assessment of the BUR 1 (para. 23) and BUR 2 (para. 19) Technical Annexes, the only issue noted by the AT was related to the use of a single sampling intensity for all sample plots in the statistical procedure used to estimate Activity Data (AD) based on the sample plots, and of the raw data used to calculate the AEF.

However, as stated in the TA corresponding to the BUR Annexes (para. 38), the procedure PNG followed was based on a single sampling factor for the Collected Earth plots, as follows:

- Plot Sampling Intensity for Smaller Larger Proportion

The decision to use different sampling intensities reflects a balance between statistical considerations and practical constraints. The AT noted that using smaller sampling intensities (0.02 degrees) captures spatial variability accurately, while larger proportions of sample plots (0.4 degrees) ensure efficient use of resources without compromising data quality.

This approach is consistent with the geographical and ecological characteristics of the area being assessed.

- Differentiating Anthropogenic and Non-anthropogenic Changes

PNG addressed this issue in its response to this issue, which included:

- Methodology: Use of satellite imagery and ground truthing to identify anthropogenic changes.
- Historical data: PNG has used historical data for comparison.
- IPCC Guidelines: Application of the IPCC 2006 Guidelines provides a framework for differentiating anthropogenic impacts from natural events.

By the time of the TAR, PNG had developed methodology effectively distinguished anthropogenic from non-anthropogenic changes.

### Statistical Considerations

PNG addressed these concerns by:

- Ensuring consistency in the statistical methodologies used for activity data collection and analysis in subsequent submissions (e.g., BUR2, FRLR).
- Sharing comprehensive databases, spreadsheets, and assumptions with the TA team during the assessment.
- This resolution assures that:
  - The data used to calculate 2015 calculations are complete, accurate, and transparent.
  - The same applies to the 2016 results, ensuring methodological consistency across reporting periods.

**Page 17**

**(iv) Consistency of the data:**

During the TA process of the Technical Annexes included in BUR1 and BUR2, the AT raised no issues related to the consistency of the data across the time series.

**Accuracy of the data:**

The AT noted that the report is overall accurate; however, several issues related to the accuracy of the data were raised. These relate to the use of the FRELFLR, which is currently being implemented in PNG.

The issues raised by the AT are primarily focused on maintaining consistency between the FRELFLR and the results. Therefore, the issues raised during the TA of the FRELFLR are valid for the Technical Annexes as well.

Assumptions about the trend in biomass stock in different types of lands affected by deforestation and forest degradation are based on the most accurate information available at the time of the FRELFLR. The sources used to develop these assumptions include data from PNG (2015), GIZ (2016) and GTZ (2017).

As mentioned in Section B.1.1, the most relevant points raised during the TA are the following:

- The emission factors for deforestation used in the BUR1 and BUR2 Technical Annexes subtract removals from the forest area. This assumption is not consistent with the fact that the accuracy of these estimations could be significantly improved by using crop-specific post-deforestation (subsequent) gross rates for non-fires and fires.

- The AT noted that it was assumed in the technical annexes that all disturbance types generate the same amount of carbon biomass stock loss of primary forests as commercial logging. While commercial logging is one major driver of forest degradation, other drivers affect 12% of the total forest area in PNG.

- The forest degradation emission factor used in the technical annexes also contains post-disturbance regrowth. This assumption is not consistent with the fact that the AT noted that sampling of plots harvested within the last four years. The AT indicates that biomass may quickly accumulate to pre-disturbance levels, which could lead to an overestimation of what extent the emission factor includes forest regrowth within different periods after logging.

- Gardening is considered a management practice leading to forest degradation in 6.2% of the forests in PNG. However, the AT noted that this assumption may be incorrect as the definition of forest stock may not be comparable to that of primary forests. Therefore, this assumption may overestimate emissions from forest degradation.

- The assumption that forest degradation occurs only once over time would understate emissions from forest degradation by excluding emissions from subsequent degradation in such areas.

However, PNG is currently working on implementing an NFI, which will enable the country to gather more accurate information about the extent of forest degradation. This will allow for a better understanding of different factors, including post-deforestation and post-forest degradation regrowth rates, considering different types of land use. The NFI will also provide more accurate estimates of emissions from sustainable forest management and track emissions from forest degradation over time.

In this regard, the AT noted that the inclusion of an NFI in the REDD+ project would improve the accuracy of emission factors. This improvement of the data will reduce the accuracy issues related to the use of a single emission factor despite the forest degradation drivers and the initial forest condition (e.g., gardening).

**(v) Indicate the number of years that took place between the last year of the FRELFLR period, and the year corresponding to the results being proposed for payments:**

The recalculation of the FRELFLR is based on the historical emissions from 2009-2013, and the proposed FRELFLR is for the period 2014-2016.

### B.2.2 Additional criteria related to the achieved results

#### (vi) Uncertainty analysis

Section 5 of the BUR1 Technical Annex and Section 8 of the Technical Annex are included in the BUR2 report on the uncertainty of the results, including a detailed section on qualitative and other quantitative uncertainties. The technical annexes were developed by PNG and reviewed by the GCF team.

In BURG the technical team identified that the propagation of uncertainty was incorrect. Therefore, the current uncertainty analysis is based on the BURG report, which includes information relative to the REDD+ annual results from 2014 to 2018.

The quantitative uncertainty analysis aggregates the uncertainty on activity data and emission factors for 2014-2016 results. The error propagation method (IPCC Tier I) is used in this analysis.

Sampling errors on activity data was estimated using the FAO Land Use Category and Conversion spreadsheet:

* For 2014-2016 results period, activity data uncertainty: Was Stable Forest 0.7%, Stable non-forest 2.4%, Deforestation 32.5%, Forest degradation 14.3%.
* For 2016-2018 results period, activity data uncertainty: Was Stable Forest 0.7%, Stable non-forest 2.4%, Deforestation 29.4%, Forest degradation 13.5%.

Emission Factors uncertainty is estimated based on the available information and aggregated applying error propagation method (IPCC Tier I) as per Guidelines to finally obtain the uncertainty by aggregated land use and land use change category:

* Primary forest degradation: 11.8%.
* Degradation of secondary forest: 17.3%.
* Forest degradation: 15.1%.

Aggregated total uncertainty and REDD+ activity included in the reports are:

* **2014-2016 Results:** Deforestation 34.33%, Forest degradation 19.87%, Degradation of secondary forest 25.50%.
* **2016-2018 Results:** Degradation of secondary forest: 11.1%.
o Forest degradation: 18.81%.

PNG uses the error propagation method (IPCC Tier I) but is considering improving the uncertainty analysis using a more robust approach in future reports.

Activity data procedures are implementing actions to minimize the systematic error. PNG has implemented a QA/QC process by applying available tools, such as Saks, to identify error plots that subsequently have been removed from the dataset. PNG is also working on developing a new tool for systematically applying a systematic warning system to re-evaluate all plots that are not disturbed but where GFIP showed some level of uncertainty and there may be other disturbances or land use changes around it.

To include systematic error in future reports, the most relevant measure to be implemented in future reports from PNG is updating emission factors by applying the information collected within the NFI under development. This will allow PNG to have more reliable and accurate information, improve uncertainty analysis, and potentially reduce it.

#### (vii) Preventing double payments

PNG is analysing its participation in other jurisdictional payments for REDD+ results that are different from the GCF RBP, and some initiatives are in place registered in the voluntary carbon markets.

The country submitted a proposal in April 2021, followed by the agreement of a Memorandum of Understanding in December 2021, which is the initial stage of analysis its participation in the LEAF Carbon Market. The REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal is based on the national scale proposal for a crediting period from 2017 to 2021. Therefore, it does not overlap with the results of the LEAF carbon market project. The REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal is based on the FREL/FRLR based on the ART TREES rules and requirements (i.e., applying a different reference period and HFC emissions factor) and the GCF FP Output 1.1, which includes land use change and emission factors. The principal components of the PNG LEAF Proposal partially complement the Action Area 3 of the REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal. This document is intended to accompany the proposal to the GCF.

- **Sustainable land planning (NRS Action Area 1 - GCF FP Output 1.1):** Includes support for the development of Sustainable Land Use Policy and Regulation, a National Land Base Information System, as well as Ward, LLG, District and Provincial Plans to include spatial elements that provide public access.
- **Enhanced forest management and plantation development (NRS Action Area 3 - GCF FP Output 1.1):** Includes support for the implementation of the PNG Forest Management Act 2006, including legal standards and enhances knowledge of planted timber and PNG forests.
- **Developing a national carbon market (NRS Action Area 3 - GCF FP Output 1.1):** Includes Action Area 3 - GCF FP Output 1.1: Considers the development and management of environmental safeguards and protected areas.
- **Sustainable agriculture (NRS Action Area 3 - GCF FP Output 2.2):** Based on the expansion of actions taken to address the growth of commercial agriculture and the establishment of Sustainable Planting Areas.

Beyond this, PNG was a founding member of the Coalition of Rainforest Nations (CRN) and has listed its reported results for the 2014-2015 period as 6,032.214 tCO2e on the groups Sovereign REDD+ Credit Reporting System (SRS). The CRN is a coalition of developing countries that aims to ensure that REDD+ is more easily accessible, with information on just one site now listed Blackstone’s plant purchase of 8.06 metric tonnes of carbon credits from PNG. This transaction was reported as 12,543 tCO2e and the second largest at 8,976.749 tCO2e as based. Based on this information, it can be inferred that these two transactions represent approximately 10% of the total emissions reductions under the CRN scheme. The carbon credits from this scheme will be accounted for in the Lima REDD+ Information Hub and subtracted from the amount of emission reductions available to PNG.

The Memorandum of Understanding between the Government of PNG and the CRN has expired and will not be renewed, with no further agreements between the parties to transfer additional emission reductions under the scheme.

Regarding the voluntary carbon markets at the project level scale, the only relevant project developed in PNG is the Apel Satuemo REDD+ Project. The project was registered on the Voluntary Carbon Standard (VCS) registry at https://registry.veritas.org/project/standards/VCS/11223. The Project aims to protect 603.579ha of forest and its biodiversity in the Apel Satuemo area, located in the Western Province of PNG and benefit local communities. The project creation period starts date is a 22nd May 2009. Since then, the Project has issued credits for the period from 1st January 2008 to 31st December 2017 corresponding to the reference period applied to the FREL/FRLR proposed to the GCF. The total carbon credits issued under the Apel Satuemo REDD+ Project as of the end of the GCF RBP proposal, the total carbon credits issued were 120,000 tCO2e (195,000 including 2017-18), distributed as follows:

| Year | Credits |

| 2014: 70,000 |
| 2015: 35,000 |
| 2016: 15,000 |
| 2017: 15,000 |
| 2018: 15,000 |

In addition to the emissions reductions transferred under the CRN scheme, PNG will subtract the carbon credits issued from the Apel Satuemo REDD+ Project from the REDD+ results offered to the GCF RBP.

Since 2008, PNG has embraced numerous multilateral and bilateral initiatives to advance the readiness and implementation phases of REDD+.

The UNDP/UNREDD Programme, with a grant of US$ 6.4 million, empowered PNG’s REDD+ readiness phase from 2011 to 2015. This initiative laid the groundwork for robust management arrangements, strengthened institutional capacity in the forestry sector and provided technical support on FREL/FRL and fostered stakeholder engagement in the REDD+ process at the national level.

Furthermore, the Readiness Fund of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), supported PNG from 2013 to 2015. This initiative was instrumental in strengthening the institutional arrangements for the REDD+ Finance and Investment Plan (RFIP), enhanced the management framework for REDD+, strengthened stakeholder engagement on FREL/FRL, and provided technical support on FREL/FRL. Despite being an FCPF Readiness country, PNG has made neither progress nor has it planned to participate in the signing of an Emission Reduction Purchase Agreement (ERPA) with the World Bank (WB) Carbon Fund.

During the crediting period, PNG received several additional grants to improve capacities and fund pilot activities under REDD+.

• US $7.5 million from the European Union through the FAD to provide “Technical Support to the Papua New Guinea Forest Sector” in the areas of forest management and capacity building. The project ran from 2013 to 2019. The project aimed To support the PNG Forest Authority to implement a continuous and multipurpose NFI which is a key component of a NFMIS that is required to establish a robust REDD+ system in PNG. The project also aimed to strengthen the initiative aimed to create an NFI data-sharing system for transparency and stakeholder engagement, leading to better decision-making.

• US $9.9 million from the GEF to implement the project “PNG: Community-Based Forest and Coastal Conservation Project” (CBFCCP) in the years 2014-2018.

• US $10.9 million from the GEF for the project “RPG: Strengthening the Management Effectiveness of the Papua New Guinea Forest Service” (RPG). The project ran from 2013 to 2017 and aimed to improve the management effectiveness of the PNGFS through capacity building, institutional strengthening and improved governance.

• US $8.8 million from the GEF for the support “the Preparation of Papua New Guinea’s Initial Biennial Update Report (BUR) under the UNFCCC” (PAPUANUG BUR). The project ran from 2013 to 2014.

• US $5.0 million from JICA for the project “Capacity Development for the Operationalization of the PNG Forest Resources Information Management System.” From 2014 to 2019.

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## (ix) Tracking Emissions Reductions:

PNG, with the support of GCF and FAO, has established a reliable registry system developed as part of the GCF Readiness for registry and meting system to facilitate climate-related investment in agriculture, forestry, and other land use sectors. The project was completed on 30 June 2023.

Before the project start date, PNG had faced increasing interest in developing sub-national actions and projects under various REDD+ initiatives, including the GCF Readiness for registry and meting system and required PNG to strengthen its regulatory and guidance framework, build capacity to manage engagement with stakeholders, and develop a Registry and Meting System (RMS).

The aim of the readiness project proposal was to build a Registry and Meting System that will enable PNG to engage in REDD+ projects, support sector engagement in reducing emissions through REDD+, and investments in the AFGOL sector.

### The objective of the REDD+ Registry Data Management System (RMS) is to monitor emission reductions and provide information on project performance. It also provides a platform for reporting on results-based payments under various REDD+ initiatives, including the GCF RBP (plus) programme.

The REDD+ Registry RMS is a repository for crucial information regarding REDD+ actions, encompassing project details, landowner information, and other relevant data related to REDD+ projects. It makes this data accessible to stakeholders for informed decisions.

Furthermore, the REDD+ registry enhances the national implementation framework, facilitating the trade of emission reductions between PNG and other countries. The RMS is a key component within this framework, emphasizing its importance for the successfully launching of the Registry and Meting System in PNG.

The PNG Registry System is accessible at the following link: https://pregisdev.shinyapps.io/pg-regist-reddplus-registry/

The registry includes details about projects and programs currently being implemented or in development, providing a Project Summary. All national projects underway at a subnational scale, along with the projects in implementation and those under evaluation are listed.

For project implementation, the system displays information regarding the exact location and boundaries of the project, including details about the proponent, area, methodology applied, scope (including landowner information), project status, project type, project start date, estimated costs, and more.

To fully register a REDD+ project, the proponent must provide information about landowners (such as Indigenous Land Groups [ILG] certifications), a Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) proposal, benefit sharing and Generances Redress Mechanism (GRM), and approval or support letters from local institutions. This is with a Project Concept Note or Project Design Document, and a Project Registration Request Form.

The REDD+ Registry RMS summarizes the REDD+ projects registered and under evaluation in PNG.

The legal framework for forest programs in PNG is the National Forest Policy (National Forest Policy 1991). The PNG Government has established the Forestry and Fisheries Department and the National Forest Development Programmes, the National Forest Development Guidelines and the Forest and Climate Change Strategy (FCCS), which set out national priorities and targets for forest development, also captured within the National REDD+ Strategy (NRS).

The National Constitution sets the overall basis for these forest programmes: the Fourth National Goal and Direction of PNG is to "protect and conserve the environment". These policies emphasize the sustainable use of forest resources, biodiversity conservation, and the involvement of customary landowners and communities in forest management. They focus on reducing deforestation and degradation while ensuring that forests are managed sustainably for livelihoods.

The National Community Development Programmes translate these policies into action by promoting reforestation, supporting community-based livelihoods like agroforestry and eco-tourism, enhancing the capacity of customary landowners to manage their lands, and encouraging sustainable forest management. These programmes are aligned with PNG’s development priorities, including international cooperation, aligned with global sustainability goals such as REDD+ and climate finance.

In addition, PNG's development strategies are committed to protecting its natural resources and ecosystems while promoting sustainable food systems for its people:

- **PNG Vision 2050**, which aims to ensure that all natural resources are used sustainably.
- **Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)**, which were launched in 2016 to transform the nation's mindset and attitude and align the people, institutions, and systems into an educated, healthy, and prosperous society.
- The National Forest Policy 1991, the National Forest Development Strategy: Strategic Plan 2010–2030 and subsequent Medium-Term Development Plans (MTDPs), guiding each of these strategies.

The National Strategy for Responsible and Sustainable Development (SRSD) outlines the goals of responsible development in PNG. It is a national strategy that provides direction to action across different sectors. It focuses on green agriculture, forest conservation, biodiversity preservation, and the development of sustainable small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).

Furthermore, climate change is a key priority for PNG:

- Climate Change Action Plan for PNG: The Climate Compatible Development Action Plan established in 2010.
- The Climate Compatible Development Management Policy (CCDMP)

In PNG, Indigenous Peoples constitute nearly the entire population. They live in traditional villages and communities in the rural areas of the country. These communities are referred to as customary landowners or customary communities. They refer to the main group of people who have a common ancestor and share a common language and culture.

The National Forest Policy (NFP) refers to customary landowners and communities as "referring to the main group of people who have a common ancestor and share a common language and culture". The NFP also states that customary landowners are defined in terms of their tenure rights over forest resources. It has been stated, among other documents, in the PNG’s REDD+ Strategy, that activities for achieving this objective under this NFP will be guided by the customary land tenure system.

The term "customary land tenure" refers to legal land tenure rights deriving from customary law. In this paper, the term "customary land tenure" is used to refer to legal land tenure rights deriving from customary law. Under PNG’s REDD+ Strategy, activities for achieving this objective under this NFP will be guided by the customary land tenure system.

The UNDRIP (United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples) recognizes that indigenous peoples have the right to own, use, and occupy their lands, territories, and resources. The UNDRIP defines "customary land" as land which is owned or held in common under traditional law by a group of people who are members of an indigenous community.

The FAO has had two other terms used within the National REDD+ administration may also be used to term “customary landowners”, such as “traditional leaders” and “community-based forest management”. The AE will assess the relevance of preparing an IP: traditional challenges in customary. Depending on their demographic composition, communities can be defined by age, gender, ethnicity, or other criteria. For example, a community may be women-, youth- marginalized and vulnerable groups who do not necessarily have the collective rights of customary landowners, but may have been dispossessed out right to land and resources over time; and the FTCs are the customary landowners.

## Transparent and effective national forest governance structures, taking into account national legislation and sovereignty

Comments:

As outlined in the Safeguard Sol., the national interpretation of Country Safeguard B for the implementation of REDD+ activity is issued and reported under four sub-components:
- Providing and disseminating information included in the Right to Freedom of Information under the National Freedom of Information Act 2014.
- Access to information, including providing public access to documents and decisions concerning forest management.
- Adherence to relevant laws and regulations applied to all agriculture subject matter affecting land accounting, auditing processes.
- The implementation of a transparent and effective national forest governance structure is complemented by the land titling processes, emphasizing gender equality in land and forest benefits distribution.

Dispute resolution mechanisms are established by the Constitution at various government levels, respecting customary law. These mechanisms include mediation, conciliation, arbitration, by-passing processes within the traditional legal system, the courts, and alternative dispute resolution mechanisms.

Almost all forests and land in PNG, 97% of the land, are under customary ownership or possession. These areas are managed through customary tenure systems that vary across different regions. Landowners and communities must form ILGs established under the Land Group Incorporation Act of 1984 (LGI Act) to register their customary lands with the PNG Government.

Efforts to formalize customary lands through registration have faced challenges. The Government of PNG introduced the Voluntary Customary Land Registration (VCLR) system in 2005 to encourage land registration and provide a legal framework for customary land management. As at 2017, only about 3% of all customary lands were registered under VCLR. In 2009, the National Land Act allowed communities to register their land through ILGs, less than 1% of the total land area has been registered.

The Special Agriculture Business Lease (SABL) mechanism, included in the 1998 Land Act, allows ILGs to lease customary land to the state, which can then lease it back for 50 years without rent. As of 2017, SABLs have been issued on about 3% of all customary lands registered under VCLR and less than 1% since then.

To address these challenges, the 2018 National Land Summit adopted several key revisions aimed at reforming land administration in a holistic and sustainable manner. One of the primary initiatives

# REDD+plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 23 OF 9)

This initiative seeks to enhance social equity and support community development.

### Harmonized Tolerance Processes

These processes are also central to the reform agenda. They include streamlining and harmonizing land tenure registration, dispute resolution mechanisms, and forest management plans for greater efficiency and transparency. Such measures aim to reduce costs, encourage private sector participation, and promote sustainable land use practices.

### Summit Emphasis on Revising Dispute Resolution Mechanisms

The summit emphasized the need to review and strengthen dispute resolution mechanisms. This includes improving access to justice through legal aid services and ensuring that customary rights are respected using appropriate methods that respect traditional practices while aligning with modern legal principles.

### Critical Issues for Future Voluntary Customary Land Registration (VCLR) System

The summit addressed critical issues concerning the future of VCLR, including its alignment with the National Forest Act and Forest Policy from 1991. The policy created key institutions such as PNG Forest Authority (PNGFA), Papua New Guinea Forestry Service (PNGFS), and the National Forest Management Committee (NFMCC). These mechanisms have significant implications for land tenure and resource management, being closely linked with customary rights and national development goals.

### PNGFA's Role in Land Administration

PNGFA is overseen by the National Forest Board, which advises the Minister for Forests and gives directions to PNGFA. The board also oversees the implementation of the Logging Code of Practice (LCP), National Planning & Monitoring, Conservation & Environment Protection Authority, PNG Forest Industry Development Strategy, and other related matters. It works closely with key stakeholders in the forest sector, namely Provincial and Local Level Governments, Landowners, Women's Groups, and Indigenous People.

### Sub-Provincial Levels

At the sub-provincial level, there are Provincial Forest Management Committees (PFMC), one for each province, which assist the National Forest Board in fulfilling the Objectives and Functions of PNGFA. The PFMCs provide technical advice to the provincial governments on forest management issues and services for the Region, the Local Level Government, the Civil Society and customary landowners.

### Conservation & Environment Protection Authority (CEPA)

In parallel, the Conservation & Environment Protection Authority (CEPA) established under the Conservation Act 1984, is responsible for conservation of natural resources. It works closely with other agencies Coupled with other conservation and environmental legislation. It sets out the powers, functions and duties of CEPA in relation to the protection and management of forests, wildlife, wetlands, standards and regulations as well as conservation areas and/or protected areas within PNG.

### Other Administration Bodies

Other administration bodies are also relevant to the land resources governance, such as the Department of Agriculture and Livestock (DAL), which is responsible for land use planning and allocation to individual sub-sections created through community boards (e.g., Cocoa and Coffee Boards) and the Department of Land Management (DLM), which administers the registration of matters on land use permits, land ownership, land development, planning and zoning in PNG.

Regarding climate change topics, including REDD+, the CCDA is the Designated National Authority (DNA) for PNG under the UNFCCC. CCDA has the mandate for the central coordination of all climate change activities in PNG.


CCDA is the national entity established by virtue of the CCDA Act 2015 and is solely responsible for governing all matters pertaining to climate change management in the country. The CCDA sets out the core operational principles, including the establishment of a National REDD+ Steering Committee with roles and responsibilities related to climate-related programs, projects and activities.


This role aims to leverage the national REDD+ process-effectively. PNG entered enabling agreements with the following institutions: the National REDD+ Steering Committee (NRC), the National REDD+ Steering Committee Board (NRCB), the National REDD+ Steering Committee Secretariat (NRC-S), the National REDD+ Steering Committee Independent Review Panel (NRC-IRP), the National REDD+ Steering Committee Independent Verification Panel (NRC-IVP), and the National REDD+ Steering Committee Independent Monitoring, Reporting and Verification Panel (NRC-MRV) pool of local REDD+ Experts.


### (ii) Respect for the knowledge and rights of indigenous peoples and members of local communities, by taking into account relevant international obligations, national circumstances and customary laws. The PNG Constitution has adopted the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

Comments:

**Rights of customary landowners and communities are promoted and protected.**

The National Constitution of PNG provides a standard criterion for defining 'Indigenous People' in PNG, which is that they are people who have lived in PNG since time immemorial. The term "Indigenous People" is not used in the Constitution or in relevant PNG laws. The legal framework rather uses terms such as 'aboriginal', 'traditional owner', and 'customary landowner'. These definitions are elaborated further in Acts of Parliament relating to land and natural resources.

The Constitutional recognition of indigenous peoples' rights is reflected in the National Land Policy, which provides that customary law should be given effect. The policy also states that it is the responsibility of the Underlying Laws governing customs and traditions and the associated rights. These are also defined by customary laws.


This legal framework outlines all available means of ensuring sufficient remedies to address any discrimination against customary landowners and communities.

The right of self-determination of Indigenous Peoples is recognized in the National Constitution and detailed where it provides for the establishment of a National Land Policy Act and to recognize this right through the primary forms of traditional decision-making structures, consistent with the international principles of free, prior and informed consent.


PNG's Constitution recognizes the collective rights of forest ownership in PNG. It establishes E.G. for the relevant land tenure systems. The Constitution also provides that customary laws relating to land tenure shall be given effect.

The Constitution recognizes the rights of customary landowners and communities in terms of the inherent right to manage their natural resources following their traditional time immemorial. This is supported by the National Land Policy, which states that it is the responsibility of the Underlying Laws governing customs and traditions and the associated rights.


Constitution section 53 recognizes the rights of citizens in situations in which there has been an unjust deprivation of property or land, particularly customary land, and provides for the process to be followed within the Constitution. The National Land Policy Act also provides for compensation under this Act. It provides for a monetary form of compensation to be awarded; however, a mitigation hierarchy approach will be applied, and other forms of compensatory non-monetary measures are also considered.


*Activities to be supported by GCF RBP proceeds will not result in any kind of land deprivation or land/resource use displacement, as per the GCF and FAO's applicable safeguards policies, as established in the exclusion list of this RBP's Environmental and Social Management Framework, as discussed in section 1.2 E.6 in Annex I - ESMP.*

### Traditional Knowledge is Recognized and Protected.

The legal framework outlines a clear objective towards safeguarding customary landowners and communities' rights as they relate to culture. It also indirectly includes a respect for their identity, customs, traditions, and practices.

Various legislation promotes the need to maintain cultural heritage through the establishment of institutions and systems that support and facilitate its preservation.

### Traditional Knowledge

Traditional knowledge is recognized by the Constitution.

Traditional knowledge is also known as "customary practice," which refers to the customs or usual practices associated with a particular society, place, or set of circumstances.

The legal framework safeguards the rights of customary landowners, respecting their identity, customs, traditions, and practices.

### Full and Effective Participation

The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, is referred to in paragraphs 70 and 72 of ICOP-16.

#### Comments:

Stakeholders are effectively mapped, consulted and participated effectively.

The National Goals and Directive Principles, 2 and ss.37 and 55 of the National Constitution provide for equal opportunities for all citizens in the exercise of their rights and duties. The decision-making process and makes it mandatory for customary resource owners and local communities to be present in decision making bodies concerning land use planning and management.

The national forest policy (NFP) provides that the participation of customary owners is seen in the National Forest Board, the Provincial Forest Management Committees (PFMCs), and the District Forest Management Committees (DFMCs).

Participation by all key stakeholders provided for under sections 98, 113 and 116 of the OLPPLA is seen in the establishment of the National Forest Policy Committee, the National Forest Board, and Technical Working Groups during the various transactions concerning the use of forest resources, especially land.

The responsibilities of all respective agencies in relation to public participation are defined within forestry and natural resource PLRs. Forestry regulations set out timeframes for PLRs. PLRs provide for the Right to Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC) under section 105 of the Environment Planning Act (EPA). Access is free, subject to certain restrictions.

There are also provisions outlining the process of providing and receiving information on land use planning and management in sections 162, 163, 164, 165, 167, 168 and Item 169 of the Forestry Regulations (1998).

Referrals of applications must state the reason and be recorded in a public registry established under Section 105 of the EPA. The public registry is open to all interested parties for inspection through transparent internal administrative processes (Section 14 of the Environment Planning Act) and external administrative processes (Sections 23, 26 and 27 of the Environment Planning Act). Access to the Register is subject to the payment of a fee (mainly in the case of applications for land use planning and management, including development plans and development projects); however, on-ground activities to be supported by GCF proceeds that will need to be carried out under Section 105 of the EPA are exempt from registration fees and GRM procedures at no cost.

The PLRs provide for an alternative option in the pursuit of further legal remedies (Section 20 of the Environment Planning Act, 1978) despite also using internal/administrative dispute resolution processes as provided under Section 18 of the Environmental Planning Act. They stipulate the timely delivery of rulings as well.

### Right to participate

The PLRs provide for legally recognized traditional structures used by customary landowners to engage with government, communities and other stakeholders in relation to planning and implementing development of their land and natural resources (The Forestry Act 1991 and forestry Regulations 1998). This is recognized and provided for within forestry, land and customary law.
