Comments:

PNG defines forests as "land spanning more than 1 hectare, with trees higher than 3 meters and the canopy cover of more than 10 per cent (t.)" and distinguishes them from forest plantations.

The National Constitution and relevant policies conserve the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity. The PNG Forest Act (2014) provides for the control of conversion in both public and private forests through environmental impact assessments and mitigation measures. The controls are required for large-scale forest conversion, road clearance, and agricultural activities.

REDD+ actions, policies and measures described in the NRS focus on:

- Improving forest governance and management by strengthening collaboration with national and local development initiatives, focusing on climate and REDD+ development indicators.
- Enhancing capacity building through training and awareness raising to improve change leadership, financing, and management. This includes forest management and enforcement practices and community engagement.
- Boosted economic productivity and sustainable livelihoods by fostering a sustainable commercial agriculture sector, strengthening food security, and increasing the productivity of small-scale family farming.

During the period of results (2014–2016), PNG advanced biodiversity conservation through updated policies and strategies. These include the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan 2015-2030, the National Forest Policy (2014), aligning with international Aichi Biodiversity Targets, and the establishment of the Conservation Areas Act (2014). The REDD+ strategy was also updated in line with the National Development Area Policy (2014) aimed to expand and manage conservation zones, while climate change efforts for the REDD+ program were initiated. The Forest Management Act (2014), the Wildlife Management Act and Forestry Act targeted sustainable resource use and illegal activities. Community-driven conservation initiatives were also strengthened through the establishment of community-based agriculture, mining, and fisheries further underlined PNG's commitment to balancing development with ecological preservation.

(vi) Actions to address the risks of reversals.

**Page 30**

The system is in place to identify and prevent reversal of REDD+ based on the NFMS. The NFMS was designed to provide information at the appropriate frequency to detect and provide information on reversals and displacement of emissions.

NFMS consists of a monitoring function to assess the implementation and impact of national policies and measures (NPMs) in the land use, land-use change, and forestry (LULUCF) sector. The NREDD’s Satellite Land Use Monitoring System (SLUMS) comprises TerreNet, a system for creating land-use, land-cover maps at 10m resolution, and the Forest Resource Information Management System (FRIMS), which provides detailed information on land use change.

SLUMS is designed to provide feedback to the NFMS to generate better information on land resources and other relevant sources such as the Forest Resource Information Management System (FRIMS) and the National Forest Inventory (NFI).

Recently, PNG has established a Deforestation Alert and Monitoring System to provide near-real-time information on deforestation to government authorities and organizations involved in agriculture. The system is designed to be accessible to the public, enhancing the transparency of PNG’s REDD+ implementation and reporting on climate change mitigation actions.

PNG has also developed a set of Performance-Based Incentives and Voluntary Emission Reduction Measures (PaIvE) related to climate change mitigation actions. Some potential applications of the PNG New Forest Policy include:

- Monitoring REDD+ activities: CCDA can use alerts to monitor REDD+ activities if persistence is maintained and reversals are not occurring.
- Prioritizing enforcement: CEPAs can use alerts to support “perimeter defence” and prioritize resources to investigate breaches.
- Logging concession monitoring: PNG has an alert system to quickly identify the activities that violate the PNG Forestry Act, including illegal logging, unauthorized forest logging without a permit and the Logging Code of Practice (LCoP), understand the forest area being exploited and send alerts with local officers.
- Management of agricultural and other productive concessions: DILs and Agricultural companies with “zero deforestation” commitments can use alerts to flag areas where suppliers may not comply with company environmental policies.
- Interdiction of illegal logging: The enforcement agencies can use alerts to prioritize field investigations and implement national zoning and forest protection policies.
- Raising awareness: Non-profits and other advocacy groups can use alert systems to sound alarms around illegal or unsustainable clearing.

Complementing the identification and prevention system, PNG has in place a set of regulations to address problematic activities:

The Forestry Policy provides background considerations concerning forest conversion within the context of Forest Management Agreements (FMAs) and outside of them. Article M.1 of the National Forest Policy states that all landowners are required to comply with the provisions of the national forestry legislation and some degree of control over land use conversion in both public and private forests.

Mitigation activities within forestry legislation relate to provisions that deter any interference within naturally forested areas such as the 10% set aside for conservation areas within FMAs or other legally protected areas.

The Environmental Regulation 2002 outlines environmental controls, e.g., Environmental Impact Assessments for large-scale forest activities in both private and public forests. The controls are required for large-scale forest conversion, road clearance, or agriculture activities.

**Under Conservation Areas**, which fall under the Conservation Areas Act, natural forest conversion is also currently permitted. Currently, areas subject to Forest Clearance Authority (PCA), outline clear procedures to reduce the development of agriculture.

**Actions to reduce displacement of emissions:**

The NRS is a comprehensive initiative aimed at achieving results on a national scale. Emission reductions are achieved through the implementation of policies that address deforestation, forest degradation, land use and development planning, food safety and family agriculture production, sustainable consumption and production patterns, and climate change adaptation.

The NRS consists of a comprehensive set of policies and measures that address the causes of deforestation at a national level, which significantly lowers the risk of displacement. The NRS also promotes the development of forest management plans in accordance with the Forest Act and other relevant legislation, enhances forest protection policies and enhances the legal framework to create positive incentives for halting deforestation and the further expansion of forests. The NRS aims to reduce illegal logging, combat forest fires, prevent disturbances and to implement effective law enforcement measures to combat and eliminate illegal forest activities in all protected areas and other critical forest areas throughout the region.

For example:

- **The forestry and conservation legislation includes substantial penalties for violations of these provisions** and specific penalties for breaches of various forest management practices.
- The Environment Act of 2000 and its subsequent regulations, the Conservation Areas Act and the Land Use Planning Act contain measures to address illegal forest activities, such as timber fellings in protected areas.

The PNGFA is promoting initiatives such as voluntary legality, chain of custody verification schemes, Forest Management Plans, and the REDD+ Web Portal. The PNGFA also promotes the full implementation of sustainable deforestation free markets and economies.

In addition, the "REDD+ Web Portal," developed through the collaboration of the government and key stakeholders, provides a comprehensive overview of the situation in Papua New Guinea and the conditions for managing land use planning and monitoring forest destruction.

Finally, it is important to emphasize that the NRS is a nationwide program, significantly minimizing the risk of emissions shifting from deforestation to forest degradation under REDD+ activities in the FRLRBL and the LULUCF sector GHG inventory ensures comprehensive coverage. # REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 29 OF 9)

PNG's national REDD+ process began in 2008 when the country joined the CRFB, gaining access to additional funding through the UN-REDD Program and the FCOP as part of the REDD+ Readiness phase. During this phase, various PMMs were developed, forming the foundation for key REED+ elements, including the National Forest Monitoring System, the FRELFS, the REDD+ Strategy, and the Safeguard Information System.

The groundwork required to help establish the important safeguard systems for PNG was funded through these programs. The development of the National Environmental Management Act (NEMA), the Strategic Investment Scheme (SIS), the REDD+ Governance Mechanism, and other key forest PMMs such as the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy, the Forest Policy, the REDD+ Strategy, NEMAS, SIS, FRELFS, etc., in 2014-15 and the establishment of various REDD+ Technical Working Groups and sub-groups which managed the development of the REDD+ Strategy, NEMAS, SIS, FRELFS, etc. (e.g., the REDD+ Strategy, SIS development etc.).

These important PMMs and/or committees/groups were created to help provide an avenue for stakeholder involvement input into the process of REDD+ development in PNG. Most of the members consisted of representatives from government agencies such as the Department of Lands and Environment, Department of Forests and Environment as well as DPM, private sector (e.g., horticulture, timber, palm oil), civil society organizations, indigenous communities, and key forest ecology associations such as NICS, TNC, CECLOR and other environmental organizations closely affiliated with customary landowners and communities from the key project sites.

These processes, which were initiated in 2014-15 and completed during the results period of this proposal, therefore, are considered as an adequate timeframe for safeguard-related considerations. The initiatives include:

* A review of existing laws, policies, and regulations towards the development of Social and Environmental Safeguards for REDD+ in PNG.
* Development of a National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) with the full and effective participation of key stakeholders developed in the provinces of East New Britain, West New Britain, and Madang.
* Establishment of a National REDD+ Monitoring System (NEMS) with the full and effective participation of key stakeholders developed in the provinces of East New Britain, West New Britain, and Madang.
* Development of a National Forest Management Strategy (NFMS) with the full and effective participation of key stakeholders developed in the provinces of East New Britain, West New Britain, and Madang.

Past stakeholder engagement processes drawn from pioneer REDD+ projects, such as the Apel-Salumri project in 2008-13, have demonstrated that a participatory and community-led approach is effective for REDD+ initiatives going as far back as 2008, using a participatory and community-led approaches. The pioneer Apel-Salumri project has been used to inform the development of the current REDD+ Strategy, NEMAS, and feedback gathered during specific stakeholder consultation processes to develop procedures for stakeholder engagement within the majority of change projects currently in the context of REDD+.

Of note is the fact that the REDD+ Strategy was developed with the full and effective participation of key stakeholders developed in the provinces of East New Britain, West New Britain, and Madang. The key stakeholders included: (1) private sector participants (see table below) from five stakeholder groups: 1) business/representatives responsible for land use change; 2) civil society organizations; 3) indigenous communities (e.g., customary landowners); 4) sub-national government staff (especially those in climate change committees and lower levels of government); and 5) customary landowners and communities members

As a result of stakeholder engagement and consultative efforts undertaken over the REDD+ readiness mission in PNG, a number of key areas where action was needed to adequately address and reflect key REDD+ RAMSAR elements were identified:

* Improvements in the laws on forestry.
* Changing existing government administrative arrangements.
* Developing new policies.
* Benefit sharing and rights to carbon.
* Access and participation of communities in decision-making.
* Devolved governance and land-use planning.
* Water management and protection, particularly in existing laws.
* Conflicts between national laws and sector policies.
* Grievance and redress mechanisms.

## Key Stakeholder Consultations

Stakeholder engagement has been a crucial part of all REDD+ projects since the inception of the REDD+ initiatives and especially as part of the UN-REDD Programme from 2011–2015. Stakeholders were engaged in consultations to identify key drivers of deforestation and forest degradation, through the identification of key drivers of deforestation and forest degradation held as part of the consultations on the National Strategy for Finance and Investment (Plan PNG) which ultimately formed the basis for the National REDD+ Strategy.

These consultations involved a wide range of representations including customary landowners and communities, government agencies, private sector actors (including universities), non-governmental organizations, farming cooperatives and local producers, larger corporations and international/foreign entities and individual investors. The consultations were guided by the general criteria identified by the CCDA (and across most relevant government authorities):

* Their mandate and function as related to REDD+ e.g., PNGFA as the entity responsible for monitoring forest cover change.
* The extent to which their oversight and involvement is required in REDD+ governance, e.g. the role of the National Forest Management Authority (NFMA) in providing technical advice and coordination of external development assistance in PNG.
* Their relationship with indigenous peoples and other Local Peoples and Local Communities, as per the Cancun Safeguards.
* Other relevant stakeholders, e.g., areas identified by GoPNG to trial REDD+ activities.
* Principles of good governance, transparency and accountability that require engagement with all stakeholders.
* Private sector industries that would be impacted by REDD+, actions, such as oil palm and logging companies.
* The necessary political oversight provided by the relevant sector or ministerial head.
* Agencies responsible for providing technical advice to government on issues related to the CEDAW (e.g., Office for the Development of Women, National Council of Women etc.)

### Key Milestones and Activities

- **2012**: UNFCCC Submissions work commenced under the UNREDD Programme.
- **2013**: National Policy on Practice Roadmap was completed for the Development and Implementation of National REDD+ Strategy and Action Plan (NRSAP).
- **2014**: First draft of principles, criteria and indicators (PCI) of the Carbon safeguards published.
- **2015**: UNFCCC Submissions work commenced under the UNREDD Programme (2013–2015), continuation from the UNREDD which adopted a Country Action Plan for REDD+ in PNG.
- **2016**: Draft Field Test reports were produced (District Level Highlights & Manus FIs, Field Test Reports).

- **2018**: REDD+ Safeguard indicators prepared.
- **2019**: Adoption of the Carbon safeguards completed.
- **2020**: Draft Social and Environmental Management Framework for BIP completed.

### Figure 2: Most relevant REDD+ stakeholder engagement activities carried out in PNG

Customary landowners and communities, and the development of the FPC Guidelines

Community involvement and participation are critical to the REDD+ development as they are the most crucial stakeholder group, holding ownership over 97% of the country's land. Many of the PAMs (Protected Areas Management) areas are customary lands, and many of these communities have their own social and environmental safeguards principle – while implementing REDD+ activities must develop policies and measures that safeguard the rights of forest-dependent people but also the rights of forest dependent people under the Social and Environmental Safeguards (SES).

An example is the development of the National Guidelines on FPC, which was initiated in 2011. In May 2013, UNDP/UNREDD PNG conducted a series of workshops with communities living within the Manus PAMs (and areas predominantly owned by customary landholders). The FPC workshops commenced in March 2014 and continued until November 2015. Training Workshops on November 2015 had in collaboration with the Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS).

The initial site selected to pilot the application of the FPC Guidelines in Manus was Tali, which was chosen because it is a small village that has been working closely with the Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS) along the north coast which is part of twenty (20) villages and eighty nine (89) wards where the Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS) had worked over the last three years. The core objectives of the field testing (consultations) were:


---
**Page 35**
---

REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 32 OF 40**

1) To test if the Guidelines and inform the Tuku I Villages of the importance of FPC and support to identify when FPC is required, at what level it shall be applied; who seeks consent; who gives consent; and how outcomes will be documented;
2) Provide lessons learned on the implementation of consultative and participatory processes where FPC is required for REDD+ programs and activities.

The field testing was attended by 28 participants with at least a third of those in attendance representing vulnerable groups. The following were observed:

* **WCS highlighted** the following challenges while implementing REDD+ demonstrations and supporting the Ward Plan in Tuku I:
    * Concerns from the Tuku I leaders
    * Lack of capacity by Tuku I
    * Lack of clarity on the role of the Village Plan development team
    * Lack of funding support as Ward Plans were not budgeted for
    * FPC meetings are seen as a waste of time and are seen to be competing with traditional leadership where the Council of Chief decides for the Wards
    * Roles and responsibilities are unclear, especially when the support is withdrawn
    * Who takes ownership of the FPC process when the support is not there? Is it the ward council or the Council of Chiefs?
    * Language must be simple but universally acceptable so that the technical terms does not lose its meaning.
    * Attendance and quality of attendance

As a result of this field testing and the key lessons learned, a revised version of the National Guidelines on FPC was developed in early 2014 and circulated through the SES TWG and FPC experts in the country. It was also distributed to all relevant stakeholders including the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Wildlife, regional Safeguarding and FPC workshops were conducted shortly after from May to June 2014, which had over 150 participants.

Methods of gathering information from key stakeholders included: formal and informal interviews, both with individuals and in small group; awareness sessions in a community forum setting; and smaller focus groups. The focus groups were separated by age (under 30 years old and above 30 years old) but not by gender, but there were few instances where the focus groups were separated according to age.

Several issues and challenges raised by stakeholders during the FPC stakeholder consultation and participation process included:

* **FPC processes applied** by projects should consider the various systems of village and community governance in provinces such as East New Britain, Manus Island, Milne Bay and some parts of Bougainville where the final decision rests with the head women.
* Importance of field testing the FPC Guidelines to determine its practicality and acceptability by both customary and formal institutions.
* The April Salamau project was also pivotal in developing the existing FPC Guidelines for CCDA. This process included thorough stakeholder review and feedback from more than 20 provincial, national and international experts. A two-day workshop was held at the PNG Forestry Department in Port Moresby meeting as part of discussions relating to the development of the REDD+ initiative and subsequent PLRN.
* Building on these local trainings, field tests and consultations in REDD pilot sites, the UN-REDD and the FCPF were able to develop a set of guidelines for FPC. The capacity building activities and awareness raising were incrementally built through a combination of awareness raising and consultation workshops outlined below:
    * Regional workshops on REDD+, awareness, FPC and safeguards were held in
        1. Kokopo, East New Britain in April 2014 with 58 participants
        2. Alotau, Milne Bay Province in May 2014 with 18 participants

As a result of this process, PNG published its National REDD+ FPIC Guidelines (https://panda.panda.org/wp-content/uploads/2014/03/PNG_Facilitated_National_REDD-FPIC_Guidelines_Clear_290314.pdf), which will serve as the basis for all future REDD+ projects in PNG. The guidelines are aligned with the existing agreement on Conservation Agreements to be used for the delivery of non-monetary benefits when revocating CCF projects.

### Use of Proceeds/Benefit Sharing

The use of proceeds has always been a key contentious issue linked at the community level and especially with sustainable development, particularly in rural communities due to the existing system of compensation payments levies enforced by the Constitution (§ 53 - unjust deprivation of property rights) and all relevant legislation on valuation and land. The current system is not clear as to who should be compensated for these payments/levies to be provided from REDD+ projects. The confusion generated over voluntary REDD+ projects has been compounded with the lack of clarity in the Benefit Sharing Distribution System (BSDS) most used in most consultations since there was an ongoing issue on quantifying carbon or monetization.

The final report for the Benefit Sharing Distribution System (BSDS) highlighted these concerns and suggested further work to be done. The current BSDS is not appropriate for REDD+ projects, and it is recommended that a more appropriate BSDS framework be developed. To date this framework has yet to be agreed and developed, and as a result, no benefit sharing will take place during the implementation phase of the project (see Component 1). No GCF proceeds will be distributed through this system.

Through stakeholder engagement processes and community-level consultations, important issues were identified that need further work. These include: the development of a National REDD+ Strategy; identification of key PAICs, including the National REDD+ Strategy and the Subseguents Information System (SIS) for PNG; and the establishment of an effective mechanism to address land tenure and compensation concerns. Please refer to the ESSA Report, annexed to this FP for a more detailed overview.

The proceeds will be strategically invested in activities outlined in the NRIS to foster a sustainable and resilient REDD+ Strategy. The focus is on the interdependence of the three REDD+ phases: Readiness, Implementation, and Results-Based Payments.

PNG will be able to access proceeds from RBP programs in a continuous cycle by:
- Fulfilling and enhancing crucial readiness elements such as PRELFLR, NIFS, and SIS in accordance with the NRIS.
- Advancing the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, increasing its ambition and impact based on lessons learned.

Through this proactive cycle, PNG can continuously improve its carbon accounting and safeguard measures and monitoring and reporting systems, positioning itself to achieve increasingly ambitious environmental goals and impacts.

### Figure 3. Outline of the REDD+ virtuous cycle

The project activities will address the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in PNG, aiming to reduce emissions in the short term while laying the foundation for sustainable development in the country's land use and forestry sector. This objective aligns with the NIFS, the country’s Vision 2050 and the National StaRS.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## PNG National REDD+ Strategy Vision:

"In to catalyse transformational change within the forest and land use sector towards a new responsible economy with lower GHG emissions, stronger long-term economic growth and community livelihoods, and the effective conservation of biodiversity and ecosystem services while ensuring that Papua New Guinea (PNG) is able to meet its international climate commitments for future generations."

## National Strategy for Responsible Sustainable Development for PNG (NRSF) calls for:

- A paradigm shift in sectors such as inclusive, green or 'lower emissions growth path', using clean and sustainable energy and resources generating low or zero carbon emissions, with the aim of strengthening PNG's strategic positioning and economic competitiveness in the world, while contributing to global climate change mitigation goals now and into the future.

### Drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in PNG:

Deforestation between 2000 and 2015: efficient 261.538 hectares in PNG, resulting in average annual emissions of over 5 MBCO2e. The main cause of deforestation has been the conversion of forestland to cropland, which accounted for approximately 74% of total deforestation between 2000 and 2015. Other drivers include forest fires, deforested land, while commercial agricultural developments, particularly oil palm plantations, account for 39% of total deforestation.

The impact of commercial agriculture has significantly increased since 2000 due to the rapid expansion of SABLs. Only a small portion of the 5 million hectares have initiated activities, and there has been an increase in land conversion from forestland to cropland. The rate of deforestation is expected to continue at this pace.

There are also significant trends towards population growth, which has an annual growth rate of 1.1% and is likely to continue in the coming years. The demand for agricultural products driven by population growth will be the main driver of forest degradation over the next decade.

Between 2000 and 2015, forest degradation affected approximately 2.5 million hectares, leading to average annual emissions of around 25 MBCO2e. A significant 98.1% of these emissions were attributed to commercial agricultural activities.

This trend is expected to persist, primarily due to the area of forests currently under actual or potential conversion for agriculture and the expansion of SABLs in PNG. Conversely, limited forest clearing to prepare land for future agricultural development in SABL areas has significantly contributed to forest degradation.

Despite the strategic goals outlined in Vision 2050, efforts to improve forest cover through reforestation and forest rehabilitation activities have been limited. The plan, as mentioned in the NRSF, aims to establish 600,000 hectares of new forests by 2030. As of June 2018, PNGAFG estimates that approximately 60,000 hectares have been reforested so far, with a target of an additional 20,000 hectares to be achieved by 2030.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 37 of 9)

### Figure 4: Drivers of deforestation and forest degradation impact in PNG. Source: PNG National REDD+ Strategy

The underlying drivers identified are linked to the development process in PNG, which is sometimes implemented ineffectively and inefficiently, leading to significant environmental degradation while failing to deliver the expected economic benefits.

A lack of coordination to implement forest and land use planning: PNG faces significant challenges in aligning forest and land use policies with broader national development objectives. The following issues exacerbate this challenge:

- Fragmented Policies and Regulations: The forestry sector operates under laws and regulations that are often fragmented, outdated, or poorly enforced.
- Land Tenure Conflicts: The Lease-Leaseback provision has been a significant driver of deforestation and land alienation. Between 2013 and 2015, approximately 47% of the total land area was leased out under SAILs, often without proper consent from customary landowners.
- Lack of Training for Local Personnel: Provincial and district-level forestry officers often lack time to train in interpreting and applying complex land-use policies. For instance, limited technical capacity has led to poor implementation of forest management guidelines, including logging or agricultural conversion projects leading to unsustainable practices.

Weak enforcement and implementation capacities of forest authorities. The enforcement mechanisms and incentives for compliance with forest laws are weak, and the penalties for violations are often underdeveloped. Specific contributing factors include:

- Lack of Resources: Many forest conservation and sustainable management plans lack specificity and enforceability. For instance, while the Forest Act of 1991 includes provisions for community-based conservation and sustainable land use practices, there is a lack of community-led conservation or mechanisms to counter illegal logging effectively.
- Land Grab, using, or rather misusing, the Lease-Leaseback provision under the Land Act, resulting in deforestation. Between 2013 and 2015, approximately 47% of the total land area was leased out under SAILs, often without proper consent from customary landowners.
- Absence of effective support for rural development targeting customary landowners and communities: Rural development efforts in PNG often fail to meet the needs of customary landholders.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 38 OF 59

### Communities, exacerbating socio-economic disparities and environmental degradation. Specific challenges include:

- Weak development of a forest economy and not enough alternative livelihoods.
- Many villages and communities rely on subsistence agriculture and hunting for their livelihoods with little support for sustainable forest-based enterprises.
- Several communities often sell timber to intermediaries at low prices, missing out on opportunities for value-added processing or market access for sustainably harvested forest products.

### Ineffective PFC Processes:
- ERFs to identify landholding groups and secure PFC are often not transparently conducted. Communities are sometimes excluded or misled from consultations processes, resulting in disputes and long-term grievances.
- Limited capacity of communities to engage with the REDD+ process.
- Lack of awareness among communities regarding their rights under the Forest Act and other laws.
- Some communities lack awareness of their legal rights regarding land and resource ownership. Some communities believe that they have no say or influence over decisions made by government officials involved in decision-making processes, leaving them unable to contest exploitative agreements.

### Policy Constraints:
- Customary landowners and communities often lack the skills to negotiate equitable agreements with private sector companies.
- Limited capacity of communities to engage with the REDD+ process.
- Some communities struggle to organize themselves into effective groups to advocate for their interests in relation to forest conservation, sustainable development, and extractive industries such as logging and mining companies.

These underlying factors undermine PNG’s ability to achieve sustainable forest management and rural development, posing significant challenges to conservation, economic growth, and community well-being.

## Project Objectives

The objectives and outputs have been designed based on the results of the REDD+ Finance and Investment Prospectus™ and the National REDD+ Strategy.

### The project is designed to tackle a critical issue: deforestation and land degradation in PNG are threatening biodiversity, reducing ecosystem services such as clean water and carbon sequestration, and endangering the livelihoods of customary landowners and communities.

The proposed project will contribute significantly towards the achievement of the National REDD+ Strategy for 2017-2027. This implementation will focus on key action areas, including strengthening forest law enforcement, forest protection, and the promotion of inclusive sustainable livelihoods for communities.

In the long term, the expected impact is to reduce emissions from land use, deforestation, and forest degradation; improve the livelihoods of customary landowners and communities; and promote forest conservation.

### Assumptions

To successfully achieve the objectives of the project, several key assumptions must be considered based on the current circumstances:

1. With a particular emphasis on ensuring the meaningful inclusion of women and other vulnerable and marginalized groups in decision-making processes.
2. Strong support will be provided during the implementation phase by both National and Provincial authorities.

---

**Note:** The CCDA, supported by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) and UNDP, invested significant effort into developing this proposal. The REDD+ Finance and Investment Prospectus™ was developed in close collaboration with communities and studies informed the RBP. Given the limited stakeholder consultation planned for the RBP funding proposal due to time constraints, a detailed stakeholder engagement plan will be developed as part of the RFP, followed by an extensive stakeholder review and endorsement.

1. The key markets will be accurately identified, allowing for targeted strategies that meet the specific needs of each market segment.
2. The locations selected for implementing project activities will be accessible, enabling participation and employment opportunities for all parties, with a focus on capacitating and empowering women and those vulnerable and marginalized ones. Capacity-building initiatives will be effectively designed to ensure that they are inclusive and gender-responsive, leading to long-term social impact.
3. Customary landowners and communities will be actively involved in the project, fostering a sense of ownership and community-driven development.
4. The project will establish appropriate mechanisms to enable improved land management practices of customary landowners and communities.
5. The legal and governance framework will comply with the legislation and regulations, with particular attention given to gender equality and inclusion.
6. The customary landowners and communities actively encourage and support young people and women's participation, helping to boost inclusivity and diversity within all project activities.
7. Conflicts between customary landowners and communities and project developers will not escalate.

Based on the assumptions, the project will address the main direct and underlying drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in the region.

The GCF RBP proceeds will be used in complementary and coherence with the most relevant climate finance received by PHIL, such as the PULU Forest County Package (compiled primarily by the EU FCLC3 Project), launched at COP26.


Project Structure

The project is structured into three Components and five Outputs, including a 2-year inception phase. The project's overall components are based on the targeted measures: Component 0 – Inception phase – focuses on intensive stakeholder engagement in order to develop a detailed design of the project activities and establish an effective governance structure for the project. This will ensure that all relevant stakeholders enable conditions to enhance the capacities and coordination of National and Subnational Government entities, civil society organizations, and other key actors in the region. The project will also support the development of enabling policies and institutional arrangements that support the economic productivity and inclusive social development of customary landowners and communities.

The project's outputs are designed to be mutually supportive. Effective coordination among different areas is essential for ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are engaged throughout the project, including the inclusion of all relevant stakeholders when developing, reviewing, and implementing activities, with particular focus on women and youth.


Component 0: Project Inception

The inception Phase sets the foundation for a successful project by ensuring that key strategic, operational, and communication elements are in place. This phase emphasizes gender inclusion, environmental and social safeguards, stakeholder engagement, and capacity building.

A crucial part of this phase is updating and implementing the Gender Action Plan (GAP) to ensure gender equity and address gender-based inequalities. The updated GAP will be based on a participatory process with all relevant stakeholders leading to an updated GAP that reflects local realities, national policies, and global best practices. This plan will guide the project in ensuring that women's rights are respected and protected, and that women groups actively participate in decision-making and benefit equitably from project outcomes. To support this,

Main objectives of the EU FCLC3 are 1) Effective implementation of evidence-based FCB3 policies, governance and institutional arrangements to ensure effective delivery of REDD+ activities; 2) Strengthening the capacity of national governments to deliver inclusive and gender-responsive green public and private sector investments and information-freeflow mechanisms; 3) Facilitating access to finance for projects that promote open, inclusive, climate change and biodiversity eq.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 4 OF 10)

Training sessions will be conducted for project staff and local stakeholders to build capacity in gender-responsive programming, and mechanisms will be put in place to collect gender-disaggregated data.

Simultaneously, the project will develop and implement the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), any necessary amendments to the Project Design Document (PDD), and a Stakeholder Engagement Action Plan. This will involve conducting an in-depth environmental and social risk assessment to ensure alignment with national regulations and policies, including the Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP) and the Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Voluntary Partnership Agreement (VPA). The ESMP will include mitigation measures, monitoring mechanisms, and compliance structures to prevent or minimize potential negative impacts on indigenous peoples. It will also incorporate elements required for Indigenous Peoples plans, as per the GCF’s Indigenous Peoples Policy and FAD ESS 8. The ESMP will be developed in accordance with the Community Forest Management Framework (COFFMF), which will include detailed provisions and procedures to guide all on-the-ground activities. This framework will ensure that indigenous peoples are involved at every stage of project design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting.

Engaging stakeholders effectively is fundamental to project success, as it helps the Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) to identify key actors, their interests, and their influence on the project. The SEP will outline indirect engagement strategies for engaging with national governments, provincial/territorial landowners, communities, government institutions, the private sector, and civil society.

To ensure a clear roadmap for achieving project goals, the Theory of Change (ToC) will be updated. This process will involve identifying key assumptions, indicators, and expected outcomes based on stakeholder insights. The updated ToC will provide a more precise understanding of how the project is expected to achieve its intended results through various initiatives.

Key risks and external influences will be reassessed to strengthen the project's strategic direction. As a result, the ToC will be integrated into the project’s planning framework and shared with all stakeholders.

A robust Monitoring and Measurement System will be implemented to track progress, evaluate impact, and facilitate learning. This system will cover social, environmental, and economic dimensions, ensuring they align with the revised ToC. A baseline study will be conducted for each dimension, and project counterparts will receive training on monitoring methodologies to ensure data quality and consistency. Periodic reviews of the monitoring framework will be carried out to adapt it as needed in response to emerging challenges and opportunities.

By the end of this component, the project will have a fully updated Gender Action Plan, an approved Environmental and Social Management Plan, an operational Stakeholder Engagement Plan, a refined Theory of Change, and a Monitoring and Measurement System. These components together will ensure the project moves forward with clarity, accountability, and a strong commitment to sustainability and inclusiveness.

This component comprises the following set of activities:

- **Activity I:** Update and implement the Project’s Gender Action Plan
- **Activity II:** Develop and implement the Environmental and Social Management Plan, and complementary ESF ESS 8
- **Activity III:** Develop and implement a Stakeholder Engagement Action Plan
- **Activity IV:** Implement a monitoring and measurement system

Component 1: Increased institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial governments in project design and implementation.

The component will focus on a set of enabling actions aimed at the development, enforcement, and application of policies and regulations related to forest management, land use planning, biodiversity conservation, wildlife management, forest land-use policies, and the REDD+ coordination and reporting. # REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) | PAGE 41 OF 59

## Institutional capacity factors stakeholder collaboration, enhancing their ability to manage forests and respond to climate risks.

To achieve expected results and ensure the long-term impact of the project, comprehensive actions across government levels and sectors are essential. These actions must be integrated with other initiatives from the national level to target provinces, districts, localities, and wards. These actions must ensure that the country’s development is in line with international principles of responsible and sustainable development, ultimately delivering the maximum long-term benefits.

The component is comprised of the following outputs:

- **Output 1. Strengthen National Sustainable Land Use Planning (NSLUP) framework for the national and provincial governments.**

This output is aligned with the Action Area “Strengthened land use and development planning PAMM” described in the NRS Action Area.

The challenges of integrated planning are evident between different levels of government, with significant gaps in the bottom-up and top-down planning processes. Limited capacity and resources at the local and ward level often result in fragmented and inconsistent policies and plans. There is also a lack of coordination among stakeholders, as well as a lack of connectivity among ward, local, district, and provincial plans. Many districts and provinces struggle to integrate their land use plans with other sectoral plans such as water resource provision and, in many cases, a failure to deliver clear benefits to communities from the planning process.

The output will contribute to:

- **Activity I. Integrate and coordinate existing governance systems.**
- **Activity II. Update forest policy to consider amendments and incorporate legal standards.**
- **Activity III. Develop and implement land use plans at national level.**
- **Activity IV. Develop or update spatially explicit subnational development plans.**

Activities will be conducted in close collaboration with the Department of Lands and Physical Planning (DLPP), in partnership with other relevant government agencies, including but not limited to those listed as the PRG’s DACs: Department of Treasury and Department of Finance, Local Level Government Institutions, and with support from the World Bank and other donors. The PRG will also work closely with its FPCs for this Activity.

Activities carried out under this output will be aligned and/or based on the achievements from the EU FCBB Project to avoid duplication of efforts and overlapping. The EU FCBB Project activities related to output 1 are expected to contribute to the following outputs: “Strengthened environmental management, protection, and enforcement” at forest management (at provincial and district level) and “the training of government officials on forestry, climate change, environment, and natural resource management.”

- **Output 2. Enhance and strengthen environmental management, protection, and land use regulations for forest authorities and local stakeholders.**

Focuses on the following activities that are already included in the NRS Action Area: “Strengthened environmental management, protection, and enforcement.”

PNG has a significant environmental management legislation with guidance on environmental protection and restoration of forests. However, there is limited capacity at all levels to effectively implement and enforcement of these acts, including gaps within their regulations, their linkages across sectors, agencies, and the systems and coordination mechanisms for implementation. The PRG will work closely with relevant government agencies to strengthen existing efforts across key sectors, including climate change, environment, forestry, agriculture, mining and natural resource management, disaster risk reduction, and urban planning.

This output comprises the following sets of activities:

- **Activity I. Application of environmental and social safeguards at the national and subnational levels.**

Activities will be conducted in close collaboration with the PNGFA, in charge of the Forest (Amendment) Act 2019 application, including the monitoring and assessment of existing and planned concessions, and to ensure that concession areas are not included in REDD+ activities. The PNGFA is also responsible for ensuring that control development activities and the implementation of the CEPRA, MRA, and other agreements are conducted in accordance with PNG’s laws.

The activities will help establish an efficient and transparent system for environmental management, and contribute to the effective implementation of the Forest (Amendment) Act 2019. The activities will also ensure that communities have access to information land use and resource decision-making by both communities and the private sector.

The activities to be carried out will be linked to the EU FCB Project to ensure efficiency and effectiveness of the project, and to ensure that the implementation of the REDD+ activities is in line with the national strategy on sustainable land management and forest management at provincial and district level. 

### Output 1: Improve coordination and reporting on the implementation of the NRS, NIDG and GHG for the CCDA.

Based on the Component 2 of the NRS “REDD+ Coordination and Reporting”, this output is focused on fulfilling the requirements of the REDD+ project in PNG by improving the coordination between the different actors involved through a stepwise approach. These improvements, together with the advance of the implementation actions, will allow for better reporting to the CCDA and other stakeholders.

Beyond the improvements of the readiness elements, further elements aiming to increase the impact of the actions implemented and to monitor the efficacy of the measures will be developed, especially the development of a baseline for carbon stock in forested areas. This will help improve the national capacity to culture to help provide a clear portal for how land is being allocated and used in PNG, and the final implementation of a national REDD+ strategy.

This output will rely on the achievements and activities carried out under the EU FCB Project and UN-REDD Programme, including the development of the NRS, NIDG, GHG, and the establishment of a baseline for monitoring systems. The results from the NRS will be crucial to improving national information on carbon emissions and removals from the forestry sector, including REDD+, and to gather better data related to the biodiversity.

It is expected that, during the inception phase of the CIF RBP programme, the specific elements to be developed in this output will be integrated into the baseline for monitoring systems. This will be done together with the EU FCB project and UN-REDD technical assistance. The necessary tools to monitor the climate impact of the REDD+ activities will also be developed during the inception phase of CIF RBP, and these elements will be integrated into the monitoring systems.

In summary, the activities to be conducted under Output 1.3 are the following:

* Activity I: Implement forest management and enforcement programs.
* Activity III: Application of PNG’s Timber Legality.
* Activity IV: Develop a National Land Use Information System linked with those for PNGFA, CEPRA, MRA, and agriculture to help provide a clear picture on how land is utilized.

### Component 2: Strengthen the capacity of customary landowners and sustainable livelihoods of customary landowners and communities in the targeted provinces.

This component, focused on direct actions to be implemented in the land, will intend to secure the primary role of communities in decision-making by ensuring that the aspirations of their leaders and managers of many PNG’s most important asset strategies. 

Customary landowners are the custodians of the land and have the most detailed long-term perspective on how it can be managed. Too often these groups are excluded from the development decision-making process, and this output will help bring these groups out of the development decision-making process. For these groups to play a more active role they must be provided with both the information and tools to engage in and, if necessary, challenge

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) PAGE 43 OF 51

The development process to ensure that their long-term interests are protected and that those resources that communities have relied upon remain for future generations:

PNG has a rapidly growing and predominantly rural population that will create an increasing demand for land for farming, settlements, and infrastructure. This will put pressure on forests and other natural resources, limiting opportunities to access sustainable economic activities that do not negatively impact the integrity and value of their ecosystems.

Component 2 will support customary landowners and communities, particularly those vulnerable and marginalized, as well as ecosystems to adapt to climate change. By conserving and restoring forests, wetlands, and other ecosystems, we can reduce greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, protect water resources, and provide clean energy, thereby mitigating human diseases, food insecurity, and poverty. It will also support the transition of customary landowners and communities away from traditional extractive forestry to sustainable forest management practices that are resilient to climate change impacts. Additionally, the component fosters the transfer of traditional knowledge and innovative technologies, supporting sustainable land-use practices and climate-resilient livelihoods.

Component 2 builds on the achievements of Component 1 and complements the work of the FCBCC program. The synergies between the two projects, particularly in the provinces where both are being implemented concurrently, will enhance their impact by combining the strengths of each project. This component also leverages the lessons learned from foundations established in other regions that are not overlapping between the two projects but can offer valuable insights.

• **Output 2.1 Strengthen Forest landscapes management technical and financial capacities for customary landowners and communities.**

This output will be achieved through the following components: *Strengthening Management and Enforcement* and *Strengthening Environmental Management, Enforcement and Protection* from NRS Action Areas 3 and 2 respectively, which are aligned with the outputs of Component 1.

The focus on customary landowners is critical because they hold a significant amount of land in PNG. Their involvement will be important under conservation agreements. The focus will be on community-based conservation areas and other forms of tenure that recognize customary rights to land and resources. This output will contribute to helping to conserve global biodiversity and local environmental services while also strengthening the rights of customary landowners and communities, which are often marginalized in forest management.

The output is fully consistent with relevant safeguards and directly contributes to the implementation of Cancun Agreements (see section 5) and the achievement of REDD+ goals. It will also contribute to the delivery of social and environmental co-benefits (please see section 6 and ESM for further detail).

Customary landowners and communities will be able to receive capacity building and technical assistance to support their participation in conservation agreements, including Community Forest Management Agreements (CFMAs) or Conservation Agreements (CAs) for ecosystem services. Beyond the investment period, the experience gained and the capacity built by customary landowners and communities can be used to continue to benefit from ecosystem services, as they support the maintenance and generation of environmental services.

The process of developing CFMs will involve engaging with customary landowners and communities through dialogue and negotiation. This will ensure that the agreements are based on mutual consent and reflect ecosystems that provide critical environmental services. Based on this process, voluntary commitments will be developed by customary landowners and communities to conserve forest landscapes in exchange for ecosystem services, as they support the maintenance and generation of environmental services.

The choice of the conservation area will be guided by criteria established under FPA and a transparent selection process. A competitive mechanism will be developed during the inception phase of the project.

The Conservation Agreements will be established with customary landowners and communities registered or as part of recognized indigenous peoples' organizations, provided that they are not subject to any restrictions on their rights to use forest resources. These agreements are governed by relevant FPA policy and regulation; the signing parties must be legally recognized as non-commercial legal entities under PNG law. In addition, where customary landowners and communities are not yet legally established non-commercial local organizations may be designated to operationalize the Conservation Agreements. The implementation of the Conservation Agreements, or additional in-kind support under these agreements, will ultimately depend on the capacity of the parties involved.

To support long-term sustainability beyond the project's use of process, lessons learned from this agreement will strengthen the development and implementation of conservation agreements for customary landowners and communities under Output 1.2 to enhance and strengthen environmental management, protection, and land-use regulations, supporting a landscape approach for forest landscapes in PNG. In addition, the output will address the transition to a sustainable forestry sector with higher levels of market access and

To address this, the project will implement a pilot program in PNG to support the development of a Forest Management Plan for the Endorsement of Forest Certification (PEFC) and the Forest Stewardship Council (FSC). This initiative will also provide technical assistance to communities and customary landowners.

#### Activities to be carried out within this activity include:

- **Activity I. Identification of priority livelihood activities:** Identify and prioritize livelihood activities, including training for the LG.
- **Activity II. Capacity building and support to customary landowners and communities on their production systems/alternative livelihoods:**
  - Implementing activities related to timber legality, tree planting, reforestation, and enhanced approaches for forest management and conservation into sustainable livelihoods while promoting greater engagement with respect to the rights of customary landowners and communities.

#### Activities will be also linked to Output 2.2, providing capacity building support to the development of sustainable agriculture in PNG. The output will be developed in alignment with the activities carried out under the EU FCUP project, which considers the access to timber finance and forest conservation finance into their key exploration.

### Output 2.2 Enhance sustainable livelihoods and green economic productivity for customary landowners and communities

#### Based on the PANGA project in the Action Area: "Enhanced economic productivity and sustainable livelihoods".

- Over 85% of the PNG population is rural and relies on family-based agricultural activities for both subsistence and basic income. This form of agriculture was responsible for over 60% of all forest conversion to agriculture.

#### However, the increasing pressure on family agriculture has not been matched by significant improvements in agricultural productivity or diversification into other livelihoods. The lack of access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, as well as severe weather events, pests, and diseases. Additionally, there has been insufficient support for community-based conservation initiatives.

#### The lack of improved agricultural methods results from capacity and institutional constraints within the agricultural sector, leading to significant gaps in the provision of extension services to customary landowners and communities. This is further exacerbated by a lack of tenure security and limited access to credit for managing their land. This will help ensure secure livelihoods for families and protect their natural resources while also contributing to climate change mitigation efforts through other carbon and biodiversity finance initiatives.

#### The project, with its focus on potential beneficiaries, will identify the most suitable alternative livelihoods for customary landowners and communities, encompassing the full spectrum of agricultural activities. This approach is designed to build on the strengths and capacities of the customary landowners while also promoting environmental sustainability and socio-economic development as key pillars.

#### The aim is to provide a range of alternative livelihoods that are economically viable but which, while generating immediate economic gains, often result in significant environmental degradation—to a level where they may be unsustainable. This project will seek to address this issue by seeking to empower customary landowners and communities with sustainable economic opportunities that harmonize with their cultural practices.

#### The project will allocate the majority of its initiatives to activities that support the development of sustainable agriculture, which will be aligned with the unique needs and challenges of communities in PNG to implement alternative livelihoods. This effort will integrate sustainable agriculture with forest landscape


---
**Page 48**
---

management, ensuring that agricultural practices not only enhance productivity but also conserve the country’s rich biodiversity and forest resources.

The project will promote cooperative-managed financial mechanisms based on PNG’s local context, enabling communities to manage their own funds for sustainable land use activities. These cooperative-managed funds can only be used to establish the cooperative-managed financial mechanisms and cannot provide funding under them. The project will also support the development of a market-based approach that is inclusive of smallholder customary landowners and communities, many of whom rely on subsistence agriculture.

Efforts are made enhancing traceability and transparency to meet international market standards, such as those outlined in the EU’s REDD+ Regulation (EU) 2019/944. By doing so, the project will contribute to PNG’s efforts to become a producer of high-value markets for sustainably produced goods. For instance, this includes the development of a carbon credit market and certification and value chain improvements.

These initiatives are expected to create jobs while fostering long-term socio-economic development and environmental sustainability. The project will also ensure that traditional knowledge and customary land ownership rights are respected throughout its implementation phases. During the inception phase of the project, the potential for mobilising private investment for sustainable land use activities is explored through a feasibility study. This study will include the identification of partnerships with industry bodies, investment funds or private companies to maximise the project’s impact by addressing key risk factors to address.

Activities to be carried out under output 2.2 are:

- Activity I: Establish conservation agreements to ensure that sacred sites are effectively protected.
- Activity II: Cooperative-managed financial mechanisms
- Activity III: Develop a market-based approach for the agriculture value chain

The output and detailed actions will be designed based on the interim results and lessons learned from the EU-FCCB project, which is currently being implemented in PNG under the EU FCFR programme.

The EU STREET Programme aims to help improve the lives of people from East Sepik and Sandun provinces of PNG by focusing on increasing sustainable and inclusive economic development of rural areas through the promotion of agroforestry systems. The project will also contribute to poverty reduction while strengthening and improving the efficiency of value chain enablers, including the business environment for smallholder farmers.

Further, the EU STREET project supports compliance with the EUDR by promoting sustainable cocoa production in agroforestry systems. It leverages geospatial monitoring to prevent land encroachment, supports certification activities through a partnership with the Rainforest Alliance, and promotes gender equality among communities and enhancing sustainable supply chains. STREET ensures environmentally and socially responsible cocoa production.

The EU FCCB project comprises a set of activities very close to output 2.2 aiming at establishing the necessary improvements to achieve deforestation-free, resilient and inclusive food systems, agriculture and forest value chains in PNG. These include promoting agroforestry systems for climate-smart value generating activities, land stewardship rewards and diversification of income generation options, the provision of technical assistance and capacity building for smallholder farmers, and the support to customary landowners and communities and small and medium-sized enterprises on sustainable deforestation-free agriculture and forest value chains with a focus on gender-responsive value chains.

The alignment between the EU-FCCB, STREET, and the GCF RBP will be further warranted due to the interdependencies of these projects.

### ASSUMPTIONS:
- A: Key stakeholders are involved, with an emphasis on women and youth.
- B: National and Provincial authorities support project implementation.
- C: Project activities are financially viable.
- D: Accessibility to sites allows.
- E: Availability of capacity building activities.

### BARRIERS:
- Weak development of a forest sector.
- Lack of coordination between national and provincial authorities.
- Local communities with limited technical, financial, and organizational capacity.
- Land tenure laws severely limit access to land for community-based organizations in decision-making processes.
- Limited capacity of local government institutions to effectively manage forests.
- Inadequate training and technical personnel.
- Weak institutional framework for forest management.
- Lack of coordination between the forestry sector with other neighboring sectors, including agriculture and biodiversity conservation.

### PNG REDD-plus Results-Based Payments 2014/2015/2016

#### Long-term impact: Reduced emissions from land use, deforestation, forest degradation, and sustainable forest management.

**Objective:** To support the National and Provincial authorities of the PNG (REDD+ provinces) to implement the National REDD Strategy 2017-2027 with emphasis on the action areas: Strengthened legal framework, improved governance, and enhanced capacity building.

#### COMPONENT I: Inception Phase
- CAP updated and implemented.
- Implementation of a Stakeholder Engagement Plan.
- Using the ToC, implementing a monitoring and measurement system.

**COMPONENT I**: Enhanced institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial authorities and local communities for targeted provinces (cmt).

**OUTPUT 1.1**: Establish the National Land Use Strategy (NLUS) framework for targeted national and provincial governments.

**OUTCOME 1**: Strengthen and strengthen the capacity of national, provincial, and local authorities and civil society organizations to implement the NLUS for the CLAD.

**COMPONENT II: Enhanced economic benefits from sustainable land use through livelihoods of customary landowners and local communities in targeted provinces (cmt).

**OUTPUT 2.1**: Improve coordination between the national and provincial governments.

**OUTCOME 2**: Common market access for commodities from targeted producers, increased productivity, and increased profitability for targeted customary landowners and local communities.

# REDD+plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 47 OF 9)

### Selection of Targeting Provinces

The project will focus on implementation of direct actions on six targeted provinces: West Sepik, East Sepik, Western, Hela, Chimbu and West New Britain. The provinces targeted were selected under a prioritization process which identifies a set of variables to balance principles of effectiveness and equity among all the provinces:

1. Rewarding provinces with high performance in reducing emissions.
2. Reducing deforestation by considering the forest landscape integrity index, a continuous index of forest condition as determined by the degree of anthropogenic modification.
3. Considering provinces that may have a higher risk of deforestation and forest degradation because of anthropogenic activities.

To calculate the average ranking for province prioritization, different weights were allocated as follows:

| Average Ranking = (0.5 × R<sub>EMI</sub>) + (0.2 × R<sub>FLI</sub>) + (0.2 × R<sub>AD</sub>) |

Where:
- **Average Ranking** is the final ranking providing provinces.
- **R<sub>EMI</sub>** is the ranking corresponding to the Emission Reduction achieved by each province.
- **R<sub>FLI</sub>** is the ranking corresponding to the Forest Landscape Integrity Index.
- **R<sub>AD</sub>** is the ranking of anthropogenic activities.

The targeted provinces cover an area of 215,978,146.83 ha (46.58% of the country's total area), emissions reduction in the provinces during the period 2014-2016 was 29.15 million tCO<sub>2</sub>s. The combined estimated population in 2021 is 3.1 million people (20% of PNG’s population).

West Sepik Province\footnote{also known as Sanduan Province}, is situated in the northwestern region of PNG. It covers a land area of 35,920 km² and has a population of 421,470, estimated in 2021. The province features diverse geographical landscapes including vast river systems, fertile plains, and dense tropical rainforests. Dominating the region is the Sepik River, which flows from the highlands to the headwaters of the Sepik River one, the longest rivers in Papua New Guinea, which plays a significant role in the economy and culture of the people.

East Sepik Province\footnote{located in the northwestern region of PNG}, spans an area of 41,426 km² and is home to approximately 305,789 inhabitants. # Beneficiaries

As per criteria in Table 7, customary landowners and communities holding property or possession rights over customary land will be considered as beneficiaries of the project. Actors involved in forestry/timber and agricultural business will be considered in partnership with customary landowners and communities.

According to the REDD+ Strategy, this RBP Project identifies preliminary groups of beneficiaries at both the national and subnational levels. Based on this preliminary list and as part of the project's participatory approach, the project team will develop a more explicit definition of activities and intervention areas. The project will identify specific beneficiaries in Table 7.

Table 7: Definition of beneficiaries for the purpose of receiving support under the project

| Type of Beneficiary | Description (e.g., CCAS, PRIGFA, DLPLP, DAL-CEA, DNPMA) | Component |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Public institutions and national agencies | Key government institutions described as CCAS, PRIGFA, DLPLP, DAL-CEA, DNPMA. | Component 1 |

## C.2.3. Timeframe of implementation (for monitoring and reporting purposes):

Please provide the following information:

The execution of the project will be over 6 years, where it is expected that the first two years will be used to carry out the inception phase. This phase will maintain a zero-tolerance policy on SEAF and GBV, mainstreaming gender equality in all aspects of the project implementation, including through ESMAF, and implementing an increasing awareness and capacity building process what the national, provincial and local landowners and communities need to know about REDD+. A Strategic Environmental Management Plan (SEMP) will be developed, including a fit-for-purpose risk screening checklist.

Additionally, a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) will be developed to ensure meaningful participation and engagement of all stakeholders in the project. The SEP will also include a gender equality action plan for sustainable and equitable program implementation.

The third year is expected to focus on building relationships and assumptions that drive the program's impact (including a system to monitor GHG emissions and removals on the ground actions). In Year 4, the project will be implemented in full. The monitoring and evaluation system to assess efficacy, verify assumptions, and generate lessons for adaptive management.

### C.2.5 Implementation arrangements

PNG Government, through the Ministry of Environment, Conservation and Climate Change, has requested FAO to act as the executing entity in responding to such request. FAO will serve both as Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE).


---
**Page 58**
---

At the project level, the FAO Rome Headquarters Office will be the GCF Accredited Entity responsible for monitoring and providing technical backstopping during project implementation. It is the only body, aside from the Project Board, that can call extraordinary meetings if needed.

At the national level, a Project Board will be established to work on strategic decisions. It will be composed of the Climate Change Development Authority (CCDA) - which convenes and chairs and is represented by the Minister of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR), the National Environment and Environmental Protection Authority (CEPA), the Secretary of the Department of National Planning and Monitoring (DNP&M), the Director General of the Department of Land and Property Management (DLPM), representing the Government of PNG, and the PNG Environment Alliance (PNGEA) represented by its Chairperson.

The primary responsibilities of the Project Board are providing overall implementation guidance, reviewing and approving the financial and operational plans, as well as any other plans, as needed improvements. The project board will not annually but can call extraordinary meetings if needed.

Project Board Secretariat: Coordination and communication with the National REDD+ Committee, responsible for overseeing the cohesive function and implementation of the REDD+ Strategy and benefit-sharing mechanism. It will be composed of the Project Board Chairperson, the National REDD+ Technical Working Committee (NREDD-TWC), which will periodically inform to the National REDD+ Committee and the Project Board on progress in implementing the REDD+ Strategy and benefit-sharing mechanism.

Project Management Unit: Daily project management and interactions with stakeholders, as well as administrative, monitoring and accounting tasks, will be carried out by the Project Management Unit (PMU) funded by the GCF. PMU's main task follows the implementation of the annual work plan. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that the effective implementation of the annual work plans. PMU will be composed, at least, of the National Project Coordinator (NPC), a dedicated Monitoring and Evaluation Officer, a Social Development Expert, a Social Safeguard Expert, a Gender Specialist and Provincial Safeguard Specialists. Given the fact that all safeguard areas are interlinked with each other and with the project's main objective, issues relevant to customary landowners and communities are integral to this ESD and complementary safeguards. The PMU will ensure that the key concepts of the ESD and complementary safeguard areas of expertise to ensure key concepts to IP are adequately managed throughout project implementation.

The PMU will be responsible for developing the project monitoring activities and reports in coordination with the GCF, FAO Country Office (CO) and other relevant stakeholders. In addition, the PMU will be responsible for:

Following the implementation of the annual work plan, FAO Country Office (CO) will be responsible for the project's financial and operational implementation. This implies that FAO CO will provide procurement and contract services following FAO operational modalities for sourcing and procedures.

At this level, the objectives are to increase government’s national and provincial institutional coordination capacity in order to ensure the effective delivery of quality and sustainable livelihoods of smallholders and customary landowners and communities in the largest provinces. The project will be implemented through existing institutional bodies at the national and provincial levels to operationalize the activities to be carried out. The roles and responsibilities of the main institutions engaged in the project are described below:

* Provincial Climate Change Committee
* Provincial Land Use Planning Board
* Oil Palm Industry Board

## REDD+plus RBP Funding Proposal

### Overview of Key Agencies and Programs

#### GCF (Green Climate Fund)
- **Purpose**: To support climate initiatives in PNG.
- **Key Functions**:
    - Develop and update national climate change policies, strategies, and programs.
    - Ensure climate-related activities across sectors such as forestry, energy, and agriculture are aligned with the NDCs.
    - Integrate climate change considerations into national and sectoral development plans.
    - Coordinate with other agencies to ensure alignment of projects with the PNG Climate Strategy, Finance and Investment Plan, and related safeguards.
- **Safeguards**: Ensure that all projects comply with GCF’s standards and international best practices.

#### PNG Forest Authority (PNGFA)
- **Purpose**: To manage forest resources for sustainable development.
- **Key Functions**:
    - Develop and update the greenhouse gas inventory to track emissions and inform climate action.
    - Mobilize financial resources from international funds, e.g., Green Climate Fund, Adaptation Fund.
    - Oversee implementation of national policies on forestry, ensuring accountability and transparency.
    - Work with development agencies, NGOs, private sector entities, and communities to design and implement climate projects.
- **PNGFA as Recognized Corporate Entity**: Empowered to oversee the administration and enforcement of forest laws and regulations in PNG. Coordinates with other key sectors including forestry policy and related guidelines and codes.

#### CEPA (Central Environmental Protection Agency)
- **Purpose**: To ensure environmental protection, biodiversity conservation, and sustainable development.
- **Key Functions**:
    - Oversee and approve EAs for proposed developments to ensure environmental and social safeguards are met.
    - Monitor compliance with environmental laws and regulations and enforce penalties for violations.
    - Establishes, administers, and monitors protected areas including national parks, wildlife sanctuaries, and conservation reserves.
    - Implements measures to conserve PNG’s unique biodiversity, including endangered and endemic species.
    - Develops and enforces policies aimed at maintaining ecosystems and protecting critical habitats.
    - Works to ensure that the exploitation of PNG's natural resources (fisheries, minerals, timber) is sustainable.

#### Collaboration
- Collaborates with stakeholders to integrate conservation objectives into land-use and development plans.
