REDD+aka RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL  
GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 57 OF 99

- Supports initiatives that address climate change impacts on biodiversity and ecosystems.
- Promotes REDD+ programs by managing forests in priority areas related to carbon sequestration.
- Provides technical support to communities, government agencies, and other stakeholders to improve environmental management.
- Mediates land tenure disputes and land conflicts, particularly between developers and local communities.

### DLPP
The DLPP is a provincial policy that aims to give law as the Acts of Canada, 1989 & Regulations, and the Physical Planning Act, 1989 & Regulations and all other related land use legislation. It includes:

- Maintains and updates land ownership records, including customary, state, and alien lands.
- Administers the allocation, leasing, and registration of state and alienated land for timber harvesting.
- Facilitates the registration of customary landowners and communities through recognition of their rights to land and resources under the DLPP.
- Provides guidance on national policies related to land use, land tenure, and urban development.
- Regulates logging and land-use activities to promote sustainable and orderly development.

### Collaborate with relevant agencies
Collaborates with relevant agencies to resolve disputes over land ownership and boundaries, particularly for customary land. Develops agreements that ensure customary landowners and communities benefit equitably from land-based development projects. Monitors the implementation of climate-related policies and physical planning regulations. Implements measures to combat illegal land acquisition and speculative activities.

### PCOD
The PCOD is a provincial policy that aims to give law as the Acts of Canada, 1989 & Regulations and all other related land use legislation. These acts aim at decentralizing bodies to align national climate policies with local priorities and practices. The key responsibilities of the PCOD are:

- Translate and implement national climate change policies, strategies, and programs developed by the CCDA within the province.
- Develop provincial forest management plans that align with the NDC and NRS.
- Coordinates climate change activities among provincial government departments, local governments, and other stakeholders.
- Facilitate meaningful engagement with customary landowners and communities as described in the DLPP.
- Work with non-governmental organizations, civil society groups, and businesses to implement national climate policies.
- Support sustainable forest management and the implementation of REDD+ projects at the provincial level.
- Monitor the implementation of climate-related projects to ensure alignment with national goals and objectives.
- Build the capacity of local government officials, community leaders, and stakeholders to implement national climate change policies.
- Facilitate the exchange of best practices and lessons learned among provinces.

### PFMC
The PFMC is a provincial policy that aims to give law as the Acts of Canada, 1989 & Regulations. It provides for:

- A system of forest management information on forest management between all relevant parties.
- Consultation with the Provincial Forestry Committees on acquisition and allocation of forest lands.
- Assisting provincial governments in preparing forest plans and development programs.
- Making recommendations to the NFB or Forest Management Agreements, selection of options for land use, and other related matters.
- Making recommendations to the Chairman of the Provincial Forestry Committees on the issuance, amendment, or surrender of timber authorities and supervising extension services.
- Overseeing the receipt and distribution of government levies and charges and other benefits due to landowners.

### Implementation arrangements scheme.

PNG's forests are among the most ecologically distinctive in the world. They are home to 191 species of mammals, over 80% of which are endemic, and 750 bird species, more than 50% of which are endemic. Additionally, PNG has a rich diversity of plants, with more than 3,200 flowering plant species recorded in the country.

PNG's forests encompass a remarkable variety of environments, from coastal stolls and islands that combine mangroves, coral reefs, and seagrass beds to vast lowland rainforests. The forested areas extend from the coast to the highland provinces, reaching elevations of over 4,500 meters. The montane forests in this area are particularly rich in biodiversity.

These forests play a central role in the social, cultural, and economic livelihoods of the country's 8 million people. More than half of PNG's population lives within or near forested areas, where more than 90% of PNG's land is under customary ownership, and with over 80% of the population living in rural areas, forests are a critical source of food security for many communities.

PNG's forests also provide clean river systems for drinking water, while more than 500 species of wild plants are used for food. Additionally, PNG's forests contain a wealth of non-timber forest products that are essential to the livelihoods and resources are essential for the resilience of communities facing environmental challenges and the impacts of climate change.

However, PNG's forests are under significant pressure. Population growth and increased national and international demand for agricultural commodities, land, and timber are contributing to rising levels of deforestation and forest degradation, along with associated emissions.


---
**Page 62**
---

REDD+plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 18 OF 9**

The extensive loss and degradation of habitats due to deforestation and forest degradation not only leads to significant emissions but also endangers various global, national, and local assets. Habitat loss is the principal driver of biodiversity loss, which in turn contributes significantly to climate change through habitat degradation, pollution of waterways, sedimentation on coral reefs, and adversely affecting the culture and livelihoods of indigenous communities. These ecosystems are crucial for the livelihood security of these communities and the country’s resilience to external shocks.

At the higher level, the expected non-carbon environmental benefits of the implementation of the NREs have been estimated as follows:

- 6.5 million hectares of forest under conservation
- 470 threatened species for which habitat secured
- 200,000 ha of degraded land restored
- $75 million in sustainable forest products

For each of the components to be implemented during the project lifetime is expected to achieve the following environmental benefits:

COMPONENT 1. Increased institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial governments in PNG and the targeted provinces:

- Enhanced Tenure and Land Use Security: Improved systems, and frameworks to secure land ownership rights, including tenure security for customary lands (including land used to ensure FRC previously), reducing conflicts and enabling better long-term land management.
- Increased Inclusivity in Decision-Making: Greater participation of women, youth, and disadvantaged groups in decision-making processes, increasing equity and diverse perspectives.
- Enhanced Monitoring and Reporting Capacities: Strengthened ability to monitor and report on natural resources, fostering transparency and better-informed resource management.
- Improved Revenue Generation and Management: Expanded opportunities for generating and managing revenue from forest products, including timber and non-timber forest products.
- Policy Development and Knowledge Sharing: A strengthened knowledge base and capacity to inform forest and land-use management policies, ensuring that practices are sustainable and evidence-based.
- Climate Change Adaptation Impact: Strengthening institutional capacity fosters stakeholder collaboration, enhancing their ability to manage forests and respond to climate risks.

COMPONENT 2. Enhanced economic productivity and sustainable livelihoods of customary landowners and communities in the targeted provinces:

- Biodiversity Protection: Increased safeguarding of PNG’s unique biodiversity, including over 400 species identified as globally or nationally threatened.
- Sustained Environmental Services: Preservation of critical ecosystem services, such as water filtration, soil fertility, and climate regulation, along with access to non-timber forest products vital for local livelihoods.
- Conservation-Linked Tourism Opportunities: Developing eco-tourism opportunities associated with conservation areas, leveraging PNG’s natural and cultural heritage for sustainable economic growth.
- Sustainable Timber Markets: Improved market access and higher price potential for sustainability products, including timber from community-managed forests and other non-timber forestry products.
- Improved Food Security: Increased agricultural productivity and diversification, enhancing food availability and nutritional outcomes for local communities.

### Boosted Agricultural Revenues: Higher revenues from sustainable agricultural production, supported by improved practices, market access, and productivity enhancements.

These outcomes are designed to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), improved livelihoods, enhanced resilience, and the preservation of PNG's rich natural and cultural heritage.

The project is a model for sustainable development, environmental protection with socio-economic growth, contributing to long-term national goals and global sustainability efforts.


## D. Investment Framework

### D.1 Impact potential

In the period 2014–2018, PNG reported significant national REDD+ results associated with reducing emissions from deforestation and reducing emissions from forest degradation:

| Year | Emissions (tCO2e) |
| --- | --- |
| 2014: | 3,054.987 tCO2e |
| 2015: | 3,546.587 tCO2e |
| 2016: | 3,134.584 tCO2e |
| 2017: | 13,773.917 tCO2e |
| 2018: | 23,137.031 tCO2e |

This proposal emphasizes "low-emission development pathways - priority mitigation result areas" through two strategic initiatives:

- M7: Supporting implementation of the phased approach to REDD+
- M6: Reducing emissions from agriculture and related land use management

By leveraging existing national systems, this project will support the continued success of REDD+ under the guidance of Papua New Guinea's NRS. The NRS provides a robust framework to achieve additional emission reductions in the forestry sector, ensuring these efforts gain global recognition through the UNFCCC.

The NRS is a strategic framework for integrating REDD+ objectives into government policies, civil society programs, and private sector initiatives. It aims to provide a clear pathway for implementing REDD+, with essential investment areas to achieve REDD+ goals while promoting green development. To realize these ambitions, the NRS will engage all stakeholders, including national governments, provincial governments, communities, and businesses under a unified approach. This requires embedding REDD+ into national policies, integrating existing policies into sustainable practices, allowing for balanced conservation and development.

This proposal has the potential to catalyze transformative change within the forest and land-use sectors in Papua New Guinea by prioritizing low-emission development; the analysis seeks to make this possible.

## Approach to land use and economic growth, setting a trajectory toward long-term sustainability.

This vision aligns with Papua New Guinea's broader ambition of fostering an economy that simultaneously achieves both poverty reduction and environmental protection. At its core, this transformation promotes the sustainable and equitable use of forest resources, ensuring that benefits are shared equitably among all stakeholders.

To achieve meaningful emission reductions, the proposal addresses both direct and indirect drivers of forest loss and degradation. Coordinated efforts across government sectors, civil society, and private stakeholders at all levels will be essential to ensure long-term success. By integrating climate change considerations into economic or social development but instead encourages sustainable resource use that maximizes long-term benefits for PNG's people and environment.

The proposal is two-pronged: the first component addresses community adaptation and mitigation of the challenges. The second component targets economic productivity and livelihoods, particularly for customary landholders and local communities. By introducing sustainable forest management and agroforestry practices, this proposal aims to improve food security, reduce poverty, and provide long-term economic stability while protecting natural resources.

### Economic Benefits: Diversified income streams for customary landowners and communities and enhanced productivity of sustainable agriculture and forestry.
### Social Benefits: Strengthened livelihoods for marginalized groups, including women and youth, and increased access to education and health services.
### Environmental Benefits: Reduced pressure on forests through sustainable land use and preserving biodiversity and natural resources while promoting green development.

By addressing the direct and underlying drivers of deforestation and forest degradation and fostering sustainable development practices, this proposal offers transformative potential for PNG. It promises to deliver significant economic benefits by improving food security, reducing poverty, and providing long-term economic stability. The proposal is positioned as a global example of how countries can achieve both poverty reduction and environmental protection while promoting green development.

The potential impact of implementing the NBS is as follows:

* Full-scale implementation of NBS could reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation by approximately 10-15 million tons of CO2 annually.

The REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) (Page 8 of 9)

### The NRSP aims to preserve large areas of PNG's Forest cover, consisting over 70% of country’s land area and could safeguard an estimated 28 million hectares of tropical forest, crucial for global biodiversity.

The strategy emphasizes sustainable livelihoods, potentially benefiting over 85% of PNG’s population who depend on forests for their subsistence while creating thousands of green jobs and improving incomes in forest-dependent communities.

The estimated budget to implement the NRSP is $1 billion US over ten years. The GCF project is expected to allocate approximately 3%, resulting in the following estimated impacts:

- A net gain in emissions due to avoided deforestation by approximately 3 to 4.5 million tons of CO2 during the six-year implementation period.
- The safeguarding of an estimated 1.4 million hectares of tropical forest, crucial for global biodiversity.
- Benefits for over 510,000 people (4.5% of PNG’s population) who depend on forests for subsistence.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential

#### Potential for scaling up and replication

PNG faces the dual challenge of a rapidly growing rural population and increasing pressure on land resources, particularly in areas where forest cover is being lost at an alarming rate. This project aims to address these issues as a primary driver of emissions in the country. The project will focus on fostering sustainable economic development through improved agricultural productivity and livelihoods for rural communities while unlocking opportunities for rural communities. This includes supporting customary landowners and enhancing their capacity to manage forest resources sustainably, which are key components of the NRSP.

The primary objective of Component 2 is to strengthen food security within rural communities while advancing sustainable commercial agriculture and forest management systems. These systems will be aligned with evolving market demands such as those for the EU/US agricultural markets, ensuring that PNG remains competitive in global markets.

The commercial agricultural sector is a cornerstone of PNG’s economy. In 2015, the sector generated nearly $1 billion in revenue from cocoa production and $34 million from coffee exports. For example, cocoa production supports the livelihoods of over 60,000 families by providing sustainable and responsible production practices that improve yields while ensuring fair wages for workers, improved working conditions, and the preservation of vital high-value markets in Australia, Europe, and the United States.

On the other hand, approximately 80% of PNG’s population resides in rural areas, relying heavily on family farming systems. Over the past decade, there has been a significant decline in agricultural land conversion between 2005 and 2015, yet significant advancements in agricultural techniques or resilience to climate change have not been achieved. This project will address these challenges by promoting the provision of extension services to rural communities, resulting in low productivity and high dependency on external inputs.

To address these systemic challenges, the project will prioritize engaging relevant institutions and stakeholders with the resources needed to transform subsistence agriculture into a sustainable and climate-resilient system that provides improved livelihoods for rural communities. This approach will also generate additional employment and expand economic opportunities in rural areas. This integrated approach will promote the transition of smallholder farmers from subsistence farming to commercial production.

The project will initially implement direct activities in six provinces: West Sepik, Western, Hela, Southern Highlands, Morobe, and Madang. These provinces represent 20% of PNG’s total agricultural land area and, as of 2021, accounted for 33% of the national population. Through these targeted interventions, the project will promote sustainable livelihoods at a household level, including reduced deforestation, enhanced agricultural productivity, and improved livelihoods.

While the main focus is on the six targeted provinces, the project's design includes mechanisms to scale its outcomes to the entire country. The following factors enhance the potential for replication and nationwide implementation:

- **Capacity Building:** Strengthened institutional capacity at national and provincial levels will create a foundation for replicating successful strategies, including improved agricultural extension services and sustainable forest management practices.
- **Policy Alignment:** By embedding best practices into provinces' policies and aligning them with international standards, the project ensures its framework can be adopted across other provinces, fostering regional collaboration in development efforts.
- **Economic Incentives:** Market incentives for sustainable products and the economic benefits of diversified rural livelihoods will incentivize broader adoption of sustainable practices.

The project offers a pathway for national transformation by addressing both environmental and socioeconomic challenges. It aims to improve the quality of life in rural areas, increase economic opportunities, and build resilience to climate change. This comprehensive approach ensures that future generations can continue to benefit from the country's rich biodiversity and natural resources. Through this vision, PNG can achieve sustainable growth while preserving its rich cultural heritage for current and future generations.

## Knowledge and Learning Potential

Capacity development within the government is a critical component of the project, ensuring that institutions are equipped to implement sustainable policies effectively. Customary landowners and communities, who are the stewards of natural resources, will be empowered through capacity building in areas such as information, modern tools, and technical support. The project aims to empower landowners to actively safeguard their lands against encroachment and illegal activities while ensuring that customary laws and traditional practices remain intact on their livelihoods, enabling them to pass these values to future generations.

### Activity 1.3: Improving Coordination and Reporting

This activity focuses on improving coordination and reporting for the implementation of the NFRS, including strengthening the National Forest Resource Strategy (NFRS) and Sustainable Institutional Reforms (SIR). By enhancing institutional readiness and operational elements and integrating cross-sectoral information systems, this activity contributes to a more robust knowledge infrastructure that fosters long-term capacity development and informed decision-making.

### Strengthening NFRS and SIR

Strengthening the NFRS and SIR involves exhaustive training, capacity building, and technical support for PNG's national forest resource strategy. This includes developing a comprehensive database of information on land use, tenure systems, and reporting. This technology becomes a valuable resource, enabling more accurate assessments of the country's natural resources.

### Activity 1.3: Sharing Knowledge

This activity aims to share knowledge that can be codified into reports, policy briefs, case studies, and technical guidelines. These products can be shared with domestic and international stakeholders through various platforms such as the National Land Use Information System and Benefit Sharing System. This enables replication models for other countries in similar contexts, enabling PNG to contribute to global learning on sustainable land management and climate action.

## Contribution to enabling environments

PNG’s efforts to implement the principles of customary land ownership have posed significant challenges to developing and implementing effective land-use and development planning. Despite the enactment of the National Land Use Planning Act (2013) and related regulations, implementation has been slow due to lack of capacity at all levels.

This project aims to address these barriers by strengthening PNG’s technical and institutional capacities in land-related regulations (such as the National Sustainable Land Use Policy and the subsequent National Land Use Plan). The project will also support the development of a national REDD+ strategy. These activities are part of the overall implementation framework of this project, aimed at overcoming these barriers and creating a favourable environment for sustainable development.

This initiative seeks to empower communities and landholders as the primary custodians of their land and managers of invaluable strategic assets. At the local level, the project will focus on building capacities and strengthening institutions in the following areas:

- Develop and implement effective land-use plans tailored to local needs.
- Protect and manage critical ecosystem resources.
- Foster sustainable livelihoods by improving livelihoods and preserving ecological balance.

These actions will create a transformative enabling environment, equipping communities with the tools and knowledge to align their resource management practices with broader sustainable development goals. This approach ensures that local communities are positioned as active agents of change, fostering long-term resilience and inclusive growth.

Another relevant activity to be implemented during the project aims to strengthen environmental management, enhance financial planning and management, and conservation of ecosystems. A favourable enabling environment requires robust coordination between different levels of government and stakeholders in order to ensure effective implementation of financial management practices.

The coordinated implementation of these measures will generate significant opportunities for local communities and national governments alike. The project will also facilitate the development of a national REDD+ strategy that can catalyse transformative change across the country. This favourable enabling environment will facilitate the successful implementation of REDD+, ensuring long-term benefits in terms of biodiversity conservation, and climate resilience—building a foundation for PNG’s sustainable future.

In addition, by advancing key readiness elements, PNG positions itself to access additional proceeds from RBP grants and other funding opportunities. The project also enhances PNG's credibility with international funders and opens opportunities for scaling and replicating successful practices in other provinces. This will further strengthen PNG's institutional and capacity build, creating a feedback loop that strengthens the enabling environment for REDD+.

## Exit strategy

The exit strategy is focused on charting local capacity and empowerment. This includes training community members, coordinating with relevant institutions to manage and expand the project’s initiatives. Knowledge transfer will emphasize practical skills, technical expertise, and leadership development to enable stakeholders to take full advantage of the project's benefits.

Livelihood diversification is another cornerstone of the exit strategy. Communities should be equipped to pursue alternative livelihoods that reduce their economic dependency on any single sector. Providing skills training for non-agricultural activities, such as handicrafts or small-scale manufacturing, enhances resilience against economic shocks.

## D.A. Needs of the recipient

PNG has experienced one of the fastest-growing economies globally in the 21st century, with an average annual growth rate of 7%. This growth has been driven by foreign investments in the natural gas sector and exports of key resources, including petroleum (38%), gold (10%), timber (15%), and palm oil (4.3%). While PNG's economy is diverse, it remains heavily reliant on a few primary sectors such as agriculture, forestry, and industries; significant challenges remain in achieving the country's Vision 2050 goals due to ongoing environmental degradation and climate change.

Despite this economic growth, PNG has faced fiscal challenges. By 2016, mounting public debt—accounting for 10% of GDP—had impacted government revenues and economic stability. Credit rating agencies Moody’s and Fitch downgraded PNG's credit ratings in 2015 due to the country's high levels of public debt. In addition, reserves plummeted from $4 billion in 2012 to $1.7 billion by mid-2016, further straining the economy. The World Bank estimated that a $3 billion loan facility compounded these issues, forcing the government to significantly cut civil spending.

Given those circumstances, the government's ability to invest in new programs, including REDD+, is severely constrained.


The estimated costs for implementing the actions to achieve REDD+ are projected to be nearly US$1 billion over 10 years. This level of expenditure is significantly higher than the current government support for the sector and would require substantial additional funding from other sources across these sectors.

Monetization of carbon credits in relation to REDD+ results from the sale of credits as a result of afforestation investment in almost any sector in PNG. Although the potential returns on investment—including emissions reductions and associated environmental, social, and economic benefits—may be substantial, investors confront several key challenges.

### High risk of investment: PNG is considered a high-risk country for investment, ranking 109 out of 189 in the World Bank’s Ease of Doing Business index. In addition, frequent and unpredictable regulatory changes make many businesses hesitant to invest.

### Lack of infrastructure: PNG has poor road networks, limited electricity supply, and diverse country, which makes it extremely difficult to establish effective connections between commodity producers, plantation developers, and markets.

### High costs of doing business: Due to its remoteness, PNG faces challenges with limited transport infrastructure. The lack of a well-developed logistics sector means that the cost of transporting goods is high. Specialized equipment and skills and the expense of getting products to market are relatively high. This presents significant barriers for investors in the forestry sector.

The Rest+ Finance and Investment Plans identify significant financing gaps and limited financing for the main policies and measures included under the NFP:

- **Strengthened land use and development planning:** Action will focus on development, strengthening and rolling out the regulations under the draft Sustainable Land Use Act. This includes developing a national forest land-use plan and land-use planning framework that recognizes areas of high social and environmental value and strengthens both inclusion of all groups and the implementation of the law. The level of expenditure is over $20m over 10 years, with finance covering policy and regulatory development, land use planning support, and capacity building for local government.

- **Strengthened forest and environmental management, protection, and enforcement:** Action within the forest sector will support the transition to a sustainable and internationally recognized industry while also strengthening forest and environmental management. This includes developing a national forest strategy in addition.

Expenditure on this action is over $20m over 10 years, equating to just over $2m per annum. This level of expenditure is close to five times less than levels of spending required by PNGDAF, which is nearly $10m per year for the same period and includes a significant proportion of costs associated with forest management.

While many of these additional costs are equivalent to transactional elements, a large proportion is also incurred in relation to the implementation of the new Forest Act. This is because the new Act requires enhanced timber licensing ongoing cost (62c per annum by the end of 15 years when all staff are on contract) and the establishment of a new forest management system (approximately $30m over 10 years per annum) alone equates to costs of £7m in additional recurrent expenditure. This is close to the existing national budget for forest management.

Actions on environmental management will focus on strengthening the application of environmental safeguards and placing areas of social and environmental importance under conservation agreements with a focus on community-managed protected areas, as well as improving the capacity of communities to manage their forests resources, thus both helping to conserve global biodiversity, and local environmental services while also strengthening forest governance and contributing towards the achievement of sustainable land use.

This action would require an investment of over $20m in enhancing environment monitoring and permitting systems and their application, as well as the development of PNG’s Protected Areas network in line with the National Forest Strategy. This is equivalent to 50% more than current community-based conservation areas. This investment would represent a 50% increase against recurrent government spending on the sector.

### Enhanced economic productivity and sustainable livelihoods:

The investment area has the potential to contribute towards country targets of increased revenue from agricultural exports while also significantly increasing productivity and earnings per ha. Strengthened forest management will improve timber quality, reduce deforestation rates, and enhance biodiversity, while improved productivity and adaptive capacity in PNG’s will help reduce hunger and vulnerability to climate fluctuations and natural disasters.

**REDD+ Coordination and management**

The establishment of an effective REDD+ coordination and management structure will enable PNG to effectively scale up its efforts in the areas of forest monitoring, land use planning and project development to support these. #### Social and institutional needs

Addressing social issues remains the key to REDD+ in PNG. Over 85% of the nation's 7.9 million people live in rural areas, relying on subsistence agriculture for their livelihoods. These communities face significant barriers including limited access to health services, a mere 50% literacy rate, and scarce development opportunities.

Customary land ownership is widespread, covering more than 97% of the country's land area. While this represents an opportunity for REDD+ implementation through community-based forest management and investment, with unintentionally often creating hesitation. As the population grows rapidly at 3.1 annually, conflicts over land use are likely to increase in the coming years.

The rising demand for resources in rural areas intensifies environmental pressures, and large-scale investments can exacerbate inequalities between those who reap the benefits and those left behind. It is essential to ensure that REDD+ projects benefit all communities, particularly women and youth, in the long term.

Addressing institutional needs is crucial to achieving effective implementation of the NRS. The CCDA, as a newly formed institution, will play an important role in this regard. However, it remains unclear whether it will act as the national coordinating agency for all climate-related efforts in the country.

CCDA is taking proactive steps to embrace this leveraging REDD+ initiative to convene diverse stakeholders and develop a comprehensive strategy that includes the Ministry of Finance, Treasury, Forestry, Lands, Agriculture, and the Office of the Prime Minister. Furthermore, NGOs such as WWF, Rainforest Action Network, and the World Bank are also involved in these pivotal discussions.

However, other institutions must be engaged to ensure that they are aware of their respective sectoral targets by the project. The PNGDA stands out as one of the most organized entities at the sub-national level with a strong focus on forest management and conservation efforts.

Simultaneously, the CCDA struggles with significant constraints in fulfilling its wide-ranging mandate, including the provision of necessary resources and support.

The project will spearhead improved coordination and fostering collaboration among government agencies and subnational bodies. By addressing these institutional gaps, we can ensure that PNG effectively navigates the complex landscape of REDD+ implementation while supporting national-level decentralization efforts. Together, we can drive meaningful change that benefits our environment and communities.

PNG has established itself as a global leader in the REDD+ initiative, demonstrating a long-standing commitment to reducing emissions from deforestation. The country's active engagement with REDD+ is pivotal to its NDCs and essential for sustainable development. This commitment is underpinned by a comprehensive NRS crafted through extensive consultations with stakeholders at all levels of government. PNG has also aligned itself with PNG’s national and international obligations, encompassing critical frameworks such as the Medium-Term Development Plan (MTDP) 2015-2030, and the Strategy for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (SRaF).

The NRS is a vital framework comprising two essential components. The first component targets key action areas that are critical to achieving PNG’s REDD+ objectives. These include developing initiatives to enhance forest cover across the country. The second component focuses on the critical support required for these actions, including capacity building and institutional strengthening activities in line with the 2015 Paris Agreement and UNFCCC guidelines. PNG must establish a robust system for

**Conserving these efforts and transparently tracking progress in emission reductions and avoided deforestation:**

Implementing this strategy can ensure a more sustainable future and protect invaluable forest assets.

The CCDA spearheaded the NRS development, actively collaborating with a diverse array of stakeholders. In addition to the CCDA, key partners included PNG's National Environment Management Authority (NEMA) and the UNFCCC focal point. The CCDA coordinated the entire process, ensuring meaningful stakeholder involvement through various workshops and meetings. These discussions were documented in the "Stakeholder Engagement Paper" and 15 focused discussions on the NRS content. These consultations spanned across the country between August 2016 and February 2017.

After this comprehensive review, the NRS was submitted to the National Executive Council and provided to the PNG government for endorsement in March 2017. The CCDA has since been working with stakeholders to finalize the NRS content and positions the country as a model for effective climate action worldwide.

**Coordinating Committee on Climate Change (CCCC) and National REDD+ Steering Committee (NCC):**

The National REDD+ Steering Committee (NCC), the National REDD+ Steering Committee (NRSC), and a range of technical working committees (TWCs) that integrate diverse stakeholders, are responsible for implementing the NRS in its initial phase. These collaborative efforts will ensure a more effective and unified approach to climate action.

**National REDD+ Strategy:**

The National REDD+ Strategy, led by the General Manager for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) and Mitigation at the CCDA, serves as an overarching framework. It provides guidance on how to implement the NRS in its initial phase while also playing a crucial role in guiding the implementation of the NRS while also taking charge of the ongoing revision and development of the NRSC and comprehensive investment and financing plans aimed at balancing REDD+ initiatives.

The CCDA, in collaboration with the PNG government, is responsible for developing the NRSC and coordinating efforts to ensure a collaborative approach to achieving the goals of the NRS.

**UNFCCC:**

The UNFCCC has established a technical and advisory forum specifically designed to assist PNG in fulfilling its commitments under the UNFCCC. This committee actively participates in the development of the national FREL, LULUCF, and the implementation of the NRSC. These tools are essential for ensuring that PNG meets its obligations related to climate change mitigation and emissions. Furthermore, the initiatives undertaken by this committee are set to be enhanced, thereby providing a more comprehensive approach to addressing these issues.

**Social and Environmental Safeguards Task Force:**

The Social and Environmental Safeguards Task Force is another significant multi-stakeholder technical and advisory forum established in response to the complex international safeguarding requirements that PNG faces. This task force aims to ensure that all safeguards are effectively addressed within the UNFCCC protocol. To effectively address these requirements, the forum adopts an inclusive, country-specific approach. The task force also ensures that social and environmental safeguards are fully integrated into all REDD+ activities. By doing so, the TWF aims to protect the rights and well-being of local communities while ensuring that projects are implemented as PNG embarks on its journey towards sustainable forest management and climate change mitigation.

### D.E. Efficiency and effectiveness

As explained previously, the proceeds will be strategically invested in activities outlined within the NRS to foster a sustainable and successful cycle of REDD+. This focus occurs on the essential interconnectedness of the three NRSC phases: Development, Implementation, and Results Reporting.

PNG will use these funds to advance its REDD+ program by ensuring a framework for fulfilling and enhancing crucial readiness elements and advancing in the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy. The project will support PNG to continuously improve their carbon accounting and safeguard systems to achieve an integrated approach that is more effective and efficient, while also providing a broader range of carbon and climate finance on a rolling basis, ensuring the effectiveness of the investment.

In addition to the project has been designed to ensure the efficiency of the resources considering the following key elements:

### Targeting Provinces

The proposal aims to ensure greater efficiency based on a focused allocation of financing to the prioritized provinces. These provinces targeted were selected under a prioritization process which identifies a set of variables to balance priorities of effectiveness and equity among all the provinces.

#### 1. Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+)

Provinces that have demonstrated strong performance in reducing greenhouse gas emissions will be prioritized. Focusing on these areas ensures that incentives are provided to communities and local authorities that have been successful in implementing REDD+ activities, thereby encouraging further action.

Supporting these provinces further strengthens their ongoing efforts, showcasing successful initiatives as models for others to follow.

#### 2. Recognizing the Values of Forests beyond Carbon

Funding allocation will consider the forest landscape integrity index, which measures the condition of forests. Such areas with lower levels of intactness and higher levels of intact ecosystems will be prioritized, ensuring that funding supports areas where ecosystems remain relatively undisturbed and have high biodiversity values.

This approach recognizes the cultural, social, economic, and environmental importance of forests to indigenous communities and local authorities.

#### 3. Addressing Provinces with Higher Risks of Deforestation and Forest Degradation

The proposal will also prioritize provinces with higher population densities, as these areas face greater anthropogenic pressures on forest resources. These provinces are strategically deployed to implement preventive and corrective measures that address activities such as logging, illegal land use expansion, and infrastructure development, ensuring sustainable land management practices are prioritized.

#### 4. Promoting Synergies with Existing REDD+ Initiatives and Relevant Programs

The selection process considers current REDD+ initiatives and other forest conservation programs to build synergies between them. This includes the integration of climate change adaptation and mitigation carbon credit schemes, and landscape restoration efforts were evaluated to avoid duplication and enhance their effectiveness.

International organizations, NGOs, and government efforts, which were carefully analyzed to ensure that the project investments complement and strengthen existing activities rather than compete with them.

### Benefit Sharing

In addition, the funds will be allocated according to the key principles described in the Benefit Sharing and Distribution Guidelines, including the development or upgrade of these guidelines into the National Benefit Sharing Plan.

The guidelines aim to ensure that the financial and non-financial benefits generated from REDD+ initiatives are distributed equitably among stakeholders. These include indigenous communities, local authorities, and relevant government agencies.

### Implementation

The guidelines also ensure that all stakeholders are aware of the benefits, risks, and impacts of any REDD+ activity is implemented. This ensures that stakeholders are fully aware of the benefits, risks, and impacts of REDD+ projects and activities. It also promotes transparency in project implementation, strengthens community trust and participation, fostering a sense of ownership and responsibility.

A substantial part of REDD+ revenues is invested in initiatives that support sustainable livelihoods and alternative income-generating activities for communities living near forests. These investments aim to help communities transition from extractive practices to sustainable economic activities, ensuring long-term environmental protection and social well-being. This includes providing training and capacity-building for local authorities with the necessary knowledge and technical skills for sustainable forest management, monitoring, and enforcement.

Clear policies and legal frameworks are established to support REDD+ governance and benefit distribution, ensuring transparency, accountability, and equity. These governance mechanisms are overseen by

### Environmental and social safeguards

#### E.1. For the period of the achieved results

The Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) report submitted with this proposal demonstrates that during the period for which REDD+ result-based payments are being requested, activities were conducted in a way that is consistent with the requirements of the GCF’s Safeguards Framework, as defined by the GCF, FAD, AGF, NAP, and ESMA, and anchored in PMIs (policy, legal and institutional framework) relevant to the proposed REDD+ Strategy.

As noted in the GCF’s Final Report for the period 2015-2018, with UNDP’s support, a National REDD+ Strategy was developed as part of the country’s NAP process. The strategy was aligned with and based on its original concept note for GCF’s REDD+ Pilot Program (RPP). The RFP had envisioned to include an assessment of potential risks and associated safeguards in the context of the proposed project, through specific PMIs and actions to mitigate these risks and enhance the benefits, as part of a dedicated ESMA.

In turn, based on identified potential risks and hazards associated to the REDD+ strategy being envisioned to be tracked through GCF’s Safeguards Information System – the SIS – and reported through its Summary Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) report, the country developed an Environmental Screening Procedure (SEP) and Social and Environmental Standards (SES) as the proposed project was aligned with the GCF’s REDD+ Pilot Program. The SEP and SES were used to support PMIs’ funding proposal. However, since ESMA was completed prior to the RFP, thus PNG acknowledged that it had not been included in the RFP.

Noting the evaluation of the GCF’s RPP framework at the time and the fact that REDD+ Readiness technical and financial support through the FCPF and UNREDD were also finalized by 2018, a comprehensive thorough ESMA was developed as part of the country’s NAP process in collaboration with PNG and NFRF. The ESMA proposed for the project builds on outstanding environmental, social and governance potential risks identified during the REDD+ Readiness phase.

As concluded in detail in the ESA Report, “during the results period, PNG’s National REDD+ Strategy has not yet been implemented and emission reductions achieved over the results period cannot be attributed to any specific activity or intervention. The country’s NAP process was still ongoing at the time of readiness, establishing solid foundations that were later consolidated in its National REDD+ Strategy and its updated NAP.”

The country’s REDD+ actions were built on a wide range of technical and institutional assessments, consultations, and policy dialogues, which included the consultation of multi-stakeholder and multi-authority bodies. The REDD+ Readiness phase was completed under the PNG’s National REDD+ Strategy that had a robust legal and institutional framework that provided a coherent governance environment. This framework was designed to ensure the conservation of biodiversity, the maintenance of forest protection, sustainable natural resource use, and the safeguarding of sustainable livelihoods.

The REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 12 OF 9)

The existing policy and legal framework, together with enabling and early actions, allowed PNG to protect rights to prevent potential negative impacts on people and the environment resulting from the implementation of REDD+. The REDD+ readiness assessment in PNG was conducted by a team of experts who reviewed the rights of customary landowners and communities over land and forest resources, supported by technical assistance.

As examined in section C.1.2., over the REDD+ Readiness and results periods, PNG undertook a series of stakeholder engagement and consultative efforts, which overall have demonstrated consistency with applicable laws and regulations, as well as being consistent with international best practices for promoting meaningful participation of relevant stakeholders, including customary landowners and communities.

However, it is worth noting that, given the high level nature of the REDD+ Strategy in PNG, and the fact that substantial progress has been made on policy development and legal reforms, there have not yet been efforts to assess the inclusiveness and effectiveness of stakeholder engagement efforts as a result of implementation of the REDD+ Strategy. This is an area for further work and strengthening in the context of the IISF have been identified (see C.2.1 and the EEA report).

Meanwhile, while recognizing that over the results period PNG's National REDD+ Strategy has not been implemented in its entirety, it should be noted that PNG has made significant progress on policy development and legal and regulatory developments focused on the conservation, restoration, and sustainable management of forest resources. This includes the Forest Policy 1951, which was amended to include a new section on retroactive due diligence assessment conducted in support of FPA 78; The ESA Report annexed to this RBP Funding Proposal provides further details.

The following table provides an illustrative summary of policy development and legal reforms. This is an illustrative manner—indicating policy implementation, legal developments, and projects—to examine the extent to which these reforms have been implemented in PNG. Further information on each of the reforms are listed below:

* **PAM 1:** Implementation of the Forest Policy 1951 through periods and detailed planning that established a total allowable cut for timber harvesting every year. The Forest Policy 1951 addresses the need for the sustainable management of forest resources and adopts an integrated approach to forest resource management.

* **PAM 2:** PNG moving away from Forest Clearance Authority (FCA) to the Forest Management Agreement (FMA) which has provisions for sustainable forest management. This is in line with the country's commitment under REDD+ to ensure that all forest land uses are subject to a legal framework.

* **PAM 3:** Government's moratorium on the issuing of Special Agriculture and Business Leases (SABLs) in 2011 and subsequent suspension of SABL in 2014. SABLs were intended to promote large-scale commercial logging companies obtaining SABL without developing agriculture or securing consent leading to deforestation. The moratorium was introduced as a result of the government's decision for failing to follow proper land acquisition procedures.

* **PAM 4:** Strengthening the Management Effectiveness of the National System of Protected Areas (GIF). This includes strengthening the management effectiveness of national and local capacities to effectively manage the national system of protected areas, and address threats to biodiversity and ecosystem services. The capacity building activities were carried out in collaboration with partners that were established by customary landowners and communities, and conservation partners in key biodiversity areas. These activities have been implemented through a community-based approach within state and customary landowners and communities to manage protected area sites.

The four PAMS were selected based on policy significance and were deemed as representative of the REDD+ narrative in PNG. They cover a range of topics including forest management, production and conservation, as well as different types of instruments including legal development, capacity building, and community engagement. It is important to note that the assessment said that PAMs' assessment does not mean demonstrate any sort of linkage or attribution in relation to the emissions reductions.

The assessment concluded that the PAMS identified for illustrative purposes in this EAA stemmed from the effective implementation of the law and policy framework. The PAMS were carried out within a solid national legal and regulatory framework, which was developed through extensive stakeholder engagement promoted stakeholder participation, and adhered to the Caravan Safeguards and PNG's Forest Environmental and Social Management (FESM) standards, as set out in section C.1.2. of this EAA.

However, that data availability limitations hindered a more detailed assessment on the extent to which social and environmental impacts associated to said PAMs were assessed, identified and managed in compliance with the applicable safeguards frameworks. The REDD+ RBP proposal was developed over time, including dedicated mechanisms for reviewing, managing and tracking complaints and feedback. PAMs are subject to ongoing monitoring and evaluation through project documentation and a final project evaluation that was considered for the purpose of this assessment.

For activities involving customary landowners and communities, potential risks identified included social conflict or tension between different groups, as well as gender-based discrimination in relation to the roles of men and women in natural resource management. The risk on social conflict was not realized as the PAMs were developed with the involvement of all stakeholders. However, the REDD+ RBP proposal includes the analysis of historical social and land-use dynamics. Gender risks were addressed by following a gender-sensitive approach during the development of the PAMs. This included involving women in project socio and governance barriers, potential risks and opportunities identified as a result of the EEA Report will be further assessed through the implementation of the REDD+ RBP proposal during the project inception period, in preparation of the project's Environmental and Social Management Plan.

The internal complaint and grievance mechanisms of the Climate Change and Forest Authorities (CCDA and PFRA) are not designed to address complaints related to PAMs. The authorities do not have the capacity or resources to manage such complaints. Even when authorities managed to address complaints, they often were unsuccessful in tracking their resolution.

In the case of REDD-related issues specifically, the information available was even more limited, with the available information focusing predominantly on the registration of emerging "REDD+" pilot initiatives which are not yet operational and do not have a defined governance structure. The PAMs were developed to establish open, accessible channels for stakeholders on the ground to raise concerns and grievances, thus giving them an opportunity to participate in the decision-making process regarding the implementation of the proposed REDD+ measures. The results of the retrospective assessments of the selected PAMs are included in Annex 1. These assessments were conducted by the EEA team using a methodology for screening check-list done to the PAMs. Annex 2 includes the retroactive assessment of the PAMs along with the correspondence of the policy, legal and regulatory framework with PNG's integrated ESR Framework.

The EIA identified key opportunities for ensuring full compliance with the applicable safeguards over the RBP-proposed activities. These include: (i) strengthening the capacity of the CCDA and PFRA to manage issues related to gender and age; (ii) improving the conditions of underprivileged groups in REDD+ projects, including women and other vulnerable groups in REDD+ actions and the potential to improve the living conditions of underprivileged groups through the implementation of the proposed RBP measures; and (iii) developing tools and mechanisms to better meet stakeholder needs, and enhancing economic opportunities and sustainable livelihoods.

The EIA also recognizes that while good governance mechanisms in PNG support multi-sectoral coordination, continued efforts are necessary to strengthen coordination and align sectoral instruments. This alignment is crucial for the implementation of REDD+ activities and the management of associated social issues. These issues are addressed in this proposal and the use of proceeds further examined in the related safeguard documentation. The EIA also recognizes that there may be some overlap between the safeguards issues relevant to REDD+ in PNG, a dedicated ESF has been prepared; and an annex to the draft RBP.

The proposed ESF provides a comprehensive policy, legal, governance and procedural framework to guide on the ongoing screening, identification, assessment and management of all relevant environmental, social and governance risks associated with the implementation of the REDD+ RBP in full compliance with all applicable safeguards frameworks, policies and standards.


### E.1.2: The use for processes

Following the safeguards frameworks applicable to PNG's REDD+ RBP proposal – namely, the Cancun Safeguards, the GCF Environmental and Social, Indigenous Peoples and Gender Policies, and FAO FESM and other relevant national laws and regulations – a draft Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) with governance issues relevant to REDD+ in PNG. A dedicated ESF has been prepared; and an annex to the draft RBP.

The proposed ESF provides a comprehensive policy, legal, governance and procedural framework to guide on the ongoing screening, identification, assessment and management of all relevant environmental, social and governance risks associated with the implementation of the REDD+ RBP in full compliance with all applicable safeguards frameworks, policies and standards.

## Consistent with the GCF's terms of reference for the pilot programme, the proposed ESMF describes:

(i) how the proposed on-the-ground activities to be supported by GCF process will meet the requirements of FAO and the GCF’s ESF, Gender, Indigenous Peoples and Prohibited Policies, and guided by prevailing environmental, social and governance (ESG) criteria of the country directly applicable to the activities under relevant international treaties and agreements.

(ii) how environmental and social risks and impacts have been identified, assessed, managed in the context of the proposed ESMF, and will be monitored and mitigated as part of the REDD+plus RBP in conformance with FAO’s Environmental and Social Operational Pillar (ESOP) Screening, assessment, management and monitoring framework.

(iii) how the project will regularly assess and maintain the assigned risk category, informed by on-going stakeholder engagement, information disclosure, and grievance, conflict resolution and accountability mechanisms.

(iv) overarching and preliminary management and M&E processes, and implementation arrangements relevant to the scope of the proposed ESMF and the identified potential environmental, social and governance risks. These will be further refined in the context of a particular programme or project design to implement REDD+ plus activities consistent with the GCF’s terms of reference for the pilot programme.

(v) information disclosure and grievance mechanisms that have been undertaken during the RBP design, and how ongoing stakeholder engagement and consultations will continue to be implemented consistent with FAO’s ESOP Screening, assessment, management and monitoring framework as part of the RBP proceeds, in conformance with FAO’s Environmental and Social Operational Pillar (2 E3SOP) Stakeholder engagement, information disclosure, and grievance, conflict resolution and accountability mechanisms.

(vi) how the project will ensure free-of-charge and culturally appropriate access to information and grievance mechanisms for all stakeholders involved in REDD+ plus activities.

(vii) a comprehensive Exclusion List for the project, consistent with the full scope of relevant policies applicable to the RBP and its ESMF, and consistent with environmental, social and governance priority risks identified as a result of the screening process.

Consistent with the high-level and strategic nature of PNG’s National REDD+ Strategy and its priority action agenda, the proposed ESMF is designed to support the implementation of the strategy. The ESMF will be further refined in the context of a particular programme or project design to implement REDD+ plus activities consistent with the GCF’s terms of reference for the pilot programme.

The ESMF will identify and address key issues, as underscored in previous sections, the potential environmental, social and governance outstanding issues associated with the proposed RBP. The ESMF will also provide a framework for ongoing identification, assessment and management of risks and impacts on the environment, society and governance that may arise from fostering and ensuring effective and meaningful stakeholder engagement and consultations consistent with the GCF’s terms of reference for the pilot programme.

The ESMF will ensure that all stakeholders have access to information and to accessible and effective channels and mechanisms provide feedback and raise and address concerns. The ESMF demonstrates consistency with all applicable environmental and social safeguards policies and standards.

### E&S Risk categorization and justification

The proposed RBP Project is designed to result in positive social and environmental benefits including ensuring long-term sustainable livelihoods for communities while enhancing land planning, law enforcement, and by providing opportunities for the development of a market-based approach to REDD+ plus activities. PNG at different levels to enhance environmental management, protection, and land use regulations and monitoring systems. The ESMF will ensure that all stakeholders have access to information and to accessible and effective channels and mechanisms provide feedback and raise and address concerns. The ESMF recognizes the potential for negative environmental and social impacts that will need to be carefully managed and monitored.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 15 OF 9)

The FAO's Environmental and Social Risk Screening Checklist has been applied to the project considering its outputs and indicative activities, leading to the classification of the project as a Moderate Risk Project (Category B). By virtue of this classification, the project will be subject to an Environmental and Social Assessment that requires the formation of environmental and social management plans. Various Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMP) have been developed for the project in relation to the following elements: ESS1, ESS2, ESS3, ESS4, ESS5, ESS6, ESS7, and ES8S. The results from the screening are included in Annex 1 and Table 7 of the ESMP (Annex T).

As the final outcomes and activities will be defined during the inception phase, the Risk Screening Checklist will be reapplied when preparing a comprehensive ESMP for the project. No activities greater than Category B will be considered.

The Environmental and Social Assessment will be conducted in a participatory manner with stakeholders during the inception phase of the RBP Program, with the following objectives:

- Screen the project against the applicable environmental and social local context of prioritized areas for implementation under the RBP Program once the requirements for benefit sharing criteria have been achieved.
- Further clarify the applicable social and environmental standards triggered by projects implemented under the RBP Program; and
- Take steps necessary in the context of the ESMP to fulfill those requirements and make recommendations on how these will be addressed as they are carried out through the life of the project.

An Environmental and Social Management Plan will be elaborated during the inception phase, as a result of this assessment. The ESMP will:

- Provide time-bound specific recommendations for avoiding adverse impacts, and where avoidance is not possible, for reducing, mitigating, and managing those impacts for all projects implemented under the RBP.
- Further identify project activities that cannot take place until certain standards, requirements and mitigation measures are in place and carried out (completing and updating what has already been identified).

Additionally, consistent with the characterization of Indigenous Peoples in PNG - who are organized under hundreds of traditional villages and communities and comprise almost the entirety of the population (see Section 1.2.1), the project will be implemented through a Community-Based Management Framework where the vast majority of beneficiaries self-identify as members of an indigenous village or community, the ESMP will include provisions for the involvement of these communities in the design and implementation of projects. These communities are framed within the context of the REDD+ strategy and this RBP project, primarily in recognition of their rights to land and resources under the PNG Constitution and the national legal framework.

Key IP concepts have been incorporated into the ESMP and will be taken up by the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which will be developed during the project's inception phase, and constitute part of the ESMP and will also be reflected in the project design and implementation. This framework will be a core component system for the project to ensure that all projects are implemented with due regard to indigenous peoples' governance risks and opportunities relevant to customary landholders and communities as part of the project.

Component 2, Consistency with the GFDRR's Indigenous Peoples Policy and FAO's WP will be ensured. (See Section PPs 1.5., and 2.1 in Annex T).

A joint approach to Gender Violence (GVV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) will be ensured throughout project implementation and streamlined in project documents and operational procedures.

E.1.3 Consultations with stakeholders

Stakeholder Engagement during PNG's RBP funding proposal preparation

As described in section C.1.2, between 2008 and 2018, PNG undertook a series of efforts to foster stakeholder engagement for REDD+ implementation. The process was initiated by the National Stakeholder Consultation Workshop carried out as part of the stakeholder participation process under the GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Project. This workshop was held in Port Moresby on 28 November 2013, and resulted in a series of consultations undertaken with PNG between November 2014 and February 2015, in preparation of this RBP.

During the preparation of this Funding Proposal, PNG carried out a National Stakeholder Consultation Workshop. The workshop was held on October 8th, 2019.


On November 8th, 2019, the National Stakeholder Consultation Workshop was held in Port Moresby, organized by PNG's GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Project.

Its goals were to engage stakeholders on PNG’s progress with REDD+, initiatives and to seek stakeholder input for the GCF RBP program Funding Proposal.


The consultation brought together 59 participants, 40 males and 19 females, from 26 institutions, including National Government Agencies, Local Government Authorities, Non-Governmental Organizations, Community Groups, Research and Academic Institutes, Private Sector, Development Partners and Provincial Climate Change Committees.

After a general update about the PNG progress on REDD+ and the opportunities in the GCF RBP pilot phase, each group was given time to discuss their concerns and provide feedback. The groups also provided specific inputs related to Safeguards, ESMF and Legal framework. Achieved results and results period (2019-2023) were discussed.

In the final session, each group presents the main findings and recommendations to be discussed in a general session. The main direct contributions from the workshop were related to:

* Inclusivity: Ensure gender equity with plans to allocate 50% of benefits to women.
* Best practices: Identify best practices for REDD+ implementation.
* Coordination: Highlight the need for stronger institutional frameworks and local-level participation.

During the National Stakeholder Consultation Workshop, a number of recommendations were made by participants to gather further contributors from stakeholders. This contribution will be assessed during a National Validation Workshop scheduled in early 2020.


The workshop was a crucial milestone in PNG’s efforts to access GCF funding. The focus on safeguards, inclusiveness, and sustainability reflects PNG's commitment to environmental conservation and equitable development.

# Stakeholder Engagement and Consultations during project implementation

PNG is committed to the full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular customary landowners and communities, in alignment with the applicable safeguards policies. As part of the REDD+ plus RBP programme, PNG will continue to engage with all relevant stakeholders at national and subnational levels.

Building on this preliminary work, and consistent with previous stakeholder engagement, participation and consultation efforts, PNG will ensure that the project is fully informed by the results of the inception phase of this project—alongside the development of the ESMP—once there is a more refined definition of activities and outputs.

A comprehensive stakeholder mapping and socio-cultural characteristics are envisioned as part of the same process. This information to inform the ESMP and the SSB. Stakeholder mapping and analysis will examine, inter alia, the following aspects to understand about each relevant stakeholder:

- Type/region of stakeholder
- Level of engagement
- Area of interest
- Relevant Activities
- Preferred Communication Methods
- Roles and responsibilities

The project will also take advantage of existing multi-stakeholder platforms at different levels. For instance, at national level: PNG has the National Forest Board which brings together representatives from government agencies, civil society organizations, private sector, indigenous peoples, local communities, NGOs, industries, Association of Foresters, provincial & local level governments, landowners, women, the civil society and other stakeholders. At subnational level: PNG has established a multi-stakeholder platform for one for each province, assisting the National Forest Board in fulfilling the objectives and functions of FAD. At district level: PNG has established a multi-stakeholder platform with the District Forestry Service for the region, the local government, civil society and landowners of the province.

The incorporation of stakeholder engagement into the project design will ensure that all activities under the project will be used to conduct further and specific stakeholder consultations and social and environmental assessments. The results from these consultations will inform the development of the project management plans and to inform the broader design of the REDD+ Program, including the identification of the key stakeholders and their roles and responsibilities. Priority areas for intervention will be developed and refined in consultation with the relevant stakeholders.

Over the inception period, periodic consultations will be held in the four priority provinces to gather further ownership and commitment from all relevant stakeholders on the project design and implementation plans, involving key groups of stakeholders as well as those with assigned roles in the project organization structure (e.g., representatives of FAD Country Office, REDD+ regional policy and technical advisors, where feasible).

Meaningful, effective and informed stakeholder engagement and participation will continue to be undertaken to build capacity for sustainable forest management and conservation. This will include identifying risks and/or mitigating any potential risks in a timely manner. The scale and frequency of the engagement will reduce as the project progresses and the project design is finalized. Any issues or concerns raised by affected communities once completed, the Stakeholder Engagement Plan will continue to evolve with the project. PNG will also ensure that all relevant stakeholders are informed about project plans, as needed. Stakeholder engagement arrangements will be consistent with requirements of the FAD FEAS and the PNG’s own safeguards framework.

PNG conducted a series of stakeholder engagement and consultation efforts. Since it is central to regarding information in a timely, accessible and culturally appropriate manner, including in relevant local languages

Noting interest challenges in the country to ensure effective and meaningful stakeholder engagement and information dissemination efforts, dedicated efforts will be supported by the project, as part of a Community Engagement Plan for the REDD+ project over the inception phase.

To date, a dedicated webpage has been available, managed by CCDA and with the support of UNDP26 on which relevant documents for a project, including the ESMF, Gender Action Plan and other safeguards information are available. The website is in English only (see details in E.5). PNG is one of the most ethnically and linguistically diverse countries in the world, with over 800 languages spoken. It has four main language groups: Papuan, Austronesian, Australian and Melanesian. The major ethno-linguistic groupings are: Austronesian (Papuan) peoples, found mainly along the coastal regions and islands; Melanesian (Austronesian) peoples, found in the central highlands and mountainous areas; Australian (New Guinean) peoples, found mostly in the highlands and interior regions. These groups speak hundreds of languages, with many communities speaking only a few hundred words.

Consistent with the GCF and FAO policies on access to information and information disclosure, all relevant documents for a project, including the ESMF, Gender Action Plan and other safeguards information are available at all stakeholders’ level, in customary landowners' and communities, in a form and language that is accessible and culturally appropriate. The project will ensure the widest dissemination of information in an effective and meaningful participation. FAO is committed to disclosing information in a timely manner, placing emphasis on the needs of specific groups which may be affected by project implementation (e.g. literacy, gender, differences in language or accessibility of technical information or communication tools).

FAO will disclose information in a timely manner and in a way that is accessible and culturally appropriate, placing due attention to the specific needs of community groups which may be affected by project implementation. In order to ensure the widest dissemination of project information, local and accessible disclosure tools including but not limited to: billboards, community radio broadcasts) will be utilized in addition to the standard portal disclosure tool.

**Feedback and Grievance Redress Mechanism**

As examined in the ESMF Report, PNG had conceived a dedicated GRM mechanism as part of the overall National REDD+ Strategy. Building on existing sectoral and institutional mechanisms and procedures in place (PNG’s Ombudsman Office, CCDA’s Complaints Unit, etc.), the project will support the development and put into operation. Rather sectoral, institutional and customary landowners and communities GRM procedures will be developed and implemented to ensure that all stakeholders’ concerns, issues and grievances related to proposed REDD+ projects and activities are addressed in a way which is accessible, predictable, equitable, transparent, rights-compliant, open to continuous improvement and complaint resolution.

FAO PNG Country Office will ensure a dedicated channel for receiving complaints, including a dedicated email address. A list of all relevant stakeholders’ contact information (including landowners and communities) from customary landowners and communities will be identified and put in place during the inception period of the project, consistent with dedicated provisions to be developed in the ESMF.

E.2 Risk assessment

E.2.1 For the period of the achieved results

REDD+ in PNG is framed within a robust legal and policy framework. The existing policies, laws and regulations of PNG provide the necessary key enabling conditions for REDD+, including guidance on benefit sharing and conflict management, guidance on environmental conservation and sustainable management of natural resources, among others.

PLRs set out the political and legislative context for climate change mitigation in the country and the institutional mandates and responsibilities of all sectors involved. The following are the key national policies, laws and regulations that have a direct bearing upon implementation of these emission reductions can be attributed.


Constitution of PNG

• Fourth National Goal and Directive Principle, and section 25: Promotes the preservation and replenishment of all natural resources that, including natural forests within the development of all national legislation and policy.

• Constitution of PNG, sect.31: Recognizes customary landowners as owners of the land.


Acts of the Parliament

• Forestry Act 1991: Regulates the management, development and protection of PNG forest resources.
• Environmental Management Regulations 2005: Governs and regulates the protection of the environment and impacts of natural resource management activities.
• Land Tenure Act 1987: Provides for the creation of customary landholder groups called K.D to hold, manage and deal with alienated land.
• Land Tenure Act 1987: Provides for the registration of customary land in PNG to register their customary land voluntarily.
• Climate Change Management Act 2015: Provides the legal basis for all climate change programs and activities in PNG, setting out the various administrative, financial and project related functions and powers.


National Development Policies and Strategies

• PNG Vision 2050 (launched in 2015): Long-term underpinning strategy for PNG's development and all its priorities.
• Medium Term Development Plan 2011 - 2015: Sets out specific indicators and targets relating to the development of PNG’s resources and the delivery of its services throughout the country within the medium term.
• Strategic National Sustainable Development 2014: This policy promotes sustainable development and management of natural resources in PNG.


Sector Plans and Policies

• National Forestry Policy 1991.
• Forest Act 1987.
• Forestry Development Guidelines 2009.
• Forest Sector Development Framework for Action Policy 2009-2015.
• National Sustainable Land Use Policy (drafted in 2012 and revised in 2014).
