# REDD-plus RFB Funding Proposal

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 8 of 9)

### Additional Relevant Regulatory Framework in PNG:

- **Environmental Impact Assessments (EIA):** EIAs are integral to the environmental licensing process and have been used as a decision-making tool in PNG. They are obligatory for all activities with a potentially significant environmental impact.
- **In the context of the management of customary lands:** PNG has a Registry of Incorporated Land Groups (ILG), a national public registry, mandatory for all customary land subject to some form of commercial and/or extractive use. The ILGs are governed by the Land Act 2009, which replaced the earlier Land Amendments (Agriculture) Act 2009 and recognized within the Land Act 1996, the Forests Act 1991, the Minerals Act 2015, and within the agriculture and mining sector legislation.
- **Regarding customary people's rights to the land:** In PNG, 87% of the land is under customary ownership by the indigenous peoples. The customary laws of Papua New Guinea (PNG) and customary practices of the peop[e]s of PNG are also recognized within Schedule 2.1.1 of the Constitution.
- **Grievance and Redress Mechanisms:**

During project design and implementation, individuals or groups may face adverse effects such as land use conflicts, loss of livelihoods, environmental degradation, or health impacts from pollution or pesticides use. To address these concerns, PNG implemented grievance mechanisms at national level and in the provinces.

Over the results period PNG's CCDA relied on prior GVM procedures to track stakeholders' concern/ issues grievances related to proposed REDD+ projects and activities. Given that PNG’s population largely lives under customary tenure systems, it is important for the project to engage with customary landowners and communities including inter alia, one involving the April Saluma ILG's and the project of the same name, a high profile case in PNG where the project was suspended due to concerns raised by the Tavola ILGs as part of an ongoing land dispute.

At provincial level, there are several mechanisms available, including the PFGCA, which replaced its through legal or higher courts. Oversight was provided by the PNG Ombudsman Commission and the National Environment Management Authority (NEMA).

Additionally, the 2015 Climate Change Management Act established a legal basis for resolving disputes arising from climate change adaptation projects in accordance with the provisions of the Land Tenure Act and addressing land disputes under the Land Dispute Settlement Act.

At provincial level, there are several mechanisms available including the PFGCA which replaced its through legal or higher courts. Oversight was provided by the PNG Ombudsman Commission and the National Environment Management Authority (NEMA).

Sub-national mechanisms included ombudsmen entities and traditional legal institutions often categorized as "tribal courts" in PNG. A number of these bodies have been established to provide a forum for provincial entities ensued grievance coordination and resolution. The Village Courts, operating under the Village Courts Act 2015, are one such example. These mechanisms were designed to address land disputes and addressing land disputes under the Land Dispute Settlement Act.

At provincial level, there are several mechanisms available including the PFGCA which replaced its through legal or higher courts. Oversight was provided by the PNG Ombudsman Commission and the National Environment Management Authority (NEMA).

Sub-national mechanisms included ombudsmen entities and traditional legal institutions often categorized as "trib

The PNG Anti-Corruption Strategy (2010-2020) was a detailed and comprehensive framework developed to tackle the pervasive corruption affecting various sectors of the country. Overall, the PNG Anti-Corruption Strategy aimed at promoting good governance, reducing corruption. By implementing its provisions, the nation aimed to create a foundation for more effective governance, promote social justice, and achieve sustainable development in PNG.

## Transparency

During the 2014-2016, PNG implemented key measures to address transparency and strengthen institutional frameworks, particularly in alignment with commitments under international agreements and national legislation. These efforts contributed to the improved government accountability and transparency in various areas of governance, including land use management. Below is an overview of some of the institutional framework for addressing transparency during this period.

### Legal and Policy Frameworks Supporting Transparency

- **Climate Change (Management) Act 2015:** The Climate Change (Management) Act 2015 was a comprehensive piece of legislation that established the CCDA as the central body to oversee, coordinate, and monitor all climate change activities, including implementing the NDC. It also provided for the establishment of the PNG Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), which supports the CCDA in carbon project development, monitoring, and reporting on GHG emissions and climate change initiatives.
- **National REDD+ Strategy:** The National REDD+ Strategy was developed to align with international standards for emission reductions and ensure compliance with the UNFCCC. The NRS emphasized the importance of community participation in forest management activities and the conservation stocks, supported by the implementation of a robust MRV system.
- **PNG Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF):** The FCPF was established to harmonize sectoral development plans with the principles of responsible and sustainable development. It provided a framework for PNG’s efforts towards national development goals, including environmental and climate-related targets.

### Institutional Mechanisms for Transparency

- **CCDA:** The CCDA was established as the focal institution for climate transparency. Its responsibilities included compiling and submitting national communications, BURs, and other reports required under international agreements. The CCDA also managed the PNG Forest Carbon Partnership Facility system and facilitated stakeholder engagement for inclusive decision-making.
- **Forest Monitoring System (FMS):** The FMS was developed to monitor forest cover changes in PNG supported by the PNGFA and the DLPUP. The PNGFA collaborated with the CCDA to provide data on forest cover changes and advocated for the development of a national land use information system, which would support the development of transport land use planning frameworks.
- **REDD+ Transparency Information System (TIS):** The TIS was central to PNG’s transparency agenda. This system was designed to collect and analyze data on forest cover, deforestation rates, and other relevant indicators related to REDD+. The TIS ensured that PNG could produce reliable and transparent reports on forest-based emissions reductions in compliance with the UNFCCC REDD+ funding.

### Transparency in Financial Management

- **Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative (EITI):** EITI’s participation in the ETI was instrumental in improving financial transparency in resource extraction, a sector linked to environmental and social impacts. PNG’s EITI process aimed at increasing transparency in the mining and natural gas sectors, aligning with broader efforts to reduce corruption and improve governance.

### Stakeholder Engagement and Capacity Building

- **Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (MSP):** MSP emphasized the importance of multi-stakeholder engagement in its transparency agenda. Various platforms were established for civil society, public sector actors, and local communities to be established to facilitate inclusive decision-making and ensure transparency in policy development.
- **Capacity Building for Data Collection and Analysis:** Capacity-building initiatives were implemented to strengthen the technical skills of government officials, researchers, and local stakeholders in data

Collective analysis, and reporting. These efforts focused on improving the quality and reliability of data used in MRV systems, GHG inventories, and national reports.

The transparency framework implemented during 2014-2016 enabled PNG to establish foundational systems for monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) of its REDD+ activities. While significant progress was made, challenges remained, including limited technical and financial resources, fragmented institutional arrangements, and a lack of capacity at the national level. The transparency framework provides a solid basis for scaling up transpiration efforts and accessing international climate finance to support PNG's low-carbon development strategy.

## E2.2 For the use of processes

### Adequate policy and regulatory support

This project will contribute to key existing national programmes that have strong political backing and direct ownership, including:

- NRS 2017–2027: PNG’s NRS establishes the country as a global leader in REDD+ efforts, which is crucial for achieving its climate change mitigation goals. Developed through extensive consultations led by the CCDA, the NRS aligns with national and international commitments to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+), including forestry, agriculture, and conservation. Rolling 5-year development plans serve as primary policy documents that guide implementation of PNG’s National Climate Change Policy, NSLUP, and Climate Change Mitigation Plans.
- Staff Development Plan: Aims at promoting green growth and efficient resource management.
- Forests and Climate Change Framework for Action Policy 2015: Guides forestry-based mitigation, governance, and partnerships.
- The Protected Areas Management Policy 2015: Provides a framework for coordinated spatial planning to manage natural resources sustainably.
- Provincial Forestry Development Plans: Focus on protected areas by 2025 in 5–10 years, focusing on biodiversity conservation.
- Climate Change and Development Policy 2014: Establishes systems for GHG MRV.
- Forest Policy and Plan 1992: Advocates for sustainable forest management through detailed planning, conservation of natural resources, and the promotion of community forestry.

These frameworks collectively support PNG’s commitment to climate adaptation, sustainable development, and forest conservation.

The project will be supported by the legal and regulatory framework, minimizing risks of conflicts, illegal disputes, or environmental degradation. The project adheres to PNG's key environmental and land use laws, including:

- Forestry Act: Governing forest management
- Environmental Impact Assessment Act: Regulates impact assessments requirements
- Land Group Incorporation Act: Protecting customary landowners' rights and ensuring their participation in the REDD+ process

The project will facilitate building for government agencies, customary landowners and communities, and other stakeholders. It promotes institutional coordination among entities like the CCDA, the PNGFA, and provincial governments.

The project will be supported by a mechanism to ensure transparent governance and accountability needed to address issues of power imbalance between central and subnational authorities, ensuring customary landowners and communities have a voice in decision-making processes.

### Comparative Advantages of FAO

With a well-established presence through the FAO Representation in PNG, the organization supports various initiatives across the country, including agriculture, forestry, food security, and land management.

The FAD Country Programming Framework (CPF) for PNG focuses on sustainable resource management, food security, and inclusive economic development. It aims to enhance forest and tree management, promote sustainable livelihoods, and improve access to markets for smallholder farmers in order to increase nutrition and food security. The CPF also seeks to increase income, employment, and food security while supporting the achievement of national priorities.

FAO's broad mandate across multiple disciplines enables a holistic and multi-sectoral approach to identifying and supporting climate change adaptation and mitigation through its programming framework.

As an implementing partner of the UN-REDD Programme, FAO has had a pivotal role in supporting PNG’s REDD+ readiness process, providing expertise through technical capacity building and sustainable forest management training.

Programs such as the Mitigation of Climate Change in Agriculture (MICCA) and the Adapting Inception to Climate Change (AICC) demonstrate FAO's capability in implementing Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) initiatives.

FAO’s commitment to capacity building and institutional strengthening is another key advantage. The organization has a long history of working with governments, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders, which is essential for securing local support and ensuring project sustainability. FAO’s ability to engage with all levels of government and its role in land-use planning can play a crucial role in land-use decisions, further enhancing the effectiveness of project implementation.

Key lessons from past projects implemented by FAO, and which will be incorporated into the implementation of this proposal, include the importance of engaging with local communities for successful project delivery. Local support is essential for success. Gender inequality remains a challenge, with women often lacking access to resources and decision-making power in forest management. The experience gained through completing the National Forest Inventory (NFI), while the GEFTI-PROJ project has demonstrated the effectiveness of participatory approaches in land-use planning and forest governance, highlights how these are essential for achieving sustainable development. Additionally, the CPF aligns with GCF’s Vision 2050 and the National Development Plan.

In addition, FAO has developed a range of tools and guidelines to support the effective implementation of climate, agriculture, social, and environmental actions that will support the project implementation.

In order to ensure the successful implementation of this proposal, FAO will continue to provide technical assistance to support land tenure governance, facilitating improved institutional coordination capacities at national and provincial levels in PNG.

### Provincial and national authorities in protecting local land rights

#### Promoting community participation in land governance

## Solutions for Land Administration (SOILA)

The SOILA suite provides technical solutions for land administration, particularly in improving government capacities for land registry management, data standardization, and the development of a single window system to improve access to land administration services, enhancing efficiency and transparency. SOILA suite includes:

- Open Data Suite for Land Registry Management: This suite provides tools and software to manage and monitor tenure rights at the local level. It can be utilized by PNG's national and provincial authorities in managing land registry data, ensuring secure tenure security, and promoting transparent decision-making in land administration.

In relation to Component 2, SOILA provides a range of tools, software, and training programs to enhance economic productivity and improve land governance.

## Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) Toolbox™

It is a comprehensive digital platform that offers practical guidance, case studies, and methodologies to improve forest management practices. It supports the development of sustainable forest management plans for timber and non-timber forest product harvesting, including agroforestry-based forest management and strengthening governance and market access for forest-related products.

## Open Forestry Suite for Forest Monitoring and Resource Assessment™

It includes software tools that enable users to collect, analyze, and visualize data related to forests. This suite helps in better managing forest resources. Key tools include Custom ELM, SEPA, and Open Forest Catalog.

## FAO Forest and Farm Facility (FFF) Support™

The FFF offers extensive strengtheners and technical assistance for timber and non-timber forest products training on cooperative models and sustainable enterprise development. It also provides support to enhance recognition of customary landowners and communities in forest governance.

### E.3 Gender considerations

#### E.3.1 For the period of the achieved results

Over the period of the achieved results, PNG had undertaken a series of efforts to promote gender equality and social inclusion, as follows:

- The call for equity and participation by PNG's Constitution under its section 55 which encourages the equal enjoyment with women in all spheres of life. Many of the key gender policies adopted at country level have effect when the law is made.
- The Papua New Guinea National Policy for Women and Gender Equality 2017-2015 as the overarching national policy development framework in PNG. This policy provides the government's mission to promote improved equality, equity, and social inclusion of women. It sets objectives to address gender inequality by establishing a comprehensive transformation agenda that promotes gender equality into reality through related policies and mechanisms. It remains a key guiding framework for gender interventions in PNG.
- Incorporation of Human Capital Development, Gender, Youth and People Empowerment as one of the strategic focus areas under PNG's Strategic Plan 2016-2050 (Vision 2050) which is the national development strategy that guides PNG's social and economic development.

- The Climate Compatible Development Management Policy is 2014 (mention) of the need for "fair and equal access" to information, representation of Women and Girls' participation in all activities so as to ensure ownership of activities.

Moreover, as underscored in the ESR Report, PNG's Constitution and land and forestry legislation guarantee clear land tenure rights to customary landowners and communities and recognizes the extent of these rights. The Constitution also provides for the participation of women in decision-making processes including in relation to decision making. The Constitution recognizes the right of women citizens to participate equally with men in all spheres of public life, including in the administration of justice as set out in Article 10(2) and Article 34 of the Constitution. This is also reflected in the PNG's National Policy for Women and Gender Equality 2011-2015.

Gender equality has been recognized as a cross-cutting element for PNG's NDCs' REDD+ Strategy, which aims to mainstream gender equality into all aspects of its REDD+ strategy. Gender equality is recognized in the context of land tenure rights and access to resources, particularly in customary landowners and communities, and is promoted under the National Policy on Women and Gender Equality 2011-2015. The NDCs' REDD+ Strategy has established a gender focal person at the national level who will be responsible for ensuring that gender equality and the right to free, prior and informed consent', and a dedicated criteria has been established to ensure that all projects are gender-sensitive. This is also reflected in the National Policy on Safeguards (d) Gender equality is promoted and protected in the context of the responsibility of PNG's NDCs' REDD+ Strategy.

Despite there being women in politics or decision-making at all levels of government, as of 2014, five out of eleven Departmental Nationals have no gender-related or gender-sensitive policies and the rest had incorporated gender equality into their existing policy structure at CCDA influenced gender responsive climate policies in the land and forest sector.

Therefore, PNG has undertaken a series of efforts to mainstream gender issues and priorities in the context of its REDD+ Strategy. These include the development of a Gender and Climate Change Action Plan and a Gender and Gender Action Plan. While acknowledging that REDD+ implementation in PNG has not yet started, no actual decision making related to REDD+ has been undertaken by the Government of PNG. However, women's participation throughout REDD+ readiness in PNG has made it a constructive initiative to provide opportunities for women to participate in climate change and forest resource management.

The Gender Engagement and Gender Action Plan provides a comprehensive overview in relation to women's relationship with forests and forest resources, as well as examines in more detail women's participation in PNG's NDCs' REDD+ Strategy. The plan also includes an analysis of the gender impacts of the project at relevant gender specific assessments undertaken in the country, as the 'Situation Analysis and


---
**Page 90**
---

According to the Situation Analysis and Recommendations for Improving Gender Inclusiveness and Participation in Papua New Guinea’s National REDD+ Strategy and Policies’ completed in 2017 with UNDP’s support, and priority strategies for women’s participation in sustainable agriculture and agroforestry practices, as defined in PNG’s NDC:

- **Male-Dominated Decision-Making Structures:** Traditional social norms often prioritize male decision-making, marginalizing women's voices in key decisions and reducing their access to REDD+ policies. This gender imbalance is further exacerbated by the lack of women’s representation in leadership roles.

- **Cultural Gender Roles:** Cultural perceptions of gender roles in PNG often restrict women’s participation. These cultural barriers can lead to a lack of awareness about rights, responsibilities, and opportunities for women, which can lead to the exclusion of their unique insights and contributions to resource management and environmental decision-making processes.

- **Lack of Access to Education and Information:** Barriers to education, especially in rural areas, impede women’s ability to participate effectively. The lack of access to information further exacerbates this issue, as many women are not aware of their rights or the mechanisms through which they can advocate for them.

- **Insufficient Support Systems:** Existing institutional frameworks often lack the necessary support mechanisms and resources to promote gender equality in REDD+ processes. This includes limited funding for initiatives that promote gender inclusion within REDD+ processes.

- **Gender-Specific Barriers:** Gender-specific barriers, such as cultural norms and practices, can hinder women’s participation in REDD+ activities. These barriers are often rooted in traditional social structures and can be difficult to address without a comprehensive approach.

As identified in the Gender Assessment and the proposed ESF for the project (section E.1.2 above), despite the recognition of a diversity of rights for women and gender equality anchored in PNG’s legal framework, there is still a lack of awareness about these rights among women and limited participation in decision-making processes, persist in the forestry and agricultural sectors.

Women's participation has historically been marginalized rather than decriminalized. Structural and cultural barriers persisting from traditional governance and negative gender norms, limited education opportunities for women, support, and access to resources continue to hinder women’s effective gender-in REDD+ readiness initiatives in benefit-sharing mechanisms, policy frameworks, and engagement strategies.

Moreover, as identified in the GAP and the EIA, given the high-level nature of PNG’s REDD+ strategy at the time when results were achieved, no specific SBARs/MEAs had not been considered, nor triggered, to ensure that women are effectively engaged in the design and implementation of the project. During the project inception, including the consideration of results of stakeholders’ consultations undertaken in preparation of this RFP, consistent with all applicable safeguards and the full scope of the ESF (see E.1.2).

Accordingly, the Project is committed to designing and implementing on-the-ground activities to be financed with the RFP funds that will ensure women’s meaningful engagement in REDD+ decision-making processes and GSV, in alignment with international frameworks on gender equality and women and girls’ empowerment and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The Project will also adopt a gender-sensitive approach towards SEV and GSV, ensuring compliance with both the GDP’s Gender Policy and FAD’s dedicated Environmental and Standard B (on gender equality and prevention of gender-based violence.

Consistent with the GDP’s Terms of Reference for the REDD+ pilot programs, the proposed RFP is expected to be aligned with the GDP’s Gender Policy 2019, and FAD’s FGS (2022), and its declaration on Environmental and Standard B (Gender equality and prevention of gender-based violence). ### E.A. Interim policy on prohibited practices

#### F.T. For the period of the achieved results

During the period of the achieved results, 2014-2016, the most relevant national policy addressing corruption and other prohibited practices was the National Anti-Corruption Strategy 2015-2020.

The PNG National Anti-Corruption Strategy 2015-2020** was a detailed and comprehensive framework document that set out the strategies for reducing levels of corruption in all sectors of the country. Below are the key points designed to provide a clearer understanding of the strategy:

* **Vision and Goals:** Establishing a Culture of Accountability and Transparency. The strategy aimed to foster an environment where government actions are transparent, and officials are held accountable for their decisions and actions.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Green Climate Fund
### Page 8 of 9

#### Strengthening Public Confidence:
By effectively addressing corruption, the strategy is to restore and enhance the trust that citizens place in government institutions, thus promoting a sense of belief in the integrity of public service.

#### Aligning Anti-Corruption Efforts with Development Goals:
The strategy intends to ensure that anti-corruption efforts complement and support the broader socio-economic development objectives laid out by Papua New Guinea, reinforcing the nation's commitment to progress and stability.

## 2. Key Objectives:

- **Prevention:** This involves implementing proactive measures to reduce the likelihood of corrupt activities, including strengthening laws and regulations, improving transparency in public procurement processes, and enhancing oversight across both public entities and private enterprises.
- **Detection:** The strategy aims to develop and implement tools and mechanisms for identifying corrupt practices, which includes enhanced reporting systems, whistleblower protections, and rigorous audits.
- **Enforcement:** This involves increasing the penalties for corruption, including imposing strict penalties and consistently applying the law to deter future offenses.
- **Public Awareness:** The strategy seeks to raise awareness among citizens about the dangers and consequences of corruption, aiming to empower citizens to recognize and report corrupt practices.
- **Capacity Building:** This includes strengthening institutions responsible for preventing and combating corruption, including law enforcement agencies and the judiciary, through training, resources, and institutional frameworks.

## 3. Strategic Pillars:

### Institutional Reforms:
The strategy called for the establishment or reinforcement of key institutions like the Anti-Corruption Commission (ACC) to ensure that public funds are managed and administered effectively.
- Legislative Reforms: The strategy aimed to improve existing anti-corruption laws and introduce new legislation that would better define and address various forms of corruption, closing loopholes that allowed corrupt practices to flourish.

### Transparency Initiatives:
The strategy sought to promote transparency by improving public access to government information through the establishment of a national portal for open data.
- Monitoring and Evaluation: Regular monitoring and evaluation were critical. This involved regular assessments of the effectiveness of anti-corruption initiatives to ensure that problems could be managed and corrected as needed.

## 4. Implementation Mechanisms:

### Multi-Stakeholder Collaboration:
The successful execution of the strategy relied on cooperative efforts between the government, civil society, and international partners.
- Action Plan Development: A detailed action plan was created to outline specific roles, timelines, and resources required for each phase of implementation.

### Reporting and Reviews:
To ensure transparency and accountability, the strategy included provisions for regular reporting on progress made and reviews to assess the outcomes of the anti-corruption initiatives.

## 5. Challenges Addressed:

- **Political Interference:** The strategy acknowledged the significant challenge of political interference, which often undermined anti-corruption efforts.
- **Limited Resources:** This involved the necessity of raising awareness among the populace regarding anti-corruption laws and encouraging active citizen engagement in reporting and combating corruption.
- **Weak Enforcement:** The strategy confronted the challenges posed by limited resources within law enforcement agencies, as well as a lack of capacity to act against corruption effectively.

## 6. Success Indicators:

E.4.2 The use of processes

The Accreditation Master Agreement between the GCF and FAO, signed in June 2018, established the principles for the application of the Accountability Framework in Chapter 9, specifications about the Standard of Care, through which GCF is committed:

* To be aware that all activities are subject to GCF Policies;
* Take appropriate measures to ensure that GCF Processes are used for the purposes for which they were provided and authorized;
* Apply the principles and standards of anti-corruption, fraud, financial sanctions and embargoes and comply with the relevant laws and regulations; and
* Aware of the Fund about issues related to the previous points.

FAO, as an ECA in the GCF, will set its policies, rules, and regulations for the management of funds is strictly applied. The following are the main principles that must be implemented as they are implemented in line with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices. These policies will be applied in all activities implemented by FAO.

Implementing these principles requires a comprehensive approach to prevent fraud and other corrupt practices, including monitoring and evaluating activities for transparent, efficient and adequate management of resources in line with the Fund's rules and regulations as well as international best practices and relevant conventions. In addition, the country has the mechanisms described in the previous section that guarantee an effective fight against corruption.

In this regard, FAO has the Policy Against Fraud and Other Corrupt Practices, updated in July 2014, designed to prevent and detect fraud and other corrupt activities.

FAO is committed to ensuring that all operations are free from fraud and other corrupt practices for the intended purpose, ensuring that all operations are free from fraud and other corrupt practices, and holding itself accountable to donors and demonstrating its commitment to preventing and combating corruption, which involves a comprehensive approach to prevent, identify, and combat these practices.

The Policy Against Fraud and Other Corrupt Practices is structured in the following key steps:

### REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 91 OF 9)

**1. Fraud Risk Management:** Fraud risks must be identified and mitigated across various areas such as human resources, procurement, inventory, and asset management. Risk management focal points are designated at FAO Country Offices to ensure that fraud risk mitigation strategies are in place.

**2. Training and Awareness:** FAO provides mandatory training on fraud for all staff to boost awareness of risks and ensure compliance with relevant procedures.

**3. Roles and Responsibilities:**

- **FAO Staff:** Adhere to the highest standards of integrity and efficiency, and be obligated to report any suspicion of fraud, corruption, or misuse of resources.
  - **Managers:** Play a crucial role in promoting integrity, enforcing the zero-tolerance policy, implementing anti-fraud and anti-corruption measures, and ensuring compliance with relevant laws and regulations.
- **Specific Units/Headquarters:** Heads of units are responsible for identifying fraud risks and implementing measures to mitigate them.
- **Office of the Director General (ODG):** Oversees risk management for the Organization and provides guidance and training on anti-fraud and anti-corruption measures.
- **Office of the Inspector General (OIG):** Conducts internal audits and investigations of potential misconduct, corruption, or fraud within FAO.

**4. Vendors, Service Providers, and Operational Partners:** FAO must ensure that their operations align with UN principles, including ethical conduct regarding fraud, corruption, conflicts of interest, and other related standards.

**5. Reporting Fraud:** All FAO personnel are obligated to report any potential fraud or corrupt practices. Reports can be made through the organization's internal reporting system, whistle-blower hotline, web form, or email, with protections under the Whistleblower Protection Policy to prevent retaliation.

The WBP will also cooperate with national authorities to investigate allegations of fraud or corruption, to the extent permitted by applicable laws and regulations.

During project implementation, FAO, as AE, will ensure close monitoring and supervision through its country, sub-regional, regional, and headquarters offices. FAO will ensure that all activities are implemented in full compliance with the signed project agreement.

FAO will establish appropriate fiduciary management and control measures to ensure that materials of a non-confidential nature are not used for purposes other than those intended by FAO (e.g., sold or misused for unauthorized, improper, or illicit purposes) based on its institutional and project-level governance policies. These include the Whistleblower Protection Policy (Admin circular 2014/427), the Whistleblower Protection Policy (Admin circular 2015/1103) and others listed in the FAO Guidelines for Compliance Reviews Following Complaints Related to the Organization's Internal Administrative Rules and Regulations.

In addition, OIG has the mandate to independently review any complaints about non-compliance with FAO’s internal administrative rules and regulations at all levels of the organization. This is detailed in the FAO Guidelines for Compliance Reviews Following Complaints Related to the Organization’s Internal Administrative Rules and Regulations (Admin circular 2015/1103). The OIG will communicate its findings to OIG by mail, courier, email or fax, directly or via any office FAO. The OIG also investigates complaints about non-compliance with FAO’s internal administrative rules and regulations of the Organization. It conducts investigations and inspections in compliance with the FAO Guidelines for Internal Administrative Rules and Regulations.

In summary, FAO has a strong and structured approach to prevent and combat fraud and corruption, ensuring integrity in the management of its programmes and accountability to stakeholders.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 12 OF 30)

The PNG highlands for thousands of years, with complex societies based on kinship, subsistence agriculture, and customary land tenure. PNG's indigenous inhabitants, organized under traditional villages and communities, have inhabited the country since at least 45,000 years ago. The majority live in many along the coastal regions and islands; these groups are related to other Austronesian-speaking communities across the Pacific region. Indigenous peoples also inhabit the mountainous highlands and interior regions; these groups speak languages from various indigenous language families. Within each of these groups there are hundreds of distinct tribes and clans, each with their own language, social structures, and traditions.

Indigenous Peoples in PNG have inhabited the region for over 50,000 years. The island's early inhabitants developed a complex society based on kinship, subsistence agriculture, and customary land tenure (PNG Heritage site, contains evidence of one of the world’s earliest farming systems). Traditional societies were organized under traditional villages and communities, which are related to other Austronesian-speaking communities across the Pacific region. Indigenous peoples also inhabit the mountainous highlands and interior regions; these groups speak languages from various indigenous language families.

During the colonial period, when PNG was ruled by Germany, Britain, and later Australia, indigenous ethnic groups were displaced from their lands and forced into labor camps or settlements. Many of them lost access to the land remains. While there were impacts associated with the introduction of Christianity, cash subsistence agriculture, and other modern technologies, these have not been as significant as those caused by the most part retained their traditional ways of life and traditional practices, regardless of whether they adopt a Christian or animist belief system.

PNG's customary landowners and communities account for over 70% of the people living in the rural areas of PNG. These people live primarily subsistence to semi-subsistence lifestyles and somewhere between 80-90% of their food is produced on their own land. The vast majority of the land owned by these people in PNG remains under customary tenure, meaning it is owned collectively, with decisions over the use of that land made through a consensus process involving all members of the community.

PNG's customary landowners and communities account for over 70% of the people living in the rural areas of PNG. These people live primarily subsistence to semi-subsistence lifestyles and somewhere between 80-90% of their food is produced on their own land. The vast majority of the land owned by these people in PNG remains under customary tenure, meaning it is owned collectively, with decisions over the use of that land made through a consensus process involving all members of the community.

The Land Act provides for three types of land tenure: (a) freehold; (b) leasehold; and (c) alienated or —more often referred to as Papuan New Guinean citizens—to equal opportunities to participate and benefit from development processes in the country, underlining the rich ethnic and cultural diversity that exists within PNG. The Constitution recognizes traditional villages and communities as viable units of Papua New Guinean society, and provides for the protection of customary land tenure systems of PNG and distinguishes between customary proprietary or possessory rights over the land.

The Land Act defines the citizens that are eligible to hold or lease land, including customary landowners, as follows: (a) a person who is a member of a traditional village or community; (b) a person who is a member of a clan group; and/or (c) a customary local group or community.

In consultation with indigenous communities in PNG, PNG's REDD+ Strategy refers to communities as a broad stakeholder category that would encompass all Papua New Guineans, and refers to landholders as the primary stakeholders. The Land Act recognizes three types of tenure: freehold, leasehold, and ownership rights recognized in the Land Act. On the other hand, when interpreting international language for treaty purposes, PNG has adopted a more narrow definition of customary landowner, which is defined by the Land Act. ### Legal framework: Governance and Rights of customary landowners and local communities

At the national level, PNG's laws and policies have always sought to preserve the customary landowners and communities' rights over their lands. This is reflected in the National Land Policy 1987 which provides for implementation of policy, laws and regulations relating to the registration of lands traditionally occupied by customary landowners and communities.

The Organic Law No.20/2004 (the National Government Act) is one of the main legal instruments that have direct bearing on this aspect of maintaining culture in PNG at the subnational level of government or community level. The law provides for the establishment of a National Land Commission to oversee land administration and resolution of disputes.

The National Museum & Art Gallery was established to preserve, protect and promote PNG's cultural heritage including its intangible aspects as well. In terms of protecting the traditional/structural sites of significance, the NMCAG Act 2015 provides for the establishment of a National Cultural Heritage Commission which remains, or the cultural or spiritual aspects of a specific site through the establishment of protected areas and/or national parks.

National laws and regulations for accessing customary land acquisition are quite comprehensive and set out the process that must be followed. The law also sets out the penalties for those who willfully cause any adverse impact as part of this process. Customary law is also recognized and used to resolve disputes arising from access to land. This is reflected in the National Land Commission Act 1987 which provides for magistrates as part of the National Judicial system in PNG (refer to in the previous section). In addition to these laws, customary practices are also respected by the State through its policies such as the Agriculture Policy 2015 which places a great emphasis on the need for customary landowners and communities to be protected and their rights to be upheld.

Policy and legal framework, and overall context on existing GIM procedures, mechanisms and customary practices in PNG are discussed further in section 3.1 above. The policy and legal framework is also recognized by customary landowners and communities, as examined in section 3.1 above, ensuring compliance with the GCF's policies and guidelines.

The Registry of LGIs is a national public registry, mandatory for all customary land which is subject to some form of commercial and business lease or natural resource development project. An LGI is established via the Land Contract Act 2007, the National Environment Management Act 1993 (as amended by the Environmental Act 1991**, the Environment Act 2000***), the Climate Change Management Act 2015**** and within the environment of the National Land Commission Act 1987.

The traditional customs and practices of the people of PNG are also recognized within Schedule 2.1.1 of the Constitution of Papua New Guinea, which provides that customary landowners and communities to not be unjustly deprived of their land and the need to respect that right especially in light of relevant international instruments such as the UNDRIP. The Act further provides for a process by which State development purposes which requires the use of FPIC as part of compulsory acquisition. The process of FPIC is also recognized and respected in Acts of Parliament such as the Forestry Act 1993** or the Oil & Gas

## Autonomy and self-determination

Autonomy and self-determination of customary landowners and communities underpin participatory decision-making processes, including those related to REDD-plus activities such as carbon stock assessment, conservation, protection, management and control. These elements are captured in the NRS objectives that reflect the specific needs and aspirations of the landowners and communities.

### A. Control and protection

A. Control and protection of customary lands and resources includes traditionally led management and monitoring practices.

### B. Sustainable management

B. Sustainable management of natural resources for the promotion of food security and income generation as well as conservation, restoration and sustainable land use; and

### C. Capacity building

C. Capacity building for customary landowners and communities as well as institutional strengthening of provincial and district level institutions.

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## E.E. Monitoring and evaluation

Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with FAO policies. The project inception phase will include the formation of a Theory of Change describing the causal relationship between our interventions and the expected results, as well as the indicators that will measure how much the change will happen. FAO will ensure a well-designed, operational, and effective impact monitoring and targeting system to monitor progress against the theory of change and to provide information on overall causes of the project. The monitoring system should be designed to understand the efficacy and targets of the project interventions in terms of their contribution to the achievement of the project’s objectives.

The monitoring will be based on a combination of quantitative and qualitative data, including information, data and lessons that can be used to feed back into the project implementation and planning components. The monitoring system should also include an evaluation component.

FAO will perform monitoring and reporting throughout the reporting period. FAO has a country’s presence and capacity to perform these tasks. FAO will implement the tools and methods to facilitate monitoring of the project, including the use of the Performance Management Framework (PMF) for continuous and standardized monitoring. The PMF will use the tools, including the work platforms, to enable the Project Manager to monitor progress against the Theory of Change and to provide feedback towards achieving results set out in the Performance Management Framework.

The monitoring system should be designed to understand the efficacy and targets of the project interventions in terms of their contribution to the achievement of the project’s objectives as well as the action plans for gender, customary landowners and communities, biodiversity, and the social and environmental components.

The day-to-day project monitoring and implementation responsibility rests on a nationally recruited Project Manager, with the support of the rest of the national and international staff, who will lead the PMLE. She will not report directly to the FAO Country Director but will be supervised by the M&E Specialist at the Unit. The M&E Specialist will coordinate the annual work plan to ensure the effective implementation of the project monitoring system. The Project Manager will be responsible for ensuring that all necessary difficulties during implementation, including the M&E plan to that appropriate and corrective measures can be adopted in a timely manner.

The PMLE will be responsible for providing regular updates on progress against the Theory of Change and accountability when monitoring and reporting project results. FAO will support the Project Manager as needed with technical assistance and training. The Project Manager will also receive ongoing troubleshooting support will be provided by FAO as needed. The CNGA, PINGA, CEPA, other related institutions and communities will provide feedback on progress against the Theory of Change to the M&E.

A project inception workshop will be implemented to: a) agree on the project theory of change; b) build a common understanding of the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that might influence implementation; c) discuss the roles and responsibilities of all parties involved, including the Project Manager reporting and coordinating with FAO; d) review and discuss the results framework and discuss monitoring, reporting requirements, e) plan and scheduling EAS meetings, and f) finalize the first year work plan. The Final Inception Report will be approved by the Board of Evaluation (BOE).

The PM and FAO M&E Specialist will prepare a Project Report for submission of implementation. The PM and the M&E Specialist will ensure that all indicators in the results framework are monitored annually. The Annual reports will be due to GFO 60 days after the end of each year. The final project annual report and the long-term evaluation report will be due to GFO 120 days after the end of the project term and final evaluations will be undertaken under the responsibility of FAO Office of Evaluation (OEO).

In summary, monitoring and evaluation at the project level will be carried out in accordance with FAO’s policies and systems and will not undermine existing or the EAS evaluation process across the project life cycle.

## Project Cycle establishes institutional standards and procedures for project management, improving accountability and quality through results-based management principles, while improving the strategic focus of projects.

The Project Cycle sets corporate standards and procedures, including quality assurance criteria for phases 1 to 3 of all F&A projects. These criteria are relevance, feasibility, and sustainability, which are complemented by the project's own indicators and measures to monitor the implementation of the project (Initial Installation, Annual Project Report, Mid-term and Final Evaluation).

The project team and the FAO Country Office will carry out the M&E in accordance with F&A procedures.

**Project Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Framework:** The M&E framework is a tool that includes additional information, such as the corresponding meaning of terms. The M&E framework is an essential element for projects under the GCF. It consists of three phases: initial installation, mid-term evaluation, and final evaluation. This information will be available online.

The following table provides an overview of the elements of this plan. The project M&E plan will be presented and finalized after an adjustment of the indicators, means of verification, and a full definition of the M&E components by the project team staff.

## Initial Installation

The Project Inception Workshop will be held within the first 3 months of the project start, involving those who are part of the project organization structure: FAO, GCF, implementing partner, national authorities, FAO regional technical advisor and other key stakeholders. The Inception Workshop is crucial to building ownership of the project results and planning the first-year annual work plan.

## Annual Report

This important report is prepared by the Project Technical Advisor, consolidated by the Project Manager, reviewed by the REDD+ TBC and finally approved by the Project Board to monitor progress made since the previous reporting period.

The format and content of the annual report will be adjusted based on the simplified reporting regime established for this project by the GCF.

## Mid-term Evaluation

The project will undergo an independent mid-term evaluation at the mid-point of project implementation. The mid-term evaluation will determine progress towards the achievement of outcomes and will suggest corrective actions to ensure that the project is on track. It will also highlight issues regarding decisions and actions, and present initial lessons learned about project design, implementation and management.

## Final Evaluation

An independent Final Evaluation will be launched within six months before the project's completion date. M&E. If it can be demonstrated that the project has achieved its objectives, outcomes, outputs, lessons learned, problems met and areas where results may not have been fully achieved, further steps that may need to be taken to ensure the sustainability and replicability of the project’s results will be recommended.

## CLIMATE CHANGE CARBON MARKET REGULATION

Climate Change Carbon Market regulations are currently before the Governor General for endorsement and will be published in the submission to this proposal. The regulation includes relevant elements, such as:

- It sets out the specifics for issuance of permits for entities entering into carbon projects.
- It provides a framework for determining carbon rights via a system of registration that will help to track and monitor as well as provide evidence of ownership of any claims made or in the event of any competing claims that may arise.

It also gives effect to the CCM amendments of 2023 so far as it relates to the framework for Benefit sharing from the sale and transfer of carbon credits including:

As it concerns the functions of the Board, Section 2 of the CCA amendments (n.17/2023) repeals section 13 of the CCMA (2015, not repealed) and restricts the scope that the Board (a) administers ERIs and the related Result-Based Payments (RBP), (b) provides oversight to the implementation of the GCF International Climate Change Development Program from the Minister back to the Board. *This has been brought into effect by the CCMA amendments (n.17/2023) and section 9 of the CCA Amendments (n.17/2023)*.

To this effect, the Board in consultation with the Minister responsible for forestry matters approves a benefit sharing agreement that is consistent with the provisions of the CCMA (2015), the CCA amendments (n.20/2023) and the GCF International Climate Change Development Program.

In the context of the above considerations and also SSR, the competent authority has to demonstrate its capacity to receive and administer Result-Based Payments (RBPs), in accordance with the legal framework for Benefit sharing from the sale and transfer of carbon credits including:

- The full respect of rights of parties involved in ERI activities (customary landowners and customary rights, and beneficiaries rights).
- Avoiding any potential claims on the results to be paid by the GCF.

Section 3 of the CCA Amendments (n.20/2023) repeals Section 3 of the principal Act and defines “option rights” as legal claims, verified through social and land mapping, on the benefit streams from carbon sinks. Further to this, Section 34A stipulates that carbon rights and ownership can be defined and regulated under customary law and/or other laws in accordance with (a) national guidelines on how carbon rights are defined and regulated; and (b) institutional arrangements, consistent with the provisions of the CCMA (2015), as amended.

In Papua New Guinea, customary law is fully recognized by the Constitution of 1975, and under customary law, landowners have the right to manage their land in accordance with their traditional practices. Section 46 of the Forestry Act (1991) states that “the rights of customary owners of forest resources are to be fully respected” and that they should be involved in all aspects of forestry activities.

Section 57 of the CCA Amendments n.20/23 amendments section 90 include rights and benefit sharing within all climate change project agreements entered into with landowners. This ensures that they have the rights to the benefits from their land, as well as the ability to participate in decision-making processes (see Section 1(3) of the Organic Law on Provincial Governments and Local-level Governments (n.29/1998), a

# REDD-plus RFB FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE N° OF 9

### Section 57 (CMA) 25/02/21 further Amends Section 90 of the Principal Act and reiterates that: "(1) A climate change related project agreement shall (b) explain and define land holder, rights, and benefits under the REDD+ Project Agreement, including any additional rights or benefits prescribed under Part IX, in consideration for the rights granted; and (c) be accompanied by a stakeholder engagement plan as described in Section 57 of the Principal Act."

To that effect, the benefit-sharing plan will specify criteria and modalities to ensure that REDD+ Results-Based Payments are distributed through appropriate mechanisms and that beneficiaries: rights, including those holding carbon rights, will be included.

In this regard, PNG has recently adopted a set of Guidelines on benefit-sharing and distribution (2021) to enable the DFO to implement the Benefit Sharing Plan (BSP). The BSP is intended to ensure that all revenues from any or incentive funds, donor money and other funding derived from national and international climate finance mechanisms are distributed in accordance with the guidelines. These include the National REDD+ Strategy (2015), the National REDD+ Strategy 2017-2027 and the REDD+ Finance and Investment Framework.

Beneficiaries may include but are not limited to, communities, civil society, and the private sector, including any nested REDD+ projects, while national and sub-national governments may also retain a certain portion of payments as part of their revenue sharing arrangements with local communities.

Eligibility criteria for beneficiaries should be clearly defined in the Benefit Sharing Plan (BSP), avoiding excluding relevant or vulnerable groups. These include:

- Primary beneficiaries who play a direct role in reducing deforestation and degradation on the ground, perform vital monitoring and reporting functions and have a regulatory or legal right to the land, forests or emissions.

- Secondary beneficiaries who have important roles in the implementation of REDD+, but their roles and impacts are largely indirect. To ensure that BSPs can be implemented effectively, practical approaches should be taken to include these groups as well. This may involve engaging with representatives of key stakeholders in Benefit Sharing Agreements (BSA). Due consideration must also be given to the recognition of rights, responsibilities, and distribution of benefits to REDD+ beneficiaries in a timely manner, reflected in the BSA.

The development of a sound, inclusive and participatory BSP will then avoid excluding vulnerable groups and other potential beneficiaries from the programme and thus any potential claim on the CDM results to be paid.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP273

Proposal name: Papua New Guinea REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014 - 2016

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Papua New Guinea

Project size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Project background

At its eighteenth meeting (B.18), the Board of the GCF decided to allocate up to USD 500 million to the request for proposals for the REDD+ Results-Based Payments Pilot Programme (RBP) in Papua New Guinea, which was selected as a country of reference for the REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme and the corresponding draft scorecard, which determines the amount of funding that will be available. The financial envelope allocated to apply to the REDD+ results submitted to GCF for payment. However, the financial envelope exhausted in 2020, with four concept notes (from the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, Papua New Guinea, Solomon Islands and Timor-Leste) still awaiting funding from the funds.

At B.18, the Board decided on an exceptional basis to allow their four legacy REDD+ RBP concept notes to be considered as eligible for funding under the REDD+ Results-Based Payments Pilot Programme during the relevant review period if diligence to be carried out by the Secretariat for a funding proposal.

The Government of Papua New Guinea’s REDD+ plus results for the period 2014-2016, which calculates that a volume of around 16.9 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂ eq) in emission reductions (ERs) from reducing emissions from deforestation have been offered to GCF under the REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme (decision B.18/07). The presented results, which comply with United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) standards and the TGR, have been submitted for consideration by the Board.

Papua New Guinea will use the proceeds from the RBP to support the national and targeted provinces in implementing the National REDD+ Strategy 2017-2027 (NRS). This implementation will focus on key action areas, including strengthened land planning, law enforcement, forest protection, and the promotion of inclusive sustainable development.

Component 1: Increased institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial governments in PNG and the targeted provinces;

Component 2: Strengthening livelihoods and sustainable livelihoods of smallholders and customary landowning groups in the targeted provinces.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 2.1 Impact potential Scale: High
The project is fully aligned with the NRS and offers a unique opportunity for PNG to continue its investments and innovations in the forest sector through the proposed use of proceeds from REDD+ funding. The project seeks to address the challenges facing both Forest and Land-use sectors by prioritizing low-emission development. It seeks to reshape land use and contribute towards achieving national development goals, including PNG's goal of achieving lower emissions, stronger economic growth, and improved community livelihoods.

The proposal addresses drivers of forest loss and degradation through coordinated efforts across multiple government policy and private stakeholders. It encourages sustainable resource use, integrating REDD+ into decision-making processes to align economic growth with environmental and climate goals.


### 2.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High
The project aims to address the challenges of a rapidly growing rural population and pressure on land resources by promoting sustainable economic development in targeted provinces. The project will support the Government's efforts to reduce deforestation and promoting small-scale forestry to mitigate the effects of agricultural expansion while unlocking opportunities for poverty reduction.

Direct activities will initially focus on six provinces. Successful outcomes will serve as replicable models for other regions, supported by capacity-building, policy integration, economic diversification, and improved governance.


The project aims to reduce deforestation, enhance agricultural productivity, and improve livelihoods in targeted provinces through sustainable land-use intensification by addressing environmental and socioeconomic challenges, ensuring sustainable growth, and preserving Papua New Guinea's environmental heritage for future generations.


---
**Page 110**
---

# Sustainable development potential

The project aims to tackle the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation while offering significant environmental, social, economic benefits. It adopts a holistic approach to sustainable development by addressing environmental degradation, social inequalities, and poverty reduction through integrated policies and governance scalability. This contributes to preserving Papua New Guinea's environmental assets, supporting biodiversity conservation, and enhancing livelihoods.

The project strengthens environmental governance, aligns land use planning with conservation principles, and enhances the enforcement of environmental regulations. It supports community-based forest management by providing training in sustainable land-use planning, conducting environmental impact assessments into development plans, and promoting community stewardship of forests.

Empowering local communities and customary landowners is central, fostering local ownership and participation in sustainable land management. The project prioritizes gender equity in land use decisions and promotes the integration of agroforestry systems to enhance agricultural systems, thereby improving food security and diversifying economic opportunities, supported by strengthened governance structures.

The project creates sustainable economic opportunities aligned with international standards, supporting local communities and fostering sustainable agriculture. It enhances productivity through improved access to markets and technology, as well as providing entrepreneurial opportunities. The initiative supports institutional coordination, fostering partnerships for a transformational, low-emission economy model that benefits all sectors of society.


## Needs of the recipient

Papua New Guinea has experienced rapid economic growth in the twenty-first century, primarily driven by foreign investment. However, this growth has mainly benefited urban areas and industrialized communities, leaving challenges in achieving equitable development and environmental protection in more rural areas. Despite the economic expansion, Papua New Guinea has faced significant challenges such as high poverty rates, limited access to basic services, and declining foreign exchange reserves, leading to reduced government revenues and economic instability. These factors have limited the government's ability to channel further investments into the forest sectors.


## Country ownership

Papua New Guinea has emerged as a global leader in REDD+, showcasing a strong commitment to combating climate change. This effort is central to its nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement, supported by the comprehensive NRS, which was developed through extensive consultations.

The Climate Change and Development Authority led the NDS development, engaging diverse stakeholders across sectors. The National REDD+ Implementation Facilitation was facilitated by the National Climate Change Board, the National REDD+ Steering Committee, and its technical working committees, ensuring a unified approach to climate action.


## Efficiency and effectiveness

The proceeds will be strategically invested to support the forest sector and increase sustainability of agriculture thereby reducing the pressure on forests. The project aims to

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### III. Environmental and social Safeguards

##### 3.1 Environmental and Social Safeguards

25 The proposal includes an environmental and social assessment (ESA) that retractsively examines the effectiveness of existing safeguards applied by Papua New Guinea during the 2014-2016 results period. These PAMS primarily included enabling activities, institutional reforms, and capacity building. The ESA is aligned with the Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) component of the Enhanced Partnership for Forests and Landscapes (EPFL), the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility and the United Nations Collaborative Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation in Developing Countries (UN-REDD). The ESA categorizes PAMS into three groups: readiness, enabling, and direct measures. It further assesses four illustrative PAMS1 selected for their policy significance and relevance to forest governance and the environment.

26 Recognizing that EUs during the assessed period are not attributable to discrete physical interventions, the assessment does not include a results matrix with applicable safeguards. It provides a structured review of enabling actions against the FAO's Framework for Environmental Social Management (FSM), GCF's interim environmental and social safeguard policy, and other relevant international standards. The ESA includes annexes.

27 The alignment of the REDD+ readiness activities and enabling activities with applicable standards and safeguards. However, due to the indirect nature of the results and lack of attribution to specific physical interventions, the assessment relies primarily on the credibility of project documentation and stakeholder interviews. The ESA acknowledges key limitations during the results period, including limited documentation of project implementation, a lack of evidence for effective grievance redress, and the absence of fully operational stakeholder engagement protocols. Genders considerations are not explicitly addressed in the PAMS, although they were considered through coverage and quality varied by activity and location. While some participatory activities were


---
**Page 112**
---

undertaken during REDD+ readiness, the depth and inclusiveness of these engagements varied across PAMs and regions.

26\. The ESMF demonstrates a reasonable level of due diligence for the period assessed, consistent with expectations for REDD+ proposals as outlined in the REEDS+ RBP Pilot Programme TOR. While the enabling nature of the activities and the absence of physical interventions are acknowledged, there is no evidence that these activities have been implemented at the community-level; these limitations are explicitly acknowledged in the EIA. The AE has committed to ensuring that safeguards will be integrated into the project design through engagement mechanisms, through safeguards instruments to be developed during the project inception phase, including the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and associated instruments.

27\. The proposal is accompanied by an environmental and social management framework (ESMF), which provides the policy and procedures for handling environmental and social risks of the project. The ESMF was prepared in accordance with the requirements set out in the REEDS+ RBP Pilot Programme TOR. The ESMF is proposed as a strategic and high-level instrument that will guide the development of a comprehensive suite of safeguard instruments, including the ESMP, Community Engagement Plan, Stakeholder Engagement Plan, Benefit-Sharing Plan, and risk screening tools. These instruments are expected to be developed during the project inception phase and will inform any on-the-ground activities.

28\. The proposal is classified as category B in the ESMF, with reference standards being the IFC Performance Standards and social safeguard(s) and the FAO’s environmental and social standards. Oversight of safeguards implementation will be carried out by FAO in coordination with PNG’s Climate Change Division (CCD). Implementation oversight will also be supported through a Project Management Unit (PMU). The PMU is expected to include safeguards expertise, and the Alliances for REDD+ project team will engage with implementing entities and local stakeholders as part of safeguards roll-out during inception.

29\. The ESMF outlines procedural commitments for screening, risk categorization, stakeholder engagement, and monitoring. It also includes a commitment to respect free, prior and informed consent, and gender-responsive planning. It also describes the development of a project-level grievance mechanism (GLGM) that will be implemented in-country; this includes escalation to the FAO Office of the Inspector General. The GLGM will include culturally appropriate and survivor-centered procedures to address Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH), as well as other forms of abuse and harassment, including through campaigns/campaigns. The ESMT commits to transparent disclosure and public access to safeguards information in accordance with the REEDS+ RBP Pilot Programme TOR, using appropriate formats and languages. The ESMF and associated instruments are expected to incorporate monitoring mechanisms for safeguard implementation during project design, implementation, interventions and sites have not yet been finalized, the implementation of safeguards remains contingent on the finalization of these elements.

30\. The ESMF is consistent with the GCF’s TOR for REEDS+ BIP proposals, particularly regarding the expectation of proactively applying safeguards use of processes. It includes a non-exhaustive list of safeguard risks and their mitigation measures, including involuntary resettlement, significant adverse impacts on critical habitats, or unresolved legacy risks such as those related to land tenure and customary rights. The ESMF is being used during the inception phase and used as assess relevant on the ground activities. The implementation of these safeguards will require close monitoring during the project inception and delivery phases.

31\. GCF funding for REDD+ projects must be consistent with the requirements of the Indigenous Peoples Policy. The AE has developed a Customary Landholders and Communities Engagement Plan (CLCEP) to ensure that the rights, interests, and needs of Indigenous Peoples were undertaken during the ex-ante stage though detailed and disaggregated information of the involvement of Indigenous Peoples was not made available. At


---
**Page 113**
---

The en poste phase, consultations with Indigenous Peoples are planned to be undertaken prior to the implementation of any activity secure FPCs where activities are implemented in Indigenous Territories. The AE has been informed that these will be conducted within the framework of the CLIP, and participatory approaches are also foreseen to encourage a strong engagement of Indigenous Peoples in the project. For the en poste stage, the AE has been requested to provide information on how it intends to ensure that Indigenous Peoples who are involved and impacted by the project, share details on the consultations that will be undertaken prior to implementation of any activity secure FPCs where activities are implemented in Indigenous Territories as a condition for implementation. The AE has also been informed of the need to submit a gender action plan (GAP) in accordance with the Gender Policy and where specific project areas will be chosen. In line with the IP Policy, the AE has excluded undertaking activities that may have adverse impacts on Indigenous Peoples. However, it is strongly recommended the AE to include a consultant with expertise on Indigenous Peoples issues in the team for the implementation phase. In line with their roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage of the project. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage.

### 3.2 Gender policy

The AE has submitted a gender assessment and a gender action plan (GAP) in accordance with the Gender Policy and the REDD+ BPP Pilot Programme TIP. The gender assessment presents a detailed review of gender inequalities in Papua New Guinea, highlighting issues related to women’s access to land and resources. It also identifies elevated levels of gender-based violence and limited institutional support for survivors of violence. Women have limited decision-making power or access to benefits, with cultural norms further restricting participation.

The REDD+ BPP TIP requires the AE to develop a gender action plan (GAP) in line with REDD+ actions in Papua New Guinea, noting that while women rely heavily on forests for subsistence needs such as firewood, food, and medicinal plants, they have limited control over high-value resources. The gender assessment notes that while the level of participation by stakeholders consultations has improved over time, yet decision-making remains male-dominated due to persistent cultural norms. Women’s contributions are often undervalued in project planning considerations in benefit-sharing mechanisms are limited, with women’s contributions to resource management and conservation being under-represented in project design and implementation. Although PNG’s REDD+ safeguards recognize gender equality and the right to Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC), there is a need for further capacity building of women in addressing risks related to Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH). Stakeholder consultations are reported to have incorporated gender considerations, but disaggregated participation data on how communities are involved in project design and implementation are consistent with broader limitations noted in the EIA, including evolving processes for adequately engaging Indigenous Peoples.

The GAP identifies strategic areas of intervention to support the mainstreaming of gender equality during the implementation of activities listed through the use of outputs. The proposed actions include: (1) capacity building of women on climate change adaptation and support for sustainable livelihoods among customary landowners [Component 13]; both components are expected to be implemented in collaboration with the AE; (2) specific actions to promote inclusive decision-making, build women’s capacity in forest governance and sustainably manage forests [Component 14]. Specific actions include enhancing women’s participation in land use planning, supporting women-led


---
**Page 114**
---

### Risk

#### Accredited entity/executing entity capability

The Government of Papua New Guinea, through the Ministry of Environment, Conservation and Climate Change, has requested FAO's technical and overall assistance for the design and implementation of the project. The Government of Papua New Guinea also specifically requested that FAO act as the executing entity (EE) for the project. In response to the request, FAO will serve both as accredited entity (AE) and EE. FAO is one of the founding members of the REDD+ Partnership and has extensive experience in providing technical oversight in REDD+ and REED+B projects, including in previously approved GEF REDD+ pilot projects.

#### Programme-specific execution risks

**Risk of double payments:** Papua New Guinea is analyzing its participation in other jurisdictional payments for REDD+ results that are different from the GEF RFPs, and some initiatives have already been implemented. This will be done during a transition period of the other projects started after 2016, thus preventing any risk of double payments.

**Use of proceeds:** The proceeds will be strategically invested in activities outlined in the NSDP for the next five years to ensure that they are consistent with the essential interconnectiveness of the three REDD+ phases: readiness, implementation and REPs.

**Land tenure:** The Department of Lands & Physical Planning develops and implements national land use plans. FAO has provided technical assistance to Papua New Guinea by developing a comprehensive set of tools, guidelines, and software systems to support land tenure governance and strengthen institutional coordination capacities at national and provincial levels in Papua New Guinea.

**Disbursement plan:** GEF will disburse the GCF proceeds to the AE in single disbursals.

#### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The funding proposal will be implemented exclusively by FAO, acting as both AE and EE, in Papua New Guinea. FAO has assessed the risks of money laundering, terrorist financing, and prohibited financial flows associated with implementing REDD+ projects in countries operating in fragile governance environments and involving direct execution by the AE. There remain inherent risks related to potential misuse of funds, institutional capacity constraints, and challenges in ensuring consistent implementation of safeguards at the field level.
