# B.I. Forest reference emission level/Forest reference level (FREL/FRL)

## Criteria Score Remarks

The FREL/FRL was not consistent with the GHG inventory in the second national communication at the time of submission. Updated GHG inventories submitted along with both BUS have increased alignment with the FREL/FRL.

Table 5 in BURE mentions and explains the remaining inconsistencies, mainly:

- N2O and CH4 were included in the inventories for BIURZ, but not in the FREL/FRL/REDD+ results;
- Litter and soil organic carbon (SOC) were included in BIURZ, but not in the FREL/FRL/REDD+ results;
- Two additional differences relating to forest regrowth and fuelwood removal, which are currently relevant for the results on reducing GHG emissions from deforestation.

### (i) Is the FREL/FRL consistent with the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory, including the definition of forest used?

1 BURE: The annex to the 2017 technical assessment report (TAR) (dated 2018) states that "the methods used for the FREL/FRL are not consistent with the latest GHG inventory" and that "[t]he definitions and information used in the FREL/FRL differ from those used in the GHG inventory in the future" (see FCC/CTR/2017/PNG, page 41). Furthermore, the 2017 TAR highlights that the definition of 'forest' used in the FREL/FRL differs from the one used in the national inventory and for reporting under the REDD+.

BURE: Based on the TAR, the definitions and information used for the assessed FREL/FRL are overall consistent with those and for the results. However, a different post-deforestation removal correction was applied to avoid an overestimation of the results, leading to inconsistency in the assessed FREL/FRL (see para 16). In paragraph 18B, BURE further notes that the definition of 'forest' used in the FREL/FRL differs from those and for the results could be recalculated in the context of a future submission to the GCF under the REDD+RRP and noted that this is beyond the scope of the technical annex.

### (ii) Is the FREL/FRL based on historical data and is it equal to or below the average annual historical

REDD+ Environmental Excellence Standard of the Architecture for REDD+ Transactions Program.

The original modified FREL/FRL is based on the following historical data (reference period: 2001-2013):
• 2008–2018 projections using the results of a linear regression model (R² = 0.9475);
• FREL/FRL, including emissions from deforestation, forest degradation and forest carbon stock enhancement.
In the REDD+ context, the period 2014-2018 was determined as a continuation of the increasing deforestation rate, thus the original modified FREL/FRL values are not the average annual historical emissions.

However, in section 4 of BURL Papua New Guinea submitted a recalculated FREL/FRL for the purpose of participating in the GCF RFPs, using the historical average of the period 2009–2013 plus the adjustment of 6.2% of the C stock yearly over the five-year period, which is justified based on Papua New Guinea’s status as an HFLD country.

(ii) Is the FREL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in decision 12/C.P/17?
The 2017 TAR states that the FREL/FRL of Papua New Guinea is in overall accordance with the guidelines for submission of information on FREL/FRLs (see para. 38 of the 2017 TAR) and did not require any further clarification.

(iii) Are the data and information provided for the FREL/FRL transparent? Has information been provided to allow for independent verification?
Although Papua New Guinea has increased the transparency of data and information over time, and the new TARs and the TAR (2017) highlight the collaborative and open approach shown by the country in providing information on its FREL/FRL, there were still some areas where there was a lack of transparency that were significant enough to require raising them with the country. They noted that key information (e.g., specific data sets regarding forest plantations, losses and concessions, and other land uses) had not been provided for all relevant years. The TAR also highlighted that activity data for the FREL/FRL was submitted only after their requests for clarification.
Further to this, there were some areas where there appeared to be a lack of transparency in emission factors (EFAs) used for forest degradation and a lack of transparency on some of the employed methods.

(iv) Is the FREL/FRL complete? Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the FREL/FRL?
The TAR (2017) did not ask for clarification on some topics (e.g., regarding the areas under special protection, and the methodology used to calculate the reference level by Papua New Guinea Forest Authority). This information was provided but not in a timely manner. The AT considered this information particularly useful to understand the accuracy of Papua New


---
**Page 121**
---

Guinea’s FREL/FRL but the remaining missing information is still material to the completeness of the FREL/FRL.

(vi) Is the FREL/FRL consistent? (Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the time series used for the construction of the FREL/FRL?)

The TAR (2017) highlighted some issues related to consistency over the time series used for the FREL/FRL and the GIG inventory. Papua New Guinea noted that this should be improved in the GCF submission. The TAR also highlighted that different versions/interpreters of the Collect Earth tool used different types of satellite imagery, which could lead to time-series inconsistencies over the historical reference period. This was particularly an issue for forest areas where there is a history of selective logging that could be better identified with higher-resolution images, thus creating inconsistencies over time. Papua New Guinea explained its efforts in addressing this issue and noted that the GCF AT commended Papua New Guinea for its efforts. Thus, the issues were considered as resolved.

(vii) Is the FREL/FRL accurate? (The data and methodologies used neither over- nor underestimate emissions and/or removals during the reference period, so far as can be judged.)

Although the TAR of the FREL (2017) recognizes the country’s efforts in improving the accuracy of the activity data, the report also notes that there are still some issues with the overall accuracy of the FREL/FRL.

- The report states a 95% degradation factor for 74.5% of the activity data used to construct the FREL/FRL. Although Papua New Guinea used quality assured activity data and managed to address over half of the errors, the TAR commended Papua New Guinea for its efforts in addressing these issues and noted that there is still room for increasing the accuracy of the FREL/FRL in future revisions.

- Another potential source of inaccuracy was the underestimation of GIG emissions. The TAR notes that this could be due to the fact that rapid regrowth is harvested or disturbed tropical forests into the development of EFS and other land uses, which are not accounted for by the GIG inventory. This occurs only once in a particular area of forest, and that all types of disturbance cause the same change in biomass/degradation as logging. This leads to either underestimation or overestimation of emissions based on the specific circumstances.

**Page 122**

The report includes the following recommendations aimed at improving accuracy of the FRE/FRL in the Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs):

- Use crop-specific post-deforestation biomass growth rates for non-forest uses to increase accuracy;
- Improve the determination of forest regrowth in the EF for degradation;
- Improve accuracy in the determination of the losses of biomass carbon stocks resulting from different types of disturbances;
- To reduce uncertainty, it is recommended that the estimation of emissions from degradation events subsequent to the first occurrence of degradation be excluded. Excluding deadwood and other forms of ground activity would further increase the accuracy of the FRE/FRL.
- Consider including emissions from deadwood. Excluding deadwood could lead to a lower accuracy of the FRE/FRL.

The AT also noted that the inclusion of the estimation of a complete National Forest Inventory (NFI) will lead to information which will help in addressing these sources of uncertainty when the next biennial transparency report is submitted.

The AT concludes that Papua New Guinea included the three most significant activities.

(2) Has the country provided information on aggregate uncertainties, taking into account national capabilities and circumstances?

Section 10 of Papua New Guinea's modified FREL/FRL submission (2017) includes an uncertainty analysis, which includes a qualitative and a quantitative uncertainty analysis as well as an approach towards reducing errors. However, the FREL/FRL does not discuss aggregated uncertainties as such.

Chapter 6 of the BURR includes an uncertainty analysis which covers qualitative uncertainty, quantitative uncertainty, uncertainty of the RFS and aggregated uncertainty analyses for both the FREL/FRL submission (2017) and the proposed REDD+ results for the period covered by the GCF RRP submission, and for both the 2016–2015 and 2016–2018 REDD+ result periods. The results for a 95% confidence level are as follows:

Uncertainty FREL/FRL (2007–2013): 10.20%.

Uncertainty FREL/FRL (2009–2013): 11.17%.

The above uncertainties were calculated using the propagation of error approach. For the FREL/FRL specific to the proposal-to-GCF (2009–2013), the total uncertainty for deforestation is 25.8% and for degradation is 13.19%. All uncertainties are below 30%, hence, according to the scorecard, a score of 2 is given.

The LULUCF experts noted that the consistency between the FREL/FRL and the GHG inventory included in Papua New Guinea’s first BIR had increased, most notably owing to the use of the Code of Practice (CoP) and the guidance provided by the technical annexes at LULUCF. However, some inconsistencies with the GHG inventory remain.

In TAR/18 (2022), Annex 4 concludes that the LULUCF experts consider that the data and information provided in the technical annex to be transparent and overall consistent, complete and accurate. Annex II states that “methods, definitions and information used for the assessed FREL/FRL are overall consistent with those used for the results”. However, a different post-deforestation approach was applied in the two BIRs. This is because of the assumption that the approach in the FREL/FRL did not reflect the assumed increase in deforestation over time during the period covered by the BIR and therefore led to an underestimation and/or overestimation of the results. In effect, thereforere is inconsistency between the FREL/FRL and the BIRs because of the different assumptions about future deforestation trends over time. Hence this is not a justification for a “laf”. The consistency between the approaches in the two BIRs was commended by the TAR, which also concluded that this difference between FREL/FRL and BIRs should be addressed in future BIRs.

### (i) Are the data and information provided in the technical annexes transparent? (Has information been provided to allow an understanding of how UNFCCC guidance on results reporting has been addressed?)

In TATR1 (2019), paragraph 23, the LULUCF experts concluded that Papua New Guinea provided the necessary information to facilitate their understanding of the estimation of the results of the implementation of REDD+ activities. The data and information provided in the technical annexes are overall considered to be transparent, consistent, complete, and accurate to the extent possible.

However, the LULUCF experts also noted that Papua New Guinea's technical annexes and supplementary information did not provide a detailed description of the statistical procedure for estimating activity date (see para. 19 of TATR1). According to the report, this negatively affects both transparency and completeness.

Furthermore, the TATR2 explains that although Papua New Guinea provides an additional calculation spreadsheet, it was not possible to reconstruct the results.

### (ii) Are the data and information provided in the technical annexes sufficient for the reconstruction of the results?

TATR1 (2019), paragraph 23, states that the data and information provided in the technical annexes are overall considered to be transparent, consistent, complete, and accurate to the extent possible.

However, the absence of a clear description of the statistical procedure used to estimate activity date data from the sampling plots and raw data used to generate [activity data] (see para. 19 of TATR1) means it was not possible to fully reconstruct the results. The LULUCF experts noted this as an area for technical improvement that would increase the transparency and completeness of future submissions.

TATR2 (2021), paragraph 19, explains that although Papua New Guinea provided additional information in the calculation spreadsheet, it was not possible to fully reconstruct the results due to "the absence of a clear description of the statistical procedure used to estimate activity data from the sampling plots and the raw data used to generate [activity data]" (page 6).

TATR 1 (2019), paragraph 23, states that the data and information provided in the technical annex are overall considered to be transparent, consistent, complete and accurate to the extent possible.

The FREL/FRL shall be established taking into account the sources of activity data and FRLs that are consistent with the Party's accounting of forest-related GHG emissions by sources and removals by sinks reported in the Party's GHG inventory." On this basis, a score of I is applied.

**Page 129**

(v) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex accurate? (Does the annex then neither over- nor under-estimate emissions and/or removals)?

TATR1 (2019) identifies several issues that can reduce accuracy. Many of these issues were linked to the incomplete nature of the NFL, which was still under implementation at the time:

* Lack of country-specific post-deforestation biomass growth rates for non-forest land uses.
* In tropical regions, biomass may accumulate quickly, making it difficult to assess forest degradation accurately. The period over which this accumulation occurs may have been established from between a few months to up to four years after logging. This can lead to an underestimation of CO2 removals during the historical reference period. TATR1 identifies the need for further research and development in this area, particularly with regard to EF degradation as an area for future technical improvement.
* The NFL does not account for forest fires or other disturbances removing aboveground biomass as the carbon fraction provided in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines (see table 4.3).
* The NFL assumes that all types of disturbance affect forest carbon stocks equally, which is not always true. This assumption may reduce the accuracy of the estimated emissions from forest degradation.
* Forest fires are a natural process and do not happen only once in a particular area of forest; thus underestimating emissions due to successive fires.
* The team assessing the FRL/FRL noted that all forests subject to logging were considered as primary forests. However, some logged forests, particularly those with high regeneration rates, are not likely to contain biomass carbon stocks comparable to primary forests. This assumption may lead to an overestimation of forest degradation emissions.

TATR2 (2021) states that the information in the BURZ uses more recent and accurate information. In para 72 of TATR1, the NFL states that "the data and information provided in the technical annex are as complete and up-to-date as possible to ensure that they are as accurate to the extent possible". The completion of the NFL allowed for many of the issues identified in TATR1 to be addressed. This is particularly relevant with regard to the use of more recent data sets used for the land-use change assessment as an area for future improvement related to accuracy.


---
**Page 130**
---

The last year of the FRL historical period in 2013. Results are considered for 2014, 2015 and 2016. There is thus one year between the end of the FRL/FRI historical period and the beginning of the period being proposed for payment.

**(viii) Has the country provided information on aggregate uncertainties, considering national capabilities and circumstances?**

Chapter 5 of the technical annex in BRR.1 includes the uncertainty analysis, which covers qualitative uncertainty, quantitative uncertainty, uncertainty of the EFs, and an aggregated uncertainty analysis for the FRL/FRI 2001–2013 as well as for the 2014–2016 REDD+ results period.

Chapter 6 of the technical annex for BRR.1 includes: aggregated uncertainties, including a retrospective recalculation of the results period 2015-2018 covered by BRR.1. The uncertainty analysis covers qualitative uncertainty, quantitative uncertainty, uncertainty of the EFs and an aggregated uncertainty analysis for the FRL/FRI 2001–2013, the FRL/FRI 2009–2016 (revised), the FRL/FRI 2014–2016, the FRL/FRI 2017–2018 and the REDD+ results period. This was the uncertainty analysis used for the purposes of assessment.

The results for a 95%-confidence interval are as follows:

- Uncertainty FRL(2001–2013): 10.26%
- Uncertainty FRL(2009–2013): 11.77%
- Uncertainty FRL(2014–2016): 15.69%
- Total uncertainty results (2016–2018): 14.98%

For both results periods, the uncertainty for degradation results was under 30% (17.50% and 16.81%, respectively). However, the uncertainty for deforestation results was 34.44%. For the period 2014–2015 and 21.11% for 2014–2016. Hence, according to the scorecard, a score of 1 is applied, because not all uncertainties are below 30%.

It outlines plans for the investment of proceeds, their alignment with other relevant forest climate strategies in Papua New Guinea, and a summary of the approach to the investment of proceeds, a theory of change diagram, and key performance indicators (KPIs) for monitoring progress on mitigation, adaptation, and institutional strengthening. A score of 2 according to paragraphs 10-13 of the GCF Investment Framework Project Proposal (IPF) confers eligibility for the 2.5% bonus for non-carbon elements.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP273

Proposal name: Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP for results period 2014 - 2016

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Papua New Guinea

Project size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Overview
1. This is the first submission of the funding proposal Papua New Guinea REDD+ RBP for results 2014 - 2016 submitted to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) under the Results-Based Payment Framework (RBP). The accredited entity (AE) is the United Nations Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO).
2. In the context of REED+, Results-based Payment refers to ex-empt payments made for climate change mitigation results, which are defined as reductions in greenhouse gas emissions determined by the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and carbon enhancements as defined under the Kyoto Protocol. The reference level (RL) is the baseline reference level (FRL) submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), and the actual GHG emissions and carbon enhancement reported in national reports to the UNFCCC are used for payment.
3. The Board decision 84/016 of October 2014 establishes the GCF Policy for results-based payments for REDD+. The policy outlines how GCF incorporates payments for verified emission reductions (VERs) into its portfolio and how it will be implemented on a yearly cycle. According to the policy, such payments can apply to "REDD+ results that have been fully measured, monitored, reported, and verified in accordance with the GCF Policy."
4. The decisions of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+ of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) are also relevant.
5. At B39 the Board agreed to allow, on an exceptional basis, four pending REDD+ RBP concept notes submitted to GCF before the closure of the pilot programme in 2022 to be submitted for consideration by the Board at its next meeting and to be considered as part of the current funding proposal from Papua New Guinea based on one of those concept notes. The Board agreed that the submission should be made under decision B19/07, with the accompanying terms of reference in annex A to decision B18/07.

### References
1. Reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation and the role of conservation, sustainable management of forests and enhancement of forest carbon stocks (REDD+): a framework negotiated under Article 2(5) of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). Decision adopted by the Conference of the Parties serving as the Meeting of the Parties to the Kyoto Protocol at its seventh session, 10-18 December 2007.
2. The REDD+ framework is therefore recognized in Article 5 of the Paris Agreement. Detailed rules for REDD+ are contained in decision B19/07 adopted by the Parties under the Warsaw Framework for REDD+.

#### Main findings and recommendations

**13 Impact potential**
Scale: High

From this section onwards, the assessment adopts a forward-looking perspective and focuses on the proposed use of proceeds.

The objective of the use of proceeds is to support the national Government and six of the provincial governments in Papua New Guinea (PNG) with the implementation of the PNG Climate Change Strategy (CCS) for 2017-2027. The emphasis is on the following action areas: strengthened land-use planning, law enforcement, protection of forests and sustainable livelihoods. Achieving this objective should contribute to reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea.

The proposed use of proceeds will be implemented through a combination of financial support for capacity building and technical assistance, as well as direct funding for the implementation of specific projects and activities. The total amount requested is US$10 million over five years (2017-2022).

The project will contribute to reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea by supporting the national Government and provincial governments with capacity building, technical assistance, and direct funding for implementation of specific projects and activities. The project will also support the development of a national climate change strategy and action plan.

The project is expected to have a significant impact on reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea. The proposed use of proceeds will be implemented through a combination of financial support for capacity building and technical assistance, as well as direct funding for the implementation of specific projects and activities. The total amount requested is US$10 million over five years (2017-2022).

The project will contribute to reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea by supporting the national Government and provincial governments with capacity building, technical assistance, and direct funding for implementation of specific projects and activities. The project will also support the development of a national climate change strategy and action plan.

The project is expected to have a significant impact on reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea.


---
**Page 141**
---

the sustainable management of forest, and the conservation and enhancement of carbon stocks.*


16. The funding proposal is structured around two components. Component 1 aims at increasing the institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial governments in Papua New Guinea (PNG) to ensure that REDD+ activities are implemented for inclusive-sustainable livelihoods of customary landowners and local communities in the targeted provinces.

17. The funding proposal covers six provinces: West Sepik, East Sepik, Western Province, Hela, Chimbu and West New Britain Provinces. The selection of these provinces resulted from applying a multi-criteria analysis (MCA) that considered factors such as reducing emissions, i) recognizing values of forest beyond carbon, based on a forest landscape integrity index, and ii) addressing the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation, including forest degradation because of anthropogenic pressure based on population density, which is considered to be one of the main drivers of deforestation in PNG’s lowland rainforest country.

18. Component 1 will promote enabling conditions at the provincial level and component 2 will offer context-specific livelihood options that address the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in each province. For more information about the development outcomes, see section 1.4 below.


While RFP funding proposals to GCF are not required to include a detailed annex 22 estimating the potential carbon sequestration or emission reductions, an estimated range of 3-4.5 Mt Co2e is to be reduced over the six-year implementation period. This figure is based on NRS financing and does not include any additional funding from other sources. It is estimated that more than 518,000 people, equivalent to approximately 6.5 per cent of Papua New Guinea’s population, will benefit directly.


The ITAP assessment is informed by the four elements of Papua New Guinea's national framework to implement REDD+, which is aligned to the corresponding decisions in the UNFCCC and its Kyoto Protocol (KP) and the National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) as submitted to the UNFCCC/REDD+ Hub, the National Forest Monitoring System, the revised version of the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC), and the NRS.


The structure of the funding proposal demonstrates strong alignment with key action areas mentioned in the NRS, including strengthening climate change legislation, financing and management arrangements for REDD+, and capacity building. The proposal clarifies how monitoring will be integrated into the National Forest Monitoring System and calculates the potential carbon sequestration or emission reductions from decreasing REED+ results through the REED+ Registry Data Management System.


In terms of reporting results to GCF, the AE will use the specified Annual Performance Report (APR) template as outlined in section V. footnote 19 of the GCF Guidelines and Procedures XL, section V. footnote 19. Furthermore, the AE clarified to the ITAP how, in addition to the RFP monitoring requirements, it will also be required to submit an annual progress report using a project-specific logical framework. The ITAP recommends that, to the extent possible in the implementation of the REDD policy, the AE consider facilitating oversight of the funds disbursed to sub-recipients and ensuring that these funds are used for their intended purposes.


In view of the above, the ITAP assesses the climate impact potential as high.

1.4 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: Medium to High**

The six targeted provinces covered by the funding proposal represent approximately 42 per cent of Papua New Guinea’s total land area and 29 per cent of the total population. According to the proposal, a significant portion of the country's GHG emissions originates from family farming and subsistence agriculture, including shifting cultivation.

To address this challenge, the proposal outlines several land-use and livelihood measures that, on one hand, establish the enabling conditions for REDD+ implementation at the national and subnational levels. On the other hand, they aim to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from land degradation, particularly those linked to population growth and unsustainable food production systems. As such, the proposal seeks to identify practical pathways to reducing GHG emissions and forest change in the six selected provinces.

Given the project’s embeddings in national REDD+ frameworks and its collaboration with local communities, it is expected to contribute significantly to the climate agenda of Papua New Guinea. Moreover, the integrated approach (linking institutional strengthening [component 1] with livelihood and economic transformation [component 2]) can generate important co-benefits for poverty reduction and food security in the targeted provinces within the national context.

However, it is important to note that some major drivers of deforestation in Papua New Guinea will not be addressed by this proposal, in particular commercial agricultural expansion, especially oil palm development, which, according to the country’s FRL, accounts for approximately 80 per cent of all forest cover lost on converted land. This limitation constrains the overall paradigm shift potential of the project.

The assessment of the ITAP assesses the paradigm shift potential of the funding proposal as medium to high.


1.5 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

Besides contributing to climate action, this funding proposal has the potential to contribute towards achieving Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 3 (Decent work and economic growth), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth), SDG 9 (Industry, innovation, and infrastructure), SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities), SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production), SDG 13 (Climate action), SDG 14 (Life below water), and SDG 15 (Life on land).

**Institutional co-benefits.** The project will strengthen the institutional capacity of the six targeted provinces for implementing REDD+ activities. In addition, through its alignment with national policies and strategies related to climate change adaptation, mitigation, and sustainable natural resource management, the project is expected to contribute to enhancing the institutional framework in these areas.

**Social and gender-related co-benefits.** Improved agricultural systems introduced by the project are expected to contribute to enhanced food security at both the household and provincial levels. The project will also support women’s empowerment within targeted local communities by supporting their involvement in planning and implementing sustainable land-use activities, including through the establishment of Women’s Land Rights Committees (WLRs) that document the application of Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC) in stakeholder consultations. The proposal outlines how FPIC will be used to empower marginalized groups in future activities.

The proposal emphasizes that gender equality has been integrated as a cross-cutting priority throughout all components of the project. This integration is expected to have a gender lens, for example recognizing women’s customary rights and land tenure claims, building the


---
**Page 143**
---

financial or technical capacity of women and youth or considering gender-specific needs in the definition of production systems and sustainable value chains.

### Economic co-benefits

The project can provide several economic co-benefits, including the creation of sustainable and diversified economic opportunities through new production systems on small farm level, enhanced productivity and improved market access for local producers.

#### 1.4 Benefits of tropical forest conservation

In addition to the benefits outlined in section 1.3, the conservation of tropical forest, the project will support the conservation of biodiversity and the long-term sustainable use of natural forests. Strengthening the enforcement of forest laws and promoting integrated land-use planning can also contribute to improved livelihoods for local communities and environmental integrity.

#### 1.5 Monitoring and funding proposal outlines how non-carbon benefits have been monitored in the past, while section C2 details how these aspects will be integrated under the proposed use of the proceeds. The proposal anticipates several measurable environmental non- carbon benefits from the project, including: (i) an increase in forest cover; (ii) areas of forest conserved; number of threatened species for which habitat will be secured; area of native forest planted and managed as sustainable forest products.

#### 1.6 Needs of the recipient

Papua New Guinea is classified as a lower-middle-income country with a population exceeding 11 million and a gross domestic product (GDP) of USD 27.3 billion in 2023. The economy is dominated by agriculture, which accounts for approximately 45% of GDP and employs the majority of the labour force, largely in informal arrangements; and (ii) minerals and energy resources.

According to the latest reports from the International Monetary Fund (IMF)[1] in 2022 approximately 40 per cent of the population lived below the extreme poverty line, and an estimated 65% of the population lives on less than $3.20 a day. The World Bank’s 2022 country risk ranks Papua New Guinea 167th out of 187 countries, with a score of 36.9, indicating the country's high vulnerability to economic and political risks.

The funding proposal notes that despite economic growth over the past decade, Papua New Guinea remains a high-risk investment environment, with persistent barriers including lack of formal land tenure systems, weak institutions, poor governance, and widespread lack of alternative livelihoods, weak enforcement and implementation capacities of the forest authorities, limited access to information and lack of capacity to effectively negotiate with land developers. The amount of land that is not formally registered can allow land grabbing and create major barriers to halting deforestation and forest degradation.

[1] See https://www.imf.org/en/Topics/imf-and-the-world/economic-outlook

[2] See https://www.pncb.org/pncb-website/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2021/12/157/Papua-New-Guinea-Fifth-Review-under-Extended-Arrangement-under-the-Extended-Fund Facility-55653.pdf

[3] See https://www.pncb.org/pncb-website/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/03/13/Papua-New-Guinea-PNG-Fifth-Review-reports/Recovery/2023/03/13/Papua-New-Guinea-Papua-New-Guinea-56379.pdf

1.7 Country ownership

* **Scale:** High

The funding proposal includes a no objection letter signed by the Climate Change and Development Authority, the National Designated Authority to GCF, dated December 2024.

Papua New Guinea submitted its second Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) in 2020 which sets out long-term targets for reducing greenhouse gas emissions from land use, agriculture, forestry and other land uses (LULUCF) and energy. The NDC includes absolute and relative emission reduction targets for the period up to 2035. The baseline year is set at a 2015 baseline. All targets considered as conditional. The NDC places particular emphasis on the ARIUS, which sets out pathways towards effective REDD+ implementation for achieving both mitigation and development objectives.

As previously noted, the funding proposal is fully aligned with Papua New Guinea’s National Strategy for Sustainable Development (NSSD) 2015-2030, as well as to nationally defined REDD+ instruments, including the FRL and the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS).

The funding proposal includes annexes on Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) and on Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). These documents contain information on the project’s potential environmental impacts and mitigation measures. The response from the project, including a list of non-eligible activities, the approach towards securing consent for the project, and the process to ensure that the project will not work as well as a work plan and budget. During the question and answer session the AE is explained to ITAP that the ESMF will be further refined during the inception phase, in response to the high degree of social complexity.

FAO has a well-recognized track record in supporting developing countries in the development and implementation of national forest policies and strategies.

In consideration of the above, the TAP assesses the country ownership of this funding proposal as high.


1.8 Efficiency and effectiveness

* **Scale:** High

The funding proposal request a result-based payment of USD 63,413,756 for emission reductions from deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea between 2014 and 2024. These emissions reductions have been independently verified by ITAP and were subsequently technically assessed by the UNFCCC secretariat. All related documents are publicly available on the GCF website (see annex 3 for a project-based scoring assessment of this funding proposal (see the annex 1 scorecard).

The GCF proceeds will be strategically invested in activities aligned with the NDS and in supporting the implementation of the ARIUS. By enhancing regulatory reforms and advancing the implementation of the NPS, Papua New Guinea aims to improve carbon


---
**Page 145**
---

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The funding proposal for Papua New Guinea (PNG) REDD+ BFP for periods result 2014 - 2016 requires that PNG should be able to demonstrate that it has achieved significant emission reductions through reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Papua New Guinea in 2014-2016 compared with 2009-2013.

The ITAP Secretariat reviewed the information regarding the achieved GgC emission reductions using the scorecard methodology adopted by the Board and the documents submitted by PNG. The ITAP also assessed the use of proceeds, applying the six investment criteria established by the Board.

The implementation of this funding proposal will allow Papua New Guinea and the Accredited Entity to achieve significant emission reductions in PNG through REDD+ by enabling action at the subnational and local levels. These activities will be integrated into the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) and the Safeguard Information System (SIS), thereby enhancing national capacity to implement REDD+ and increasing alignment with international standards.

Considering the results of the review and the assessment of the proposed use of proceeds, the ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

### Criteria Score Remarks

#### (c) Is the FREL/PREL consistent with the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions inventory used for the definition of forest use?

1 The funding proposal states that the GHG inventories in the first and second BiHs and the FRL are based on the same data sources, methods, definitions and assumptions.

Table 5 in the second BiH and p. if of the funding proposal mention that the "N2O and CH4 were included in the inventories for the BiH, but not in the FRL/REDD+ methods."

#### (ii) Is the FREL/PREL based on historical data and it equal to or less than the average annual historical emissions over a reference period, unless a country is an RFLED country?

The original modified BiH was based on historical data from 2001-2013.

Historical reference period: 2001 - 2013

For each year in this period, the results of a linear regression model (Y = a + bX) were calculated. The model included deforestation, forest degradation and forest carbon stock enhancement in the reference period as well as the assumption that these factors would continue at the rate of the increasing deforestation rate, thus the original modified BiH values are the average annual historical emissions.

However, in Section 6 of the second BiH Papua New Guinea submitted a revised version of the FREL/PREL based on data starting in the GCF-REDD+, using the annual average of the period 2009-


---
**Page 149**
---

### (ii) In the FRL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in decision LC/CFR/2013 plus the adjustment of 0.2% of the C stock purely over the five-year period resulting from the characterization of Papua New Guinea as a BHL country.

#### (iv) Are the data and information provided for the FRL/FRL complete? (Has information been provided to clarify the understanding of how UNFCCC guidelines on submission of information on reference periods has been addressed?)

Although Papua New Guinea has increased transparency of data and information over time as stated in the two TARs and the TAR (2017) highlights the collaborative and open approach shown by the country, for the 2015 FRL/FRL assessment, the assessment team noted that key information (e.g. specific data on forest degradation and deforestation) was not submitted only after their request for clarification. Furthermore, the AR4 FRL/FRL assessment of Papua New Guinea did not use SFs used for forest degradation and lack of transparency on some of the employed methods.

TAR (2015): The TAR asked for clarification on some topics e.g. regarding the data and information provided by the country on land leases, forest plantations and viable concessions, as identified by the AR4 FRL/FRL assessment team. This clarification was not provided but on a timely manner. Hence, the TAR considered the information incomplete.

TAR (2017): The TAR highlighted that there is no consistency between the data for the GHG inventory. Papua New Guinea clarified that this should be improved in the FRL planned for 2018 at the time of the AR5 FRL/FRL assessment. The country also stated that the CE tool used different types of satellite imagery, which could lead to differences in the identification of forest areas during the reference period and issues related to the identification of forest areas adjacent to non-forest land uses. These issues were not identified with higher resolution images, thus creating inconsistency over time. Papua New Guinea explained its efforts to improve this issue for the 2018 FRL/FRL assessment but Papua New Guinea has not yet provided data on this. Thus, the issues were considered as resolved.

Excluding deadwood can lead to a lower accuracy of the FRL

The TAR (2017) notes that Papua New Guinea includes reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks. The emissions from deforestation and forest degradation and enhancement of forest carbon stock were reported as zero during the historical reference period.

The TA concludes that Papua New Guinea included the most significant pools in terms of their contribution to the scope of the FRL, by also assessing the significance of any other activities and considering the inclusion of additional pools such as emissions from deforestation and forest carbon stocks and sustainable management of forests, as part of this approach.

All of the most significant pools been included?

The FRL includes both living pools: AGI and BGL. The TA concluded that the most significant pools in terms of their contribution to the scope were included in the FRL (para 39). However, improvements in the estimation of soil organic carbon and deadwood were recommended

# Deadwood is not included and may be significant

## Papua New Guinea utilizes fuel wood with very high carbon content and deforestation is expected to increase.

### According to Field Assessment, Papua New Guinea may account for 10-25% of total carbon stocks in the modified FRL. Papua New Guinea explains that the data are based on a mix of reliable data or default values in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines and that more data on deadwood will be available in the future.

## Soil organic carbon, Papua New Guinea explained that significant emissions may occur from the loss of carbon in soil owing to deforestation. 40-40 per cent of total carbon stocks during the conversion of forest lands into agricultural land. In the modified FRL submission, Papua New Guinea explained that significant emissions may occur from the loss of carbon in soils owing to deforestation and the loss of soil organic carbon.

### The FRL includes GHG and exclude non-CO2 gases from biomass burning. The TAR (2017) highlights that considering that Papua New Guinea has experienced a large increase in forest fires during "El Niño" years, large spikes in levels of burning occur. Further studies are likely to be increased and noted that non-GHG emissions will be included.

## (ii) Is the information provided in the construction of the FRL/FRLR, methodologies and emissions (para.) the most current applicable IPCC guidance and guidelines as adopted by the conference of the Parties?

The TAR (2017) states that Papua New Guinea uses the methodology described below on the six land-use categories for constructing the FRL/FRLR. (para. 33)

Papua New Guinea uses the average RFU for tropical rainforest from Fon et al., 2016 (223 t CO₂/ha). For the remaining ecological zones, Papua New Guinea uses the average RFU provided in the IPCC guidelines.

The removal factor for top soil degradation is based on forest land conversion and its transition to grassland with perennial woody vegetation. This is based on a weighted average of data from Fon et al., 2016 (235 t CO₂/ha) and crop types and grasslands provided in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines (274 t CO₂/ha).

## (iii) Have any significant issues related to the application of IPCC Good Practice Guidance arisen in the TAR report?

The TAR (2017) does not include any significant issues related to the application of the IPCC guidelines or the Good Practice Guidance.

## (iv) What is the reference period for the FRL/FRLR?

In the modified FRL, the historical reference period is 2003 - 2013 (11 years). The FRL extrapolates values using a linear regression model. (para. 4)

The FRL was calculated for the GCF-RFP and this re-calculated in the second RBR submission. The re-calculated of the FRL is based on the average RFU emissions from the period 2009 to 2013 (5 years).

The re-calculated FRL submitted in the second RBR was used in the GCF-RFP. The re-calculated of the FRL/FRLR, included in the modified submission in the Lima Hub (see table below) is lower than the modified FRL using the linear regression model.

1. **INTRODUCTION**

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) recognizes the central importance of gender considerations in terms of both women’s access to climate funding. Consistent with the GCF's Terms of Reference for the REDD+ Results Based Financing (RBP) pilot programme, Papua New Guinea's (PNG). RBP is underpinned by both the GCF's Gender Policy (2019) and FAO's Framework for Environmental and Social Management (ESM) for REDD+. The GCF’s Gender Strategy 2018-2023 (GSCF GS) states that gender equality and prevention of gender-based violence. ESG is geared at ensuring that the design and implementation of projects are inclusive, equitable, and responsive to the needs of women and girls, addressing gender inequalities, discrimination and gender-based violence (GBV), in alignment with international frameworks on gender equality and women's empowerment and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment.

The purpose of this Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAAP) is to provide a comprehensive overview in relation to the role of women in the development and management of forests and forest resources and with this their potential role in the implementation of activities to be supported by the REDD+ RBP. The GAAP will also consider engagement and consultation efforts over the REDD+ readiness period, and to look at relevant gender gaps and barriers that may exist within the project area. These considerations were included in the REDD+ RBP proposal to enable compliance with national and international commitments on gender equality and women's rights.

The REDD+ RBP proposal is in line with PNG’s National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) and benefits from gender perspectives, including those of the World Bank Programme and World Bank’s FCG program implemented as part of the REDD+ Readiness phase in this country. Also, the PNG government has made progress in advancing gender-related legislation and regulations, and in the design of initiatives to address gender equality and women's empowerment (GEWE) through the CGI Gender Mainstreaming Guideline for Project Implementers and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The GAAP will also consider alignment with the gender policy of CGI and FAO, as well as with the environmental and social safeguards.

This GAAP is structured into two parts: a) A Gender Assessment (GA) covering section two (2) to five (5) of this document; and b) a Gender Action Plan (GAP), included in the Annex.


* **Gender Assessment (GA):** provides comprehensive background information and key insights into the specific needs, challenges, and opportunities related to gender dynamics within the project area. The GA will also describe efforts of gender mainstreaming in the context of REDD+ in PNG and provides recommendations for addressing gender gaps and barriers that may exist within this RBP project. Moreover, it has informed the due diligence Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) report, prepared as an Annex 2 to the RBP.
* **Gender Action Plan (GAP):** outlines strategies for overcoming existing gender barriers and gaps as highlighted by the GA, proposing high-level and strategic gender-responsive actions and measures to be taken throughout project implementation, and which will be refined during the project’s inception phase consistent with the ESM for the project.


---
**Page 167**
---

Overall, as an integrated document, the GAAP was based upon available data from studies conducted by the Government of PNG (GoPNG), key donor agencies and multilateral organizations, civil society and research organizations. These studies include:

- Desktop review to complement and, where necessary, further update and revise information gleaned from previous consultations conducted as part of the UNDP-led GCF proposal preparation process in 2019 and aligning approaches with this proposal with the national priorities of PNG.
- Review of national aspirations as detailed in the national policies and development plans for ongoing projects.
- Reports with lessons learned and assessments on gender equality in PNG from the GoPNG, and other international donor agencies, development organizations, civil society and research organizations.

### 1.2 Brief Overview of the Proposed REDD+ Project

The proposed RBP Project has been developed under the umbrella of PNG’s National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) and is thus envisioned to directly contribute to the implementation of selected policies and measures in prioritized provinces. By providing positive incentives for reductions in emissions from deforestation, forest degradation, sustainable forest management, conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks, the REDD+ project aims to address specific actions targeting low emission development activities that will also provide long-term sustainable livelihoods for communities living within or adjacent to forests through improved monitoring and management systems required to ensure transparency of results delivered and the effective management of resources received.

The REDD+ RBP Project is structured in the following way:

COMPONENT 1. Increased institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial governments in PNG and in the targeted provinces (40%).

Output 1.1: Establish the National Sustainable Land Use Planning (NSLUP) framework for the targeted provinces.

Output 1.2: Enhance and strengthen environmental management, protection, and land use reporting systems at national and local levels.

Output 1.3: Improve coordination and reporting on the implementation of the NRS, NDC and GHG for the CCAD.

OUTCOME:

a) Coordination among government institutions at the national and provincial levels has improved.
b) The NSLUP framework was established at the national level and implemented in targeted provinces.
c) Regulations on environmental management, protection, and land use have been enhanced and strengthened.
d) PNG is able to report timely to the UNFCCC regarding the implementation of the NRS and other climate actions


---
**Page 168**
---

COMPONENT 2: Enhanced economic productivity and inclusive sustainable livelihoods of smallholders and customary landowning groups in the targeted provinces (60%)

- Output 2.1: Strengthen forest landscapes management technical and financial capacities for customary landowners and local communities.
- Output 2.2: Promote green economic systems and green economic productivity for customary landowners and local communities

OUTCOME:
a) Customary landowners and local communities from targeted provinces adopt inclusive sustainable livelihoods and increase productivity.
b) Forest landscapes are sustainably managed by landowners and local communities.
c) Green economic systems and sustainable livelihoods are implemented in targeted provinces.


---
**Page 169**

# THE GENDER ASSESSMENT (GA)

## 2. EXISTING GENDER DYNAMICS IN PNG

Over the last two years, PNG has made some strides in promoting gender equality within the country and has taken actions to promote and integrate gender equality into the broader policy-making agenda with some notable advances in women's economic education1 and equality before the law.2 Yet, progress remains relatively slow in the areas of women’s economic opportunities which also includes access to land, credit, and financial services. The country continues to face challenges related to gender-based violence, cultural restrictions, low rates of education, and high rates of poverty. It makes it difficult for women to participate equally in the economic development of their own natural resources.

### 2.1 Gender Inequality Indexes

Several indexes have been developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality and to allow comparisons over time and across countries. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and the Gender Development Index (GDI) to achieve this purpose. The GII measures differences in outcomes between women and men in reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market while also measuring achievements in education, political participation, economic resources.

The GDI considers gender gaps in human development between men and women. Currently, PNG has a very low GII value of 0.740, ranking it 161 out of 182 countries in the UNDP’s latest Gender Inequality Indexes.

### 2.2 Education

#### Papua New Guinea (PNG) has achieved gender parity in elementary school enrolment, but disparities remain at higher levels. Girls are more likely to be enrolled than boys in secondary education and girls equal nearly in elementary education (grades 1 and 2) and mostly near parity in primary school (grades 3 to 8). A significant gap emerges in secondary school enrolment. Girls are more likely to drop out of school at the end of grade eight, with only one girl enrolled for every ten boys in 2018. Despite some progress at the primary level, PNG lags behind regional peers in achieving gender equality in secondary education3.

Refer to National Education Plan (NEP), 2015 - 2019.

PNG’s age expectancy at birth increased by 7.8 years in the years between 1990 and 2018 according to surveys and reports from PNG Statistics Department.

Section 53 of the Constitution refers to Equality of All citizens and the Policy on Gender Equity and Social Inclusion (GESI) which is a national policy that aims at promoting gender equality and social inclusion in all areas that are promoted at the National and Subnational levels of government. It also promotes the participation, empowerment and representation of women in the labour market, currently standing at 46.0 percent compared to 47.8 for men.


---
**Page 170**
---

Girls often leave school early due to safety concerns, financial constraints, inadequate infrastructure, and domestic responsibilities. Rigid gender norms and societal expectations place significant burdens on girls and women, making it challenging for them to continue their education. Household-level factors such as parents' attitudes towards daughters' education, the perception of the benefits of girls' education compared to that of boys, resulting in lower investments in daughters' schooling, and the lack of access to safe schools are some of the reasons why girls drop out. The notable increase in girls' enrollment highlights the importance of providing free basic education to ensure universal access.

Poor school environments further contribute to female dropout rates. Many girls report feeling unsafe or harassed by older male peers and male teachers, which is compounded by inadequate infrastructure, such as a lack of proper sanitation facilities (Edwards, 2015, Department of Education, 2020).

These challenges are even more pronounced in rural areas, where overall school attendance is lower, and dropout rates are wider. Rural girls face additional pressures, including higher rates of early marriage and pregnancy, as well as more pressing domestic expectations. Women's involvement in subsistence agriculture is critical for sustaining families and communities, and their household responsibilities often far exceed those of men.

### 2.3 Political Participation and Decision-Making

Women's political participation and representation is extremely weak in PNG with none of the 111 parliamentarians elected as women. In the most recent elections, 165 women ran for parliament out of a total of 1,000 candidates, making up only five percent of the total number of candidates, indicating a huge disparity in gender within the political sphere. Although PNG has had 7 female representatives since independence, there have been no female ministers or deputy ministers with seats out of a total of 111, following the election of 2012 when women accounted for less than 3% of the representation in parliament. The low number of women in political positions is not only due to the lack of women's participation but also because there is an overwhelming bias against women in leadership in general which reflects the general discrimination women face within PNG and especially in the sphere of politics. Furthermore, the few women who have managed to enter into political life have often been relegated to less powerful roles or were often given "lesser", such technical positions in areas such as community development and religion.

As a result of the high gender inequality observed within this sphere proposals were put forward to increase women's representation in parliament. In 2017, the government introduced a bill to the Government to pass proposed changes to the Organic Law on the Integrity of Political Parties and Candidates (ULPPIAC). One of the provisions was that at least one-third or less than 20% of the total number of candidates nominated by a political party must be women. In 2018, the government directed the PNG Constitutional and Law Reform Commission (CLRC) to review the Organic Law on National and Local Level Government Elections. One


---
**Page 171**
---

of the 12 terms of reference was for “women and special interest representation” in Parliament[1]

• Not related to parliament, but it is also worth noting that Autonomous Region of Bougainville also passed the Bugniville Women’s Federation Bill in June 2020.

The gender disparity in politics is also reflected in public service, which is the largest employer in PNG with women holding only 31% of senior positions. In 2019, it was reported that just 38 percent of civil servants are women with 22 percent of those women in executive management roles and only 7 percent as heads of departments. Furthermore, there is minimal government-wide coordination on gender equality issues and the lack of a national gender strategy hampers efforts to address these gaps within the public service making it difficult to assess the accuracy of the current gender gaps reported.

### Participation in Labour Force

The labour force[2] (participation) (LFPR) rate is the proportion of the population ages 15 and older who are economically active, i.e. all people who supply labour for the production of goods and services or who are unemployed but seeking work. The LFPR among women aged 15+ in PNG was 45.78% as of 2019. Its highest value over the previous 29 years was 71.15% in 1990, while its lowest value was 65.73% in 2000. The LFPR for women aged 15+ has been on a downward trend since the mid-1980s and continued to decline during the course of that period, especially among unskilled young men and adults. At the same time skilled labour and female participation in the formal economy have increased. This is reflected by the increase of 180 countries assessed on the participation of women in the labour force, which is reflective of the statistics provided.

Women face significant disadvantages in economic participation. Only 45 percent of working women aged 25–64 may be engaged only in self-employment activities in the informal sector. Women play a large role in agriculture, but female-headed households earn 43 percent less than male-headed households[3]. The lack of access to education and training opportunities, as well as discriminatory social norms contribute to girls dropping out of school early, limit women’s access to productive resources and reduce their economic opportunities. 58 percent of women have experienced gender-based violence in PNG with the impact of such violence extending beyond the personal impact also takes a heavy toll on families and the economy[4].

Women's limited access to economic opportunities arises from interconnected challenges at home, in the workplace, and within their communities. Factors such as gaps in education and skills, domestic responsibilities, insufficient family planning resources, male-dominated cultural norms,

13 International Labour Organization. GLOSTAR database. Data retrieved in September 2019.

There are numerous terms used to refer to violence against women, with no universally agreed upon definition. This document uses gender-based violence and violence against women interchangeably to refer to acts leading to physical, sexual or psychological harm by a range of perpetrators, including intimate partners and family members, which is the term commonly used to refer to such acts in PNG.

17 World Bank., 2023


---
**Page 172**
---

and legal or institutional barriers combine to restrict their economic empowerment. These inequalities also hinder women’s access to networks and information, affecting their livelihoods and increasing health risks. The COVID-19 pandemic highlighted these issues, with many female entrepreneurs unaware of available financial assistance and vaccine hesitancy significantly higher among women.

In PNG, significant gender disparities exist in paid employment, within a broader context of low labour market participation for both women and men. According to the 2016–2018 Demographic and Health Survey, only 31% of women (aged 15-49) participate in the labour force (other than employed in agriculture), compared with 70% of men. The female paid employment rate is lower than the average for comparable countries, with a wider gender gap in PNG than in other Pacific Island countries. Despite these challenges, and despite these statistics, recent data confirms that a substantial proportion of women in PNG remain outside paid employment. From 2016 to 2018, only 45% of employed women and 51% of employed men primarily work for pay.

This variation in remuneration also shows up not only in the differences in gender representation in different sectors, but also in vocational training and entrepreneurship. In PNG, few women have leading roles in big businesses, SMEs or informal enterprises. The data on SME sectors from a survey conducted by the World Bank (2018) reveals that 95% of all firms are owned by men; only 3% female-owned in addition. Female employment is still concentrated in sectors related to traditional gender roles such as agriculture, fishing, and domestic services. Together, these sectors account for approximately 45% of working females, while about 48% of employed men are engaged in the agricultural, industrial, and construction sectors.

Business training offered to women is often gender-biased, particularly in rural areas, where women often take courses on topics related to their traditional roles, such as cooking and sewing. Female-owned businesses are concentrated in the micro and small end of the spectrum. These businesses tend to be more vulnerable than male-owned firms, which are more likely to be located in the commerce, services, and manufacturing sectors, which tend to be less profitable overall.

2.5 Access to Land and Resources

PNG has both patrilineal and matrilinial systems of traditional governance in the various regions of PNG.17 Even in the case of a matrilinial community, decisions over the use of land and resources will still be made by male relatives (either the brothers or sons) of the female landowner.

Generally, under customary practices in PNG, governing over 97% of its population - which largely belong to indigenous communities - is done through traditional governance systems. Decision-making authority are passed from the male patriarch/chief to the eldest male child, whereby the male child also inherits the position of leadership in the clan and all rights of access to land as well as the right to make decisions over land resources. These rights will only pass to the female child as part of

17 Ibid.

https://www.researchgate.net/publication/299596791_A_scoping_study_to_provide_an_assessment_of_SME_pol icy_priority_areas_for_Papua_New_Guinea

20 ibid.


---
**Page 173**
---

her inheritance if there are no male children born into the family. However, even in such circumstances, the male patriarch/husband will usually request another male relative to assist the female child in all processes of decision-making.

As such, there are many gender disparities in access to and control over key agricultural, forestry or natural resources and services, knowledge, opportunities and markets. Rural women are major contributors to household food security but their contributions have not been recognized nor have they benefited equally from post economic growth. Many are systematically excluded from access to resources, essential services, and decision-making.

At the household level, men often make the decisions about family income and spending. Women are less likely than men to own land or other assets; friends and family are relied upon for financial assistance. There are few community credit schemes available and major commercial bank requirements are not suited to the average rural woman interested in starting and/or expanding her business.

As examined in detail in the Environmental and Social Management Framework, underpinning this GAAP, the majority of land in PNG (97%) is vested in the customary landowners and local communities. For any development project involving use of land, forests or water, apart from those for traditional purposes, a Land Use Agreement must be negotiated with the relevant community. This process ensures that rights related to specific extractive, agricultural, fishing, hunting, crafts and other activities are recognized and respected by developers and/or resource users and/or resource owners. In PNG, this process of acquiring legal recognition over land known as incorporating Land Groups (LGs) involves all members of the community, both women and men alike. However, in practice, women have been systematically excluded from participating in these issues that were initiated through amendments to the ILG Act in 2009 which made it mandatory for at least one-third of the management committee to include women.

With remaining 3% of State land and land under private ownership in PNG only about 40 percent of women own land compared to 60 percent of men. Among those who do own any land, half of the men own it alone as opposed to jointly, whereas just 33 percent of women do (NSO, 2019).

Joint land titles in PNG, especially involving marriage couples, does not always help to secure a woman's rights over her land. In fact, joint titles can sometimes reinforce a husband's status and security. There are studies which have shown that in some instances titling jointly has helped to institutionalize the dominance of men over women's land ownership. This is particularly true when the case when the woman has acquired the land either through customary inheritance (in matrilineal societies) or by her own merit as a business owner/professional/woman. Her husband will demand that she cede control of this land to him, and in some cases, he may resort to violent acts of violence/abuse which further institutionalizes the dominance and 'control' her assets over her.

Additionally, land tenure in the country is difficult to track and follow. There is no central database containing information on land ownership and title and the official land registry system is often outdated, archaic and unreliable. Rather, each land office has both the provincial and national level

22 FAO Country Gender Assessment of Agriculture and Rural Sector in PNG, 2019 Report.

World Bank, 2023


---
**Page 174**
---

has its own paper-based system. This situation makes it equally difficult to track and compile gender data and statistics on land ownership.

In relation to land held under customary tenure, women have little to no control over it even if it is held in the name of clan (wamkwit) and most of the control is in the hands of the male chief, his male sons and/or immediate male next of kin. While women typically have user rights to cultivate land, gather firewood, collect water, and harvest wild plants, they do not have formal property rights or have control over productive resources. Planting annual food crops does not secure long-term user rights over land, unlike the planting of small plantations of recurrant crops such as cocoa, coffee and palm oil. The latter are semi-permanent markers of property rights and confer status upon the woman who controls them.

When customary land is leased for plantations, logging or mining, or when access to marine and riverine rights is granted, women seldom participate in such negotiations. Furthermore, women are not usually considered to have the right to claim a direct share of leases, royalties or compensation payments from these activities. However, there are some exceptions to this rule, then which have helped to boost the status of women in land ownership, particularly those in the professional fields who are literate and have sufficient finances to access and thereby own property, including land.

It is not necessary for a customary land holder to exercise a right over a piece of land or to use it temporarily. A great deal of land is cultivated by non-customary holders, with the permission of, or at the request of, the customary holder. When a person who does not have rights to a piece of land cultivates that land, they are usually required to pay rent to the customary holder for general labour arrangements or to ensure the maintenance of marriage, exchange and social relationships; the right to cultivate is often conditional on these obligations being met.

However, these unfruitful arrangements are usually only limited to growing annual crops. People living in rural areas who do not have access to land on which to grow food and are consequently prevented from planting trees, particularly export cash crop species like cocoa or coffee, but also food-producing trees such as breadfruit, sago and coconut. Tree planting (and the burial of the dead) is usually done by men because women are considered to be too weak to do this work. Although land is cultivated under unsatisfactory arrangements, it can be difficult for women and unmarried men to gain access to land on which to plant tree crops or to develop cash-earning business enterprises.

The Land Act (1996) includes provisions related to customary land, such as its registration, but does not address other transactions, such as the management of unregistered customary land among or between generations. Women are often excluded from these transactions and from public life. Women often face challenges in enforcing their rights to land and property or claiming income from cash crops and land leases. More broadly, women's interactions with the state are often

13


---
**Page 175**
---

mediated through family and clan structures, limiting the state’s ability to address the various inequities they encounter**.

### 2.6 Gender Based Violence and Access to Social Resources

Most violence against women in PNG is committed by intimate partners; the prevalence of non-partner sexual violence (NPSV) is also high, with annual rates also alarmingly high compared to WHOD regional and global estimates. The lack of an institutionalized system for collecting administrative data on GBV makes it difficult to obtain accurate information. Women are less likely than men to report violence experienced emotional abuse (such as threats, insults, or public humiliation), physical violence, and/or sexual violence from an intimate partner at some point in their lives.

Violence and harassment against women are major impediments to the achievement of not only social and health indicators but also economic indicators. Various surveys carried out within the private sector/businesses in PNG have shown that:

- On average, employees experienced a total of 7.8 incidents of GBV over the past 12 months.
- 50% of the firms surveyed reported experiencing an average of 9.4 incidents in the past year.

On average, each staff member loses 11.1 days of work per year as a result of the impacts of gender violence: 2.0 days lost to “presenteeism,” 5.0 days to absenteeism and 4.1 days helping other victims of GBV. For one of the firms in this survey, this means an estimated 26,300 hours per year.

The cost of staff time lost due to GBV is high. For one of the firms covered, it is estimated to be two times their total salary bill for men and three times that of women, representing 8% and 9% respectively of those companies’ total salary bills.

If other direct costs are included (recruitment, recruitment and induction costs, medical costs, etc.), this figure increases to between 10.5% and 43%.

Different measures have been put in place to address this issue, including the development of the National Strategy to Prevent and Respond to Gender Based Violence, 2016–2027** which provides a framework that calls for high-level oversight in the form of a parliamentary committee on gender-based violence. The strategy also aims at increasing the capacity of communities to fight against gender-based violence in the country. At the political level, individual leaders such as

### Social Services

In terms of access to social services, the Government of PNG through both national, provincial and local levels of government have some support programs in place for the provision of basic counselling, psychological health, and legal support to women in urban areas. For those within the rural and remote areas however, this access is very limited.

### Grievance Redress Mechanisms

Most GRI mechanisms are handled by local or district police and referred to village courts for remediation. These GRM measures are included as part of social services which cover access to justice and are either handled by the formal legal system, e.g., the Police or the village and district courts, or through informal means such as mediation, arbitration, or through customary/traditional forms of conflict resolution. Not all services are fully operational, accessible or reliable and where there are gaps many civil society organisations such as charities and NGOs also assist.

There are also many international donor agencies as well as international NGOs2 which seek to help the GRIWG in reaching most people, especially women and vulnerable people. Those services that are not on the GRIWG are ruled out through the Department of Community Development who have branches at the provincial courts and in some districts (as per a recent Government led initiative).3

### Challenges to Vulnerable and Marginalized Groups

#### Elderly

The elderly are the smallest demographic group in PNG accounting for approximately 3.51% of the population.4 The most prominent issue concerning this fragile group of people in PNG is the lack of social security, which means that they have no access to any form of specific needs. This means the burden to care for the elderly is often borne by the immediate family.

Although a minor percentage of this group are pensioners and receive their employment entitlements at their retirement, prolonged delays with processing the payouts or budget/financial constraints will mean that many pensioners turn again towards these much-needed funds. This increases the susceptibility to physical and emotional/mental diseases, illnesses and poverty. The
