statistics for men are higher than women in many cases due to the fact that most delayed pensions involve those within the armed/disciplinary forces.28

For those with no form of education and reside in the urban cities from the rural areas, the statistics show a work force through customary support system do recognize and encourage the need to care for and esteem the elderly members of family, tribe or clan; this is observed more within the rural communities. The elderly who are widowed, abandoned by husbands and those with disability and sickness are found among the elderly living in settlements in the cities. Women who are widowed or those who have been abandoned by husbands and the disabled are amongst the most vulnerable groups of people. These women are often unable to earn a livelihood and are excluded from the community due to their inability to regularly work or contribute to the family livelihood.

Another serious issue that has risen among elderly women in the rural areas is the increasing number who become prey to vigilante killings and human rights abuses because of alleged involvement with witchcraft. These women are often accused of being witches and are killed in cold blood. In most cases, the motivation behind these wanton acts of violence were either due to conflict related to land or resources or a desire by some individuals to steal land or resources from the alleged offender/acused29. The government has enacted the Sorcery and Witchcraft Accusation Related Violent National Action Plan 2015, which seeks to address violence associated with sorcery accusations.

The government repealed the previous anti-sorcery legislation in 201330, which had provided a defense for violent crime if the accused was acting to stop witchcraft. The government established the Sorcery National Action Plan in 2016, to be implemented by a national committee. The efforts are currently ongoing.

The Youth

According to the United Nations, youth are those members of the local population who fall within the ages of 15 and 2431. PNG is the largest demographic group occupying almost 60 percent of PNG’s population equating to about 7.3 million inhabitants. If current population trends continue, the country will reach a population peak in 2038 when it has its first child before the age of 18 and the country’s population is projected to reach 13 million by 203232.

A multitude of social and economic challenges plague the country's youth and prevent them from flourishing into productive citizens. The lack of education, poor health conditions, and the contrasts in leadership between the traditional social perspective compared to the Western ideals. The former is often seen as a barrier to development and progress for PNG’s youth who are often excluded from and denied roles and responsibilities within the community, workplaces, and society despite increasing modernization. Young people also face the growing demand to help bring wealth into their communities through their participation in activities or community decisions that create an avenue to access and develop this wealth.

In PNG (youth and children) are also exposed to the highest rate of violence in the East Asia and Pacific Region. Small-scale studies consistently show that violence is a part of everyday life as a large number of children are physically, verbally and sexually abused.[1]

Although, the formal justice system segregates youth offenders from adult offenders as part of the Juvenile Justice system, the response to youth’s access to justice is varying with many victims of violence not being able to access justice. The most common form of compensation in lieu of court action for serious crimes such as rape or assault.

Without investments in the health, education and employability of this large population cohort, the potential of this group to become susceptible to gangs and the proliferation of crime drastically increases. This is particularly true for young men who are out of school or within the justice system. The same is true for young women, who become more susceptible to prostitution or fall into the cycle of violence as they experience servitude with employed relatives.[2]

Although a relatively new concept to PNG, human trafficking is occurring and many of the victims are children. In 2016, PNG has partnered with the US department of justice and US department of state to push various measures in PNG to address this issue such as the development and enactment of human trafficking legislation whereby all perpetrators and victims will be able to be prosecuted under the law. The awareness program was launched by the following NGOs: Western, AROB, West Sepik, Eastern Highlands, Morobe and Madang. The awareness program started in 2016 with most of the target provinces competing awareness.

### Gender and Agriculture

The economic growth of Papua New Guinea is driven by the agricultural sector, which contributes nearly 28 percent of the GDP[3], providing employment and livelihoods for 87 percent of the population. The vast majority of rural economic sector is predominantly comprised of subsistence and semi-subsistence farming.

In the village agriculture sector the division of labor is strongly defined by gender; women are responsible for most of the agricultural activities, including planting and harvesting crops (mainly root crops) such as yams, cassava, taro, sweet potatoes, and vegetables. Women also produce food for their families through livestock production (thus earning cash income), and rearing of small livestock such as poultry and pigs. Women produce over 80% of the country’s food and account for 60-70% of the work related to food crop production.[4] In addition, women are responsible for most of the post-harvest activities; they harvest crops, common, tend to work on the estates as wage workers, while women take care of crops and livestock at home for provision for the household. Though there are regional differences, women’s contribution in terms of food production is significant. Despite their important role towards household/national food security, their work is significantly underappreciated and undervalued.

Rural women play multiple roles simultaneously, including managing triple responsibilities in their workplaces, households and communities. They take on the primary responsibility to ensure that

[4] World Bank Development Indicators, 2018.

[5] ICA Country Gender Report 2019.


---
**Page 179**
---

The nutritional, childcare and health care needs of their families are fulfilled, in addition to the community, social, and cultural roles they are required to perform. Given these multiple roles and duties, rural women lack the time to participate in other opportunities that could potentially help to enhance their knowledge, skills, and self-esteem. Women disproportionately bear the consequences of climate change and the division of household members as compared to men, as well as face higher rates of domestic violence.

Those living in highly marginal environments suffer from periodic food shortages. As a buffer against such risks, production of staple food crops such as sweet potatoes, potatoes, yams, and bananas is more common than cash crop production. Women are the main producers of horticultural activity for rural PNG, especially for rural women. Women engage mostly in growing and selling fresh food crops (e.g., taro, sweet potato, banana, cassava, pumpkin, papaya and vanilla) and are responsible for most of the planting, weeding, and harvesting of these crops.

The sale of fresh food crops in local or urban markets generates cash for women and their families. Women tend to sell fresh food crops in local or urban markets, while men tend to be more involved in long-distance trade and export of cash crops such as cocoa, coffee, palm oil, and copra for sale. Such a division of duties has implications for economic equality, as women earn less from their sales at local markets than men earn from selling export cash crops. Women are also likely to be paid less by buyers who purchase fresh food crops directly from them or through other ways[1]. Among the reasons that women are largely limited to production and sale of fresh food crop is a lack of access to credit, land tenure security, and transport infrastructure as well as being limited in their ability to travel greater distances, for instance, to urban and regional markets, for various reasons, including security. However, as PNG experiences a gradual shift toward more intensive cash cropping systems, women are increasingly becoming involved[2], for instance in the adoption of improved farming and irrigation methods, if adequately supported.

Gender and rural finance

There are important gender-specific barriers to accessing financing (or extension services), as women tend not to own the land, fixed assets, or other resources that are needed to meet collateral requirements. Women also face a number of social, economic, and cultural barriers, such as legal, financial, and educational gender-based barriers that are pervasive. It is therefore necessary to collect, analyse and use sex-disaggregated data to track progress, as well as to tackle the discrimination and inequality that women face in accessing finance[3]. The World Bank’s Global Monitoring Report (WDR) 2018 on Gender Equality and Development states that promoting gender equality in economic development is a key strategy for reducing poverty. This is because it can lead to increased growth and faster poverty reduction. Because women find it more difficult to access credit than men in PNG, only 30 percent of all customers of financial inclusion services in the country are female.

Access to climate finance is essential for fostering climate-resilient agriculture and ensuring food security. A review of Papua New Guinea’s (PNG) financial services conducted by the Bank of Papua New Guinea (BPNG) and the Australian Development Finance Corporation (AIFC) highlighted significant challenges related to access, credit and financial products and services in PNG, including in vulnerable situations. Inequalities are evident in the lack of transparency around fees, rates, and

[2] Bourke and Howard, 2009.

[3] UNDP, 2016.


---
**Page 180**
---

conditions, combined with an absence of financial consumer protection. This leaves vulnerable community members exposed to unregulated lending practices. Women have less access to and control over, the financial resources needed for their economic autonomy. The Bank of PNG (BPG) estimates that only 14 percent of women are able to secure bank loans, compared to 20 percent of men in rural areas. This is partly due to low levels of financial literacy. The Government of PNG, supported by donors such as the ADB, Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade (DFAT), and AusAID, has been working on increasing access to affordable financial inclusion of underserved communities and women. Women form 46 percent of the informally employed population and the GPGNG is keen to improve women's financial literacy and to reduce gender gaps in financial inclusion. The National Financial Inclusion Strategy (NFIS) focuses on financial inclusion, the National Informal Economy Policy provided bank accounts to 50 000 women in 2013. [1]

### 2.8 Health

In PNG women and children suffer from serious health-related disadvantages. Child mortality rates are high, with one out of every thirteen children dying before reaching the age of six. The risk of maternal death has increased due to a combination of high fertility levels and lack of skilled health personnel, who attend only an estimated 51 percent of births throughout the country.[2] For both women and men, the leading causes of death are malaria, tuberculosis, diarrhea diseases, acute respiratory disease[3]. HIV/AIDS is also highly prevalent, affecting nearly 7 percent of the adult population. Studies conducted in 2018 revealed data that showed a 54% increase in the number of people living with HIV/AIDS since the National Capital District had HIV infection (compared with 2.8% prevalence nationally).[4]

These dismal statistics, however, there are signs of progress, with declining malaria rates and improvements in the care and treatment of HIV/AIDS, which received much attention as a government priority.[5] The maternal mortality ratio fell from 103 per 100,000 live births in 1990 to 69 in 2013. Some 98% of births are currently attended by skilled personnel[6]. However, despite these improvements, the overall health status of PNG is poor, and progress in the delivery of better health care in PNG is significantly limited by multiple, interrelated factors, of which the most basic are availability of medical resources such as beds, medical assistants, and low levels of training for health workers.[7]

The accessibility of basic services by rural many rural communities is constrained by the topography of the country, which affects both the availability of health workers and the delivery of supplies. [8] The percentage of GDP expenditure on health care is low by international comparison[9] and the available funds are vulnerable to systemic corruption in the medical/health-care system. Despite

3. POLICY AND REGULATORY FRAMEWORK PROTECTING WOMEN AND GENDER EQUALITY

### 3.1 Key International Protocols and Frameworks Supporting Gender Equality, Women's Empowerment, and Human Rights

PNG has signed and ratified different international conventions and treaties on human rights, social inclusion and equality, some of which have helped to form the basis for promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment in PNG and around the world. Refer to Table 1 below.

#### Table 1: List of Relevant International Treaties Signed or ratified by PNG

| International Treaty | Ratification |
|---|---|
| UN Declaration on Human Rights | Not Available |
| International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights | 2008 |
| The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, including its Optional Protocol | 1995 |
| International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights | 2008 |
| The Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous People | Not Available |
| The International Convention for the Suppression and Punishment of the Crime of Racial Discrimination | 1973 |

#### Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action

Other global and regional initiatives on gender equality/women’s empowerment such as the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action signed during the Fourth World Conference on Women (1995) is considered one of the most progressive plans to promote women's rights. This regional policy plan was developed in 2000, and it has been recognized that it would not be sufficient if there were no specific activities aimed at the empowerment of women, and to take into account gender considerations in all cross sectoral policies and programs to consider gender impacts.

As a result of this international platform and plan of action, PNG chose to model the development of a National Gender Equality Strategy (NGES) in 2010. The NGES identified 35 strategic actions prioritized under 10 priority action areas. This platform was also instrumental in helping to design and develop the implementation plan for specific gender policies and action area within the framework of the PNG’s development plan. It includes key strategies, performance indicators, key actors and illustrative budgets for those specific policies.

#### Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) Implementation & Progress in PNG

As part the implementation of the United Nations gender equality goals in PNG, PNG is following the United Nations Development Assistance Framework (UNDAF) 2018-2022. This outlines the


---
**Page 183**
---

partnership between the UN and the Government of PNG in support of the 2030 Agenda.* PNG adopted the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and the Small Island Developing States (SIDS) Accelerated Modalities of Action (SAMOA Pathway) to achieve the country’s Vision 2050. The SDGs were also integrated into key national policies such as the Medium-Term Development Plan (MTDP) 2013-2017 and carried on into the MTDP V (2023 - 2027) with other national policies, legislation and budgets.


Progress has been challenging for many of the thematic areas particularly for Gender Equality due to internal/external pressures. However, the first Voluntary National Review Report (VNR*) provides the most comprehensive overview of progress made in PNG towards achieving the SDGs, including the specific actions that relate to Goal 5 on Gender Equality. The most notable are covered below.


PNG has signed up to a number of progressive international conventions and instruments to improve the status of women and attain gender equality. (Refer to Section 3.1 of the Report) Progress in this area is being made through various initiatives, including those related to women’s empowerment. Examples include:


* The National Women Violence Action Committee: A sectoral committee under CMC operating since 2001 playing a pivotal role in coordinating provincial and sectoral data collection for a national response.


* Certain government departments: For example, the National Department of Health is rolling out Family Support Centers.


The program to roll out the Family and Sexual Violence Unit in 2008 growing to 36 across the country by 2019, staffed by 109 police officers in total.


* The Office of the Public Prosecutor is initiating a Victim Liaison Officer, which in due time will be extended to other provinces.


* Parliament also has passed the Family Protection Act In 2015.


* Some progress has been made on maternal and child health. For example, maternal mortality improved from 733 per 100,000 live births in 2006 to 145 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2019. Eighty-seven percent of births to urban mothers were assisted by a skilled provider and 85 percent were delivered at health facilities. The proportion of births attended by a skilled provider increased for births to rural women.


* Regarding education, the gender parity ratio for elementary schools was recorded at 1:01, primary schools at 09:11, and secondary schools at 04:65. This is an unprecedented development, showing that more girls are entering the education system.


* Positive trends have been observed in terms of women’s economic empowerment through the Household and Income Expenditure Survey (HIES) 2009-2010 showing that a similar proportion of women (68 percent) as men were employed. However, women are more likely to be self-employed or work at home although less likely to hold a wage job in the formal sector (66 percent of men than 38 percent of women). Women’s engagement in SME and financial inclusion has grown significantly since 2015 with 47 percent of women engaged in SMEs compared to 39 percent by men. 28 percent were by women, with GoPNG aiming to reach 50 percent under the Maya Declaration signed in 2013.

3.2 National Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Frameworks, Policies and Laws

### 3.2.1 National Gender Laws and regulations

Human rights and specifically those international instruments that promote gender equality and non-discrimination have been ratified by the Government of PNG and domesticated within the National Gender Policy (NGP) 2015. The NGP is a comprehensive policy document that seeks to ensure equal participation by women citizens in all economic, activities. It also enshrines these basic human rights in the Constitution of Papua New Guinea 1975 (as amended in 2003 and 2013) and within respective Acts of Parliament such as the Marriage Act, 1951, Matrimonial Causes Act, 1961, Deverted Wives and Children’s Act, 1951, Family Protection Act, 2013 and the Lukutian Pilkini Act, 2017.

Apart from addressing the social needs of women who are considered more vulnerable to issues of abandonment and unfair/unjust treatment within the context of marriage or relationships, the last two pieces of legislation apply to the broader concerns of violence against women and girls as a whole. The first piece of legislation was the Family Protection Act 2013 (FPA) which replaced the PING as part of revisions which introduced a range of new offences in addition to the ones introduced by the Deverted Wives and Children’s Act, 1951. The most significant and most controversial was the offence of marital rape; a concept unheard of in PNG until its introduction. This was further developed to include grading according to the seriousness of harm, and the number of victims involved as well as penalties against offenders involved in acts of sexual violence committed against women and girls.

In addition to these advances in legislation, certain policies and measures to address the specific issues of gender discrimination and gender-based violence were also achieved. Some of the more notable include:

- The Office of the Public Prosecutor Initiated a Victim Liaison Officer, which in due time will be placed within the Family and Sexual Violence Unit.
- The national Family Sexual Violence Action Committee, a sectoral committee under the Commission for Gender Equality (CGE), played a pivotal role in coordinating provincial and sectoral data collection for a national response.
- Certain initiatives were also introduced by the National Department of Health began rolling out Family Support Centres in 2009.
- GoPNG aimed to economically empower 50 percent of all women under the Maya Declaration signed in 2011.

### 3.2.2 Gender equality in forest sector laws

Gender equality within forestry sector legislation has developed slowly in the last 15 - 20 years and particularly within the forestry sector. The forest sector operates predominantly in remote areas, with households earning income through royalties, lease payments, and wages primarily directed to men. Women are often involved in other forms of economic activity such as subsistence activities, such as propagating plants and maintaining nurseries. Women typically lack control over valuable forest resources like timber, which are often managed by men, and they frequently lose access to these during land conflicts.


---
**Page 185**
---

Although the Forestry Act, 1992 (section 103(1)(b)) recognizes the rights of women in decision-making over the management of forest resources through women representatives on the National Forest Board, women's lack of representation in decision-making within key subnational level mechanisms continues to be a hindrance to achieving gender equality. There are specific references to equal participation and representation for women in the Forestry Act (1992) and the National Forestry Policy 1990 Part V(b); however, in reality, forestry continues to be one of the more male dominated resource sectors in the country.

Despite these gender gaps, informal initiatives have commenced in recent years to address them throughout PNG. These include the Women's Forest Management Network (WFMN) which addresses issues affecting PNGGA female employees with extension to women in forest concessions as well. The WFMN has also been instrumental in raising awareness of women's rights and has raised awareness in areas of health, lifestyle diseases such as obesity, diabetes and hypertension, violence and abuse at home and in the workplace and has also focused on financial empowerment for women through savings groups. These groups are often linked to microfinance initiatives with banks especially with the local banks, Bank South Pacific (BSP) and microfinance institutions.

In 2009 amendments to the Land Groups Incorporation (Amendment) Act were initiated in relation to customary land tenure and the legal mechanisms that recognize, hold and administer customary land. i.e., the Land Groups Incorporation Act. The Act provides for the establishment of established and managing land owning groups especially in relation to resource development projects where a formal management committee is required. Under this Act at least two female representatives (out of 6 representatives) must be elected to ILG management committees, as a way of meeting the 30% minimum requirement for women's representation. As a result of these amendments, the process is now adhered to in all natural resource development projects where ILGs are required.

In relation to gender and climate change the Climate Change Management Act 2015 provides for a woman representative on the Board of the Climate Change Development Authority (CCDA) and also provides for the inclusion of Gender Development Officers within the CCDA. The Gender and Climate Change (drafted in 2014) which is presently in the process of being incorporated into a formal workplace policy.

Conflict resolution as it relates to natural resource acquisition and customary land development is also a major issue for the GoPNG with a legal system in place that extends to all tiers of government and provides avenues for both formal and informal means of redress in PNG as part of the judicial system. The key issue here is that while there are provisions within the law regarding women's access and use of these mechanisms is a right that is provided by law and extends down to the grassroots level through entities such as the Village Courts which are set up for this purpose and developed under the auspices of the Department of Justice. However, in practice where decisions made accessible by women, many of the customs incorporated into the forms of redress provided tend to be discriminatory against or disadvantage women because of the strong customary and traditional influence.


### 3.2.3 National Gender Policies, Plans and Programs


---
**Page 186**
---

Many of the key gender policies and plans in the country came into effect within the REED+ results period including the Papua New Guinea National Policy for Women and Gender Equality 2011-2015. This was the overarching national policy developed to govern gender discrimination issues within the public service and across all sectors of government. This Policy described the government’s commitment to gender equality, outlined its strategic objectives and goals, and refers the establishment of a policy environment that translates government commitments on gender equality into reality.

While gender mainstreaming across government has resulted in some policies and strategies, few of these have been developed with a specific focus on gender equality. Despite the existence of this national policy, it still remains a key guiding framework for gender interventions in PNG. There are also other specific policies which address the issue of gender equality but are limited to the context of that sector.

The National Strategic Plan 2010-2050 (Vision 2050) is the national development strategy that guides PNG’s social and economic development. One of the key focus areas of this Policy is ‘Human Capital’. This policy provides a framework for the implementation of specific gender strategies and actions by the Government. The National Gender Policy 2013-2025 (NGP), which was developed from the GEDD Policy and the National Strategy to Prevent and Respond to Gender Based Violence, 2016-2025.

As mentioned in the previous section, there were sweeping reforms to PNG’s approach towards GBV initiated between 2009 and 2019. One such reform was the National Strategy to Prevent and Respond to Gender-Based Violence (GBV) 2016–2025. The strategy is a comprehensive response of the problem ensuring the issue was brought to the fore in the government’s planning processes and encouraging the development of policies, strategies, and service provision. Despite the positive inroads being made within government to address gender inequality and discrimination issues, various factors also work to undermine this progress as well.

The biggest challenge is the lack of enforcement of women’s rights under the law, particularly the Constitution. These laws hold the power of enforcement within Village Courts, and are drawn from customary law which often does not accord women equal rights in relation to social rights and property. Studies of GBV indicate that such violence appears to be widely accepted and “culturally condoned.” This also aligns with the strict zero tolerance policy towards SEAH and GBV enacted by the Government under the National Strategic Plan 2010-2050, the Gender Equality Standard and Social Standard 6 on Gender equality and prevention of gender-based violence.

The National Informal Economy Policy 2011 was another key gender policy introduced with the desire to further the economic empowerment of women and men taking into consideration the existing informal economy. The policy aimed at promoting the transition from subsistence agriculture to high value market activities utilising PNG’s unique cultural context. This policy also aims to promote development and more importantly allow women to advance within the supportive network of their families and communities. It sets the vision and the

framework to achieving key objectives, it also outlines the key agencies responsible for implementation and requires a concerted effort by all stakeholders to ensure this occurs.

Part of the change is in ensuring the environment necessary for women entrepreneurs in the informal sector to grow their business and be able to expand into the formal economy. The economic empowerment of women has multiplicative impacts on other development issues and therefore, it is important that women’s economic empowerment is not seen as a separate issue but to promote women in business, yet much of this work is driven by development partners and non-government organisations. ### 3.2 Gender Institutions and participation in decision making

National Council of Women (NCW) was the first formal entity established by the CGIAC in 1979 to represent women’s issues on gender and children in PNG and to give effect to the rights of women and children established under the National Constitution. Having been in operation in PNG for 40 years it is now a key institution that has played an important role in shaping the country. It is designated to be the link between the national level and the subnational level.

Shortly after the establishment of the NCW, a government department was created to further expand its mandate and to address issues concerning women, youth, children and related social issues. The Department for Community Development (DCD) was established in 1980. In addition to this, the Department for Community Development (DFCD) in 2008. In addition to this, the Office for the Development of Women (ODW) was also established and activated in July 2009. The latter being responsible for the implementation of gender equality policies and programmes at the subnational level with coordination with key line agencies and monitoring of achievements in relation to international commitments.

Both entities serve to address the issue of gender equality in PNG; however, issues concerning lack in sufficient budgeting by the government for the implementation of these various gender strategies and plans seriously undermines their progress. The National Council of Women, in partnership with other stakeholders including the Department of Justice and Law Reform Commission began discussions in 2016 on the establishment of proper administrative structures through the development of NOW regulations and a five-year strategic plan to ensure greater representation for women at all levels.

Apart from the government mandated authorities there are also many CSOs and private sector entities which also partner with these agencies to implement specific gender programs/action plans at the subnational level where the central DFCD and key national agencies are not fully operational.

In relation to gender and climate change the Climate Change Management Act 2015 provides for a woman representative on the Board of the Climate Change Development Authority (CCDA) and also promotes a draft Climate Change Development Authority workplace GuideLine for Gender and Climate Change. This was launched in 2016 which is presently in the process of being incorporated into a formal workplace policy.


---
**Page 188**
---

32.4 Gender and Climate Change Policies

PNG’s NDC emphasizes the critical role of the LULUCF (Land Use, Land-Use Change, and Forestry) sector in achieving its climate goals, aiming to maintain its high forest cover (78%) and its reduction in emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (EDD). To ensure effective implementation of the management of land and forest resources, it recommends the involvement of local communities, youth, and women in the implementation of sustainable agricultural and agroforestry practices. Potential strategies include:

- Promoting socially inclusive climate-smart agriculture
- Enhancing women’s access to resources, information, and climate smart technologies.
- Strengthening the role of men and women in promoting rural value chains.
- Promoting inclusive decision-making on land use and timber management.

The NDC also highlights the importance of a gender-responsive and inclusive approach, ensuring the participation of men, women, youth, and vulnerable groups in consultations, planning, decision-making processes, and implementation. This seeks to promote equitable opportunities for sustainable, low-carbon livelihoods, reinforcing the human rights-based framework in addressing climate change across priority sectors.

Further to this, the Climate Change Management Act 2015 provides for women representatives on key decision-making bodies such as the National Climate Change Council (NCCC) and the Technical Working Committees. A draft CCDA workplace Guidance for Gender and Climate Change (drafted in 2014), is presently in the process of being incorporated into a workplace policy.

The Climate Compatible Development Management Policy of 2014 also mentions the need for "fair and equitable access to land, resources, and opportunities" as well as the need for women to be involved in decision-making processes. It also refers to the need for women to have equal access to information on climate change and its impacts.

There was reference already made to the Forestry Policy 1990 and its promotion of the equality of women and men as part of Forest Training and Education, although not clearly emphasized as much as within the Agriculture sector. This sector specifically addresses gender equality as seen in the National Gender Strategy (2013-2017) which includes a focus on gender equality, including gender-based violence, family planning, and HIV/AIDS.

As with most legal and policy reforms, adequately applying and enforcing the new laws and policies requires more robust attention to funding and resource which remains a concern for the GoPNG that has been faced with issues of huge budget deficits in the last few years."

### 3.3 Institutional Arrangements

In 2008, when the Department of Women and Children was reformed into the Department for Community Development (DCD), the national gender machinery, known as the Office for the Development of Women (ODW), was also established in the DCD and activated in July 2009. ODW


---
**Page 189**
---

addresses issues concerning policy and strategy formulation, gender-related research, coordination with other line agencies, and monitoring of achievements in relation to international commitment.

In addition to these mechanisms are those that are currently in operation and which were established in 1979, known as the National Council of Women. Both offices serve to address the issue of gender equality and women's empowerment by ensuring that there is sufficient budgeting for gender within government for the implementation of these various strategies and plans. The NCW has been in operation in PNG for 40 years having established 22 presidents' committees on gender equality at both the national level and the subnational level. The National Council of Women, in partnership with the Department of Gender Equality and the Ministry of Justice and Law Reform, began discussions in 2016 on the establishment of proper administrative structures through the development of NCW regulations and a five-year strategic plan to ensure greater empowerment at all levels.

Apart from the government mandated authorities there are also many CSOs and private sector entities which also partner with these agencies to implement specific gender programs/action plans at the subnational level where the central DFCD and key national agencies are not operational.

In the event of specific legal or policy changes relating to gender considerations, special institutions such as the Law Reform Commission, effect recommendations from the Government on key gender issues such as lack of political representation, participation in customary land management, GBV, polygamy, etc. However, many of these laws and regulations have been amended or passed to accommodate these priority gender issues in PNG; however, more legal reform is required to bring about greater rights for women.

The justice and law enforcement systems are also insufficient due to the demand. Police and legal professionals need to be trained on how to apply and enforce the law governing gender discrimination or GBV. Village courts also use customary principles to govern matters at the district and local level which also further exacerbate gender discrimination against women.

# 4. GENDER ASPECTS OF FORESTS IN PNG AND GENDER MAINSTREAMING IN REDD+ ACTIONS

## Forests and gender in PNG

The forests of PNG cover approximately 77.8 percent of its total land area (which is about 35,891,241 hectares of land). Both women and men in forested areas of Papua New Guinea rely on forest resources for their livelihoods; they collect firewood, food, medicinal plants, and materials for crafts, which meet their daily needs. Women are responsible for gathering and harvesting timber species. Despite this mutual reliance, there are significant gender inequalities in access to rights, benefits, and responsibilities related to forest resources. Deforestation and forest degradation disproportionately affect women because they depend on forests as a source of fuel, food, and income; often, a role overshadowed by women and children, especially girls.

Women and youth play an important role in management and production in forest lands, although they are not landholders. While they have use rights, women rarely own heritage rights over productive forest resources. Women’s access to forest resources is also limited because of the right to claim a direct share of leases, royalties or compensation payments[1]. The primary objectives of women in PNG forests include food security and income generation for their families; however, once these needs are met, they become important vehicles through which income-generating opportunities and enterprises can be developed[2].

The timber industry in PNG contributes approximately USD $530 million per annum according to 2017/18 data. The forestry sector is the largest contributor of revenue, which accounts for approximately 6.3% in total exports of USD $9.8 billion making it one of the key sectors for revenue generation in PNG. Currently about 50% of the country is covered with production forests[3], so there is a huge emphasis on timber harvesting and processing. However, women are not directly involved or employed by them with little to no opportunities to participate in the industry; despite, a small percentage of women graduating with relevant qualifications in forestry.

The forestry sector operates predominantly in remote areas, with households earning income through薪金 from forest products. Women are often excluded from activities directed to men employed in logging operations. However, women often participate in reforestation activities, such as propagating plants and planting nurseries[4]. Women typically lack control over valuable forest resources like timber, which are often managed by men; and they frequently lose assets, such as trees, during tribal conflicts.

In some regions, sago palms serve as a staple food resource. Sago is also an important cooperative activity between women in PNG forests. The sago found in the wild is harvested by members who provide the staple food resource which it often also sold in markets for daily sustenance[5]. The role of cutting down, splitting the palms and constructing makeshift sluices is given to the men. The women

[4] Papua New Guinea Forestry Outlook Study Working Paper Series. Working Paper No. APF/SSG/WP/2006/19
[5] Papua New Guinea Forestry Outlook Study. PNGFA. FAO. 2009.

extract, wash, stone and cook the sage starch[1]. Mangrove forests also play a very crucial cultural and ecological role in the lives of coastal people, who depend heavily on them for food, firewood, shelter and safety. Women rely on them for the harvests of various mollusc and crustacean species which they are able to sell at markets. Mangroves are also crucial breeding grounds for fish.

• The NDC of Papua New Guinea (PNG) recommends that, given the importance of customary land tenure systems in PNG, it is important to involve communities, youth, and women to implement sustainable agricultural and agroforestry practices. Promoting socially inclusive climate-smart agriculture.
- Enhancing the capacity of farmers to adopt climate-smart technologies.
- Strengthening the role of women and youth in agricultural value chains.
- Promoting sustainable forest management and timber management.

These actions aim to ensure an integrated and equitable approach to achieving sustainable development goals in the agricultural and forestry sectors. PNG signed the Forest and Land Use declaration in 2021, committing to "halt and reverse forest loss and degradation by 2030 while delivering sustainable development and promoting an inclusive rural transformation".

### 4.1 Efforts to mainstream gender in REDD+

#### 4.1.1 Gender in the National REDD+ Strategy

In 2017 PNG's National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) was approved by the Government[2] following an 8-month consultation process, which took place in conjunction with the development and submission of its forest reference level (FRL) for the same year. These efforts resulted in the notable progress towards PNG’s early REDD+ implementation and the scaling up of actions to reduce emissions as per the Warsaw Framework for REDD+, and more recently, the Paris Agreement of 2015.

The NRS is the guiding document for REDD+ in PNG and it is structured in two key components which are further developed into five policy and action areas. Through these, the NRS promotes low-emission development strategies (LEDS) that address climate change adaptation and mitigation activities, as well as establishment of the coordination, monitoring, reporting and management systems required for the full transparency of results in this devolved and the effective management of funding received.

The NRS is aligned with relevant PLRs such as the National Constitution which recognizes the right of women to participate in economic activities and national development plans and policies that promote gender equality. The NRS also aligns with the PNG Gender Policy (PNGGP) and the PNGPC. Given this harmonization with PLRs, gender equality is addressed and promoted by the NRS implementation. Furthermore, the NRS within each of its action areas, establishes the need to

[2] NEC Decision-Decision 16A/07/13.

[3] The FRL report submitted in 2017 and published by the UNFCCC in 2018 is available at the following link: https://unfccc.int/resource/docs/2017/wglims.pdf

**Page 192**

### Transparency International PNG REDD + Risk Assessment (2014)

Between 2012 and 2013, Transparency International PNG (TIPNG) conducted initial research into the potential risks associated with REDD+ projects, particularly regarding the potential risks posed by REDD+ funding and finance. This was especially relevant given the challenges related to weak government institutions and management of natural resources in Papua New Guinea.

As part of its main findings, it identified weaknesses in project design practice and highlighted priority areas for reform. Among the critical risks noted in the report were concerns related to the design and implementation of environmental and social safeguards, as well as the failure of the existing legal framework to adequately protect indigenous peoples' rights to free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC) processes at the local community level.

The assessment strongly emphasized that neglecting appropriate FPIC processes could have serious negative impacts on vulnerable groups, particularly women and children, who are most at risk in terms of land tenure security. The report also highlighted the need for more transparent public consultations, which help initiate discussions on the need for REDD+ specific safeguards and mechanisms to protect the rights of vulnerable groups, with particular attention to women and children in forest-dependent communities.

### Situational Analysis on Gender in PNG (2016-2017)

Between 2016 and 2017 an extensive study was carried out on gender and REDD+ entitled the Situation Analysis and Recommendations for Improving Gender Inclusiveness and Participation in Papua New Guinea's National REDD+ Strategy. The study was conducted with the support of the Secretariat of the CCDA and sought to conduct a deep-dive review of the current status of gender and stakeholder dynamics within the country, including within both formal and informal spheres.

The analysis revealed that women are underrepresented in decision-making processes at all levels, from the low ranking of women in almost all measures of health, education, employment, access to economic opportunities, and political participation. Women's representation is particularly low daily included in all priority social, economic and political policies and actions, especially within the natural resource sector. REDD+ directly links to forest use and local food production in PNG as it’s imperative that women are involved in decision-making processes for sustainable development. This depends upon greater inclusion of women, as well as men and youth, from planning through implementation.

31


---
**Page 193**
---

This REDD+ Gender Assessment aimed to evaluate the status of gender indicators in PNG as they relate to REDD+ programs, actions, and activities. The assessment looked at the Government's capacity to ensure gender equality and inclusion in REDD+ activities while identifying opportunities to promote gender empowerment and mainstreaming across related programs. As a result of the assessment, recommendations were made for the National REDD+ Strategy and the Resource Investment Plan (RIP) developed under the FCPS’s REDD+ Readiness Program as well as the final National REDD+ Strategy. The findings from this assessment have been integrated into both the planning and implementation processes. Some of the most pertinent issues highlighted from this assessment were as follows:

- **Male-Dominated Decision-Making Structures:** Traditional social norms often prioritize male decision-making in PNG, which can lead to key decisions and negotiations related to REDD+ policies. This gender imbalance results in women being underrepresented in leadership roles and decision-making bodies.
  
- **Cultural Norms and Practices:** Cultural norms and practices that are deeply rooted in communities often restrict women's participation. These norms dictate that women’s responsibilities primarily involve around domestic tasks, which can limit their access to relevant information and unique insights and contributions to resource management and environmental policies.

- **Lack of Access to Education and Information:** Barriers to education, especially in rural areas, impact women's ability to participate fully in REDD+ actions. Limited access to relevant information further exacerbates this issue, as many women are not aware of their rights or how they can contribute effectively to REDD+ initiatives.

- **Insufficient Support Systems:** Existing institutional frameworks often lack the necessary support mechanisms for facilitating women’s participation. This includes inadequate training and capacity-building programs specifically designed to empower women within REDD+ processes.

- **Gender-Specific Policies:** The absence of comprehensive gender-sensitive policies within various sectors reduces the effectiveness of engagement strategies. While some organizations may acknowledge gender disparities, without formal guidelines, practices remain inconsistent across different initiatives.

- **Cultural Resistance to Change:** Deep-rooted cultural beliefs and practices can resist shifts towards more inclusive approaches to engage women in REDD+ initiatives. Navigating these cultural barriers often requires tailored approaches that respect local traditions while promoting inclusive practices.

Although PNG’s National REDD+ strategy had not been implemented at the time this and the definition of gender-sensitive strategies were developed, several key areas where gender should be considered in benefit sharing design processes were also identified. These include:

- **Recognition of Women's Roles:** The design of benefit-sharing mechanisms acknowledged that women play crucial roles in resource management, yet their contributions and needs had not been adequately addressed within existing frameworks. This oversight could undermine the effectiveness of benefit-sharing strategies.

- **Gender Disaggregation of Data:** Proper incorporation of gender into benefit-sharing processes is essential for ensuring equitable access to resources such as land security, employment rights, and resources. However, there was often a lack of available data which hampers effective gender-disaggregated decision-making in REDD+ initiatives.

- **Culturally-Sensitive Approaches:** Effective benefit-sharing designs must consider local cultural contexts and gender norms to tailor strategies that motivate women's


---
**Page 194**
---

participation. Strategies had been suggested to align community project goals with specific gender roles, ensuring that women are engaged based on their roles and responsibilities within the community.

• Policy Frameworks and Guidance: While some policy frameworks had started incorporating gender considerations in many areas, there were inadequacies in policy provisions for gender inclusivity in benefit-sharing arrangements. This gap could lead to unequal distribution of benefits, reinforcing existing gender inequalities.


Apart from REDD+, specific gender-related assessments, copious reports have also been produced by international organizations and NGOs on the impacts of REDD+ implementation on women’s livelihoods and natural resource management and implementation, as well as in changes to their well-being in the context of REDD+. These studies have highlighted the need for greater attention towards integrating gender considerations in the forestry and natural resource sector and potential gains to consider in REDD+ implementation and also to inform PNG’s efforts to how to integrate gender perspectives in its REDD+ efforts moving forward.


### 4.1.2 Gender inclusion in the REDD+ stakeholder engagement process

The REDD+ Readiness Partnership (RFP) took place between 2015-2021, resulting in the design of the National REDD+ Strategy and Action Plan elements to enable its effective implementation through the establishment of institutional arrangements, the development of the National MRV system and the Forest Management Information System. The RFP process was mainly supported by the UNDP, USAID, and the FCPF’s Readiness Fund and other key stakeholders.


One of the major benefits of these programs was the funding and coordination of important stakeholder engagement and consultation as well as a variety of multiple stakeholders in REDD+ and climate change issues. At the time of readiness implementation, there were no exclusive women's participation as seen by the number of women who were targeted to attend these meetings.


During the readiness phase, some of the vital efforts focused on awareness raising on gender and related activities, which contributed towards informing stakeholders and institutions of gender-related development issues in PNG. The establishment of a Gender Focal Point under the national REDD+ Readiness phase also helped to enable the government agencies and other key actors at the subnational level to collect, interpret and analyze data in a way that identified gender-based land use dynamics, important for success in the REDD+ programmatic implementation.


Multi-stakeholder engagement around REDD+ extended beyond the definition of the NRS and the specific GCF RBP project activities to be implemented and helped generate broader knowledge and capacity building. The multi-stakeholder engagement process under the national REDD+ participatory process included a series of expert training, meetings and consultations held mainly by the CGIAR-CCAFS in collaboration with PNG’s government agencies in collaboration with key sectoral agencies such as the PNGFA, DIAL, DFPPA and CEPF. The USAID-funded Lowering Emissions in Asia’s Forests (LEAF) program was also instrumental in assisting with capacity building between 2011 to 2016. Key partnerships were established with national NGOs such as the Nature Conservancy (TNC), AusAID (DFAT), local CSOs and local and customary communities in the Adelbert Ranges of Madang Province. Part of these actions included gender awareness, knowledge sharing and capacity building across various regions, gender was a pivotal component.

Most of the gender awareness, knowledge sharing and capacity building was conducted through a series of gender trainings carried out in 2014 and 2015, targeting leading female staff from the CCDA Ranges. The project also looked into REDD+ policy and legislation in PNG and the implications of REDD+ on gender. It sought to highlight the importance of further assessing the level of engagement of women in land use planning efforts, and how these efforts can help close the gender gap through gender balanced decision-making across the sector.

The USAID-funded program, Lowering Emissions in Asia’s Forests (LEAF) was a regional program for the Asia-Pacific implemented from 2011 to 2016. Its overall objective was to strengthen the capacity of target countries to achieve meaningful and sustained reductions in greenhouse gas emissions through the implementation of REDD+ activities. Key partnerships were established with Nature Conservancy (TNC), AusAID (DFAT), local CSOs and local communities in Adelbert Ranges, Madang Province. Part of these actions such as low emission land use planning into existing community land use planning efforts in Madang Province. It was also pivotal in influencing national/sub-national level policy support around the implementation of REDD+ activities through AusAID and the Madang Provincial Government. As part of the core program activities which involved knowledge sharing and capacity building across the various regions, gender was a pivotal component of this work.

Most of the gender awareness, knowledge sharing and capacity building was conducted through a series of gender trainings carried out in 2014 and 2015, targeting leading female staff from CCDA Ranges. The training was also supported by AusAID (DFAT), TNC, PNG Department of Community Development, University of PNG and local community champions. Participants contributed to the dialogue on gender and women’s empowerment issues in REDD+. Some of the key outputs that emerged from this work were: 1) internal gender trainings carried out by the trainers within their own agencies/departments/communities; and 2) initial development of a Draft Gender Guideline for CCDA.

community champions[32]. Participants contributed to the dialogue on gender and women’s empowerment issues in REDD+ work and the broader NRM issues in the region.

Some of the more notable outcomes from this work were: 1) internal gender trainings carried out by project staff within each department/communities; and 2) the initial development of a Draft Gender Guideline for CCDA.

The project also looked into REDD+ policy and legislation in PNG and the implications of REDD+ on gender. This worked highlighted the importance of further assessing the level of engagement of women and men with REDD+ policy and legislation, including at the sub-national level by promoting gender balanced decision-making and applying lessons from other sectors.

### 4.1.3 Gender and Free, Prior and Informed Consent supported from the UN REDD Programme

PNG developed a National Guidelines on FPC after three and a half years of work, including over thirteen consultations and three field missions. Released for public comment and expert review from April to July 2015, these guidelines provide guidance on how to integrate free, prior and informed consent considerations into their operational framework at national, provincial, district, and project levels.

They recognize both women and men as customary landowners and primary users of land and resources. They also recognize that gender equality is a key principle in ensuring that the needs, rights, and interests are equally addressed in REDD+ design and implementation.


### 4.1.4 Gender in Customary Land Tenure and customary landownership

of PNG and as respective land and forestry legislation guarantee clear and tenure rights to customary landowners and users, they also provide a basis for compensation in relation to forest resources.

They also provide the basis for just compensation to be awarded in the event of development and acquisition of land/ resources from customary landowners[33], which is applicable to both statutory and customary land. This is particularly important when it comes to women’s rights, their landowning groups and relates to decision making processes over forest/land tenure arrangements and benefits.

Despite the positive inroads being made within government to address gender inequality and discrimination issues, various factors also work to undermine this progress as well. The biggest factor is customary law. Its practices and beliefs in PNG which are recognised under the Constitution[34] can be seen as a barrier to women’s empowerment and their participation from traditional cultural practices that tend to discriminate against women in relation to social rights and property.

Studies of GBV indicate that such violence appears to be widely accepted and culturally condoned.


Women and youth also play an important role in management and production in forest lands, although in many cases they are not landholders. While they have user rights, women rarely have ownership rights over productive resources. When customary land rights are negotiated, women tend not to have the right to claim a direct share of leases, royalties or compensation payments.

### 4.1.3 Gender in grievance redress and conflict resolution

Customary land development is also a major priority area for the GoPNG with a legal system in place that extends to all tiers of government and provides avenues for both formal and informal means of redress in PNG as part of the inherent right of access to justice established within the Constitution.

Women's access and use of these mechanisms is a right that is provided by law and extends down to the grassroots level through entities such as the Village Courts which are set up for this purpose and developed to integrate both customary, medatory and quasi-judicial forms of redress. Although women have been involved in the development of some of these mechanisms, others provided tend to discriminate against or disadvantage women because of the strong customary and traditional influence.

#### 2017 REDD+ Gender Assessment

The most extensive study carried out on gender and REDD+ is entitled the 'Situation Analysis and Recommendations for Improving Gender Inclusiveness and Participation in Papua New Guinea's National REDD+ Strategy and Policies.' This was conducted in 2017 by UNDP with the support and endorsement of the CCSDA. This analysis involved taking stock and reviewing the current status of gender equality and women’s empowerment within the national REDD+ strategy and policy domain (e.g., policies, institutions, etc.) spheres as well as formulating a set of recommendations to help address these issues. The assessment also aimed at identifying opportunities for increasing stakeholder engagement in the REDD+ process. It also aimed to assess corresponding implications for REDD+ implementation and future outcomes.

The Gender Assessment highlighted that the empowerment of women and gender equality are core issues within the REDD+ strategy. However, as with previous sections, women in PNG are generally underrepresented as decision makers and in positions of power but they play key roles in the economy and resource management. The findings from this assessment were presented to the CCSDA in 2017.

The Gender Assessment, the REDD+ Finance and Investment Plan (FIP), developed under the FCPS's REDD+ Readiness Program, incorporated the economic viability of the National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) into its planning and implementation phases, with key gender considerations integrated into both the planning and implementation processes.

Apart from REDD+ specific gender-related assessments, copious reports have also been produced by the international community and locally to assess broader impacts on women, both in terms of their role as resource users and managers and their contribution to poverty reduction and well-being in the course of its implementation. The results of these studies helped shed light on existing gender inequalities within the natural resource sector and potential gaps to consider in REDD+ implementation and national reform efforts in how to integrate gender perspectives in its REDD+ efforts moving forward.

### 4.1.3 Other relevant efforts to mainstream gender in REDD+

#### Capacity Building and gender-specific data collection


---
**Page 199**

Another key measure for promoting gender equality was through training CDA/REDD+ staff in the collection and management of sex-disaggregated data and looking for opportunities to allocate these trained personnel in the pilot projects. This was done to ensure that appropriate information/data was collected for monitoring and evaluation. The inclusion of sex-disaggregated data in national systems such as Forest Information Systems (FIS) and Environmental Management Information Systems (EMIS) also helped better identify gender gaps, inform policy development and implementation, and improve these measures across core sectors as part of national gender priorities including the REDD+RBP proposal development. Collecting sex-disaggregated data also helped and continues to help in the identification of gender-based vulnerabilities and risks for women and children by the CDDA in specific regions/provinces/ of PNG as part of the process of field testing this important information. The awareness raising activities were designed to raise awareness on issues and activities were anticipated to occur. As part of this process, the CCDA officers involved took particular care in applying gender sensitive approaches in the context of their awareness raising and capacity building efforts.

In each awareness raising activity that was conducted, women were often separated from the main assembly and divided into their own groups where female staff from the CCDA would then take them through the brief process of FPIC, its rationale, the laws relating to this and the reasons for its necessity. The facilitators also took care in ensuring a balance between male and female participants between the male and female participants was to ensure that women were given an opportunity to contribute their views on issues affecting them as well as their children. This ensured that the women’s and children's perspective. Women were commonly being overlooked in the core meetings and many times women were either ignored or prohibited from speaking during the meetings. The gender imbalance is prevalent, particularly within a region of the country where male dominance is prevalent and often enforced.

Strengthening women’s representation in REDD+ governance

There are still many gender gaps in REDD+ design efforts to date, but PNG has taken steps as part of its REDD+ implementation to ensure equitable representation of women, including within its government. The Minister for Environment and Conservation is a woman; the Forestry Division division is female with her head of division, also a woman, and a key renowned supporter and advocate of REDD+ in PNG since its inception. Many representatives from the different government sector attend meetings on REDD+ issues and are involved in the development of the Technical Working Committees (TWCs) are also women and at least one of the Technical Working Committee focusing on the Social and Environmental Safeguards (SES TWG), 50% are female representative.

REDD+ SES TWG was established in 2014 and acted as an advisory committee to help oversee the process of ensuring the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards were adapted to the PNG context and effectively implemented within all REDD+ projects and programs across the country.

Despite this level of representation, however, there is still a need for specific gender experts to assist with the development of these REDD+ Policies and measures within both the CCDA and as part of the development and implementation of the REDD+ RBP program, especially as it relates to gender specific interventions. The roles and responsibilities of the expert are detailed in the

### 4.2 Gender Considerations in National REDD+ Social and Environmental Safeguards

The application of the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards requires adapting them to the reality of each country, taking into account national and regional context, and aligning them with existing policies and national REDD+ actions. Given this, the CCDA created a REDD+ Social and Environmental Safeguard Action Plan (SAP) in 2013. The SAP is a living document that is updated as the PNG legislates oversee the process of ensuring the seven UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards are adapted to the PNG context and can be effectively implemented within all REDD+ actions in the country. As a result, PNG has developed its own safeguard framework for REDD+, which is reflected in the SAP by identifying the various rights and obligations that are embodied in the Cancun Safeguards, and examining how these are reflected in the specific country context in the country’s legal framework.

Within this national interpretation of the REDD+ safeguards, the following gender consideration was incorporated:

- **Safeguard (d) - The right to participate**: In particular for customary landowners, local communities, and indigenous peoples, safeguard (d) is implemented through the National REDD+ Strategy through the relevant PLRs of the country, which include the promotion of gender equality and the right to free, prior and informed consent.

In addition, PNG’s first SOI presents indicators on how safeguards have been addressed that incorporates gender considerations:

#### Cancun Safeguard B

The Constitution and land and forestry legislation guarantee clear land tenure rights to customary landowners. This progresses the extent of these rights in relation to forest resources[53]. They also provide the basis for just compensation to be awarded in the event of development and acquisition of land resources from customary landowners[54], which is applicable to both statutory and customary landholders. The Constitution provides a framework for customary land tenure, empowers legislation and invoke the formation of land holding groups to govern them. Gender equality is recognized as a fundamental principle in the Constitution and rules on decision-making processes over forests/land tenure arrangements and benefits.

#### Cancun Safeguard D

The Constitution recognizes Gender Equality[56] and the right of women citizens to participate in all economic activities. This is further recognized and encouraged in specific PLRs at the national level[57]. Gender discrimination issues are addressed within specific policies of the public service[58] and applicable within all sectors. There are also dedicated public and private agencies established


---
**Page 201**
---

to implement this right and to raise awareness and implement policies on gender equality and discrimination in PNG.

# 5. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

Papua New Guinea has a regional and international framework on gender and human rights that provides a basis for protecting the rights of women and communities. However, despite this regulatory framework, significant gaps remain in its practical implementation, requiring substantial efforts to bridge the divide between policy and practice. Therefore, the guiding principles of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) are to ensure the effective implementation of international legislation on gender-related matters, as well as strengthening gender considerations to ensure gender equality, the protection of rights, and the full participation of women.

This Gender Assessment provides comprehensive background information to inform gender mainstreaming efforts in the context of the Pacific REDD+ Acceleration Project (the REDD+ Project). As identified, despite the recognition of a diversity of rights for women and gender equality anchored in PNG’s Constitution, there are significant gaps in the implementation of these rights, particularly in land rights, and decision-making. Specifically, this GA achieves the following results:

- Identification of gender gaps and inequalities within PNG's land tenure system;
- Identification of gender inequalities and challenges and risks faced by women and other marginalized groups as a result of their exclusion from policy development actions, such as decision-making processes, persists in the forestry and natural resource sectors, labor force participation, forest use, land tenure, safeguards, etc.

Women’s participation has been guaranteed throughout the stakeholder consultations; however this is still not reflected in the final project design. There are also gaps at the local or community level. As a result of cultural barriers including male-dominated governance structures, restrictive gender norms, limited access to education, weak institutional support and insufficient gender-sensitive policies, women remain within the benefit-sharing mechanisms, policy frameworks, and engagement strategies.

Moreover, given the high-level nature of PNG’s REDD+ strategy at the time when the results were achieved, no specific SEAH/GBV risks were considered (nor triggered) throughout PNG’s REDD+ readiness process. However, it is important to be aware that there are gender considerations including considering the results of stakeholders’ consultations undertaken in preparation of this RAP, consistent with an appropriate design.

As understood within the BEP and its approval and its accompanying ESMP which underpins this GAP, in PNG, projects designed to designing and implementing on-the-ground activities to be financed with GCF proceeds in a way that does not create or exacerbate existing gender inequalities. The Project has adopted a zero tolerance policy towards SEAH and GBV as clearly stated in the PNG’s Gender Policy and the PNG’s National Strategy for Gender Equality (PNG’s Gender Policy and FAG) dedicated Environmental and Social Standard 6 on gender equality and prevention of sexual harassment and violence. The Papua New Guinea Government’s Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan will be updated as part of the comprehensive Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA), Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) and Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP).


---
**Page 203**
---

sharing plan (Component 1) and refining activities to be implemented on-the-ground (component 2). Some areas of particular attention include:

- Identification of sectors and thematic area of particular relevance in REDD+ in which gender-responsive actions can catalyse transformational positive change for marginalized groups, such as women and youth;
- Identification of entry points, strategies, and opportunities for mainstreaming gender in the proposed project and broader implementation of REDD+ actions and safeguards, while developing a gender approach that effectively integrates considerations of gender, women's rights, and power dynamics at all levels: national, provincial, country, local, and context.
- Identification of preliminary areas of activities to incorporate into the Climate Action Plan.

Moreover, as key insights from the GA, a series of recommendations for gender actions to be integrated are provided in the RBP report, particularly relevant for its implementation, were identified and are listed below. These recommendations are in line with PNGs overarching regulatory framework and the NBSR and seek to address potential inequalities and discrimination that may arise as part of the Project's execution. The recommendations are organized by theme as follows:

### Policy
- Integrate gender considerations across existing laws, policies, and guidelines of key REDD+ sector agencies as part of regulatory processes, e.g., guidelines for approval of FMA’s in forestry; guidelines for agricultural project approvals; PA approval processes or as part of IUC approval processes.

### Project Implementation
- Ensure there is consistency on gender responsiveness (via key focal points) across the key government sectors/categorics and within the communities involved in the implementation of the project activities, especially at the subnational levels.
- Where appropriate, CCOs, NGOs, or other contractors or supply funding for REDD+ activities, it could be beneficial to use that position to leverage on contracted parties to require that there be inclusion of women in activities carried out and compliance with REDD+ gender action plans.
- Undertake measurements within the work on REDD+ Pilot projects (e.g. establishment of quotas, quotas, or other measures to address inequalities and inequities within PNG’s land tenure system and incentives for environmental services).
- Take affirmative measures to solicit and incorporate the perspectives of women in the design and implementation of the project activities and outputs as well as in the evaluation of this proposed project.
- Hire a gender specialist with the project to support the mainstreaming of gender throughout the design and implementation of the outputs of the project, as well as any associated gender action plans and mitigation measures that are developed during the safeguarding process.
- Devote and allocate adequate funds, resources and expertise for implementing gender-related actions and ensuring accountability. This includes holding individuals and institutions accountable for outcomes that promote gender equality.


---
**Page 204**
---

* During project implementation conduct qualitative assessments on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated to the project.

### Capacity building

* Ensure there is gender-specific training carried out within CCDA and integrated into REDD+ training programs within relevant sectors, e.g., training for enhanced forest management, sustainable land use, environmental management within protected area committees, etc.
* In stakeholder engagement activities, ensure a gender approach is fully integrated into all levels of the project. This will ensure that women’s voices are heard and that vulnerable groups can actively and equitably participate and that their interests, concerns and perspectives are considered in decision-making processes.
* Build capacity of women and youth from customary landowning groups and local communities on REDD+ and provide them with adequate resources (e.g. training, financing, etc.) to enable them to effectively participate in the project and benefit from its outputs. Strengthening specific capacity needs would facilitate the empowerment of women and other vulnerable groups.

### Governance

* The CCDA should encourage strong representation by women on all REDD+ management and committees and ensure CC projects include activities that target women’s direct involvement at the community level.
* Introduce measures to assess the extent of involvement or representation of women’s interests in decision-making processes, including those within the IUG management committees.
* Ensure governance arrangements with REDD+ pilot projects (e.g. Program Management Unit) and Project Steering Committees (PSCs) are gender equitable in their memberships. In cases where there are seats for civil society groups, ensure women and youth are represented.
* The establishment of women’s forest networks or federations at the provincial level in PNG can give women a voice on issues that affect them directly. When these networks are well organized and resourced, these networks can effectively promote women's interests, offering opportunities for skill development, awareness-raising, reduced transaction costs, market access, etc.

### Data & Monitoring

* Ensure there is a core group within the CCDA and sub-groups within the sectors who are responsible for collecting sex-disaggregated data or gender-specific data/information and for disseminating this to respective sections to feed into key information management systems (e.g. GIS systems, databases, etc.) which are used to design-and-develop REDD+ programs, policies and plans/activities.
* Strengthen the collection of additional baseline data on gender (e.g. on land tenure, women’s access to resources, etc.), as well as other relevant data.
* Actions should also consider the gender digital gap and differing access rates of youth and remote communities to information.
